## 1.1 The Internet vs The World Wide Web

<a id="11-the-internet-vs-the-world-wide-web"></a>

### What is the Internet?

The **Internet** is a global network of interconnected computers and devices that communicate with each other using standardized protocols (primarily TCP/IP). It is the **physical and logical infrastructure** — think of it as the roads and highways of the digital world.

> **Hinglish:** Internet ek bahut bada network hai jisme duniya bhar ke computers aapas me connected hain — jaise ek badi highway system jo sab cities ko connect karti hai.

### What is the World Wide Web (WWW)?

The **World Wide Web** is a system of interlinked hypertext documents and resources that are accessed **over the Internet** using a web browser. It is one of the **services** that runs on top of the Internet — invented by **Tim Berners-Lee in 1989**.

> **Hinglish:** WWW internet ke upar ek service hai — jaise highway (internet) par chalti gKshitijyaan (websites). Internet ke bina WWW nahi hoga, lekin internet sirf WWW nahi hai.

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **Common Interview Trap:** The Internet and the World Wide Web are NOT the same thing. The Internet is the infrastructure; the WWW is a service that uses that infrastructure. Other services include Email (SMTP), FTP, SSH, DNS, etc.

### Internet vs WWW — Comparison Table

| Feature     | Internet                           | World Wide Web                  |
| ----------- | ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------- |
| Definition  | Global network of computers        | System of hyperlinked documents |
| Inventor    | ARPANET (US Military, 1960s)       | Tim Berners-Lee (1989)          |
| Protocol    | TCP/IP                             | HTTP/HTTPS                      |
| Access Tool | Any networked device               | Web Browser                     |
| Scope       | Broader — includes Email, FTP, DNS | Subset of Internet              |
| Example     | Sending an email                   | Visiting amazon.com             |

---

### Brief History: Web 1.0 → Web 2.0 → Web 3.0

```mermaid
timeline
    title Evolution of the Web
    1991-2004 : Web 1.0
              : Static HTML pages
              : Read-only content
              : No user interaction
              : Yahoo Directory, early websites
    2004-2016 : Web 2.0
              : Dynamic, interactive content
              : User-generated content
              : Social media, blogs, apps
              : Facebook, YouTube, Twitter
    2016-Now  : Web 3.0
              : Decentralized web
              : Blockchain & cryptocurrency
              : Semantic web, AI integration
              : NFTs, DeFi, Metaverse
```

| Feature    | Web 1.0                 | Web 2.0                 | Web 3.0                      |
| ---------- | ----------------------- | ----------------------- | ---------------------------- |
| Era        | 1991–2004               | 2004–2016               | 2016–Present                 |
| Nature     | Read-only               | Read-Write              | Read-Write-Own               |
| Content    | Static HTML             | Dynamic, User-generated | Decentralized                |
| Technology | HTML, basic CSS         | AJAX, JavaScript, APIs  | Blockchain, AI, Semantic Web |
| Example    | Static company websites | Facebook, YouTube       | Ethereum, NFT platforms      |
| User Role  | Passive reader          | Active contributor      | Owner/Participant            |

---

### How Data Travels Across the Internet

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Your Device\nSender] -->|1. Data broken into packets| B[Router\nLocal ISP]
    B -->|2. Packets routed via best path| C[Backbone\nNetworks]
    C -->|3. Packets may take different routes| D[Destination ISP\nRouter]
    D -->|4. Packets reassembled| E[Server\nReceiver]
    E -->|5. Response sent back| A
```

**Key concept — Packet Switching:** Data is broken into small **packets**, each routed independently across the network, then reassembled at the destination. This is more efficient than circuit switching (like old telephone lines).

> **Hinglish:** Jab aap YouTube par ek video download karte hain, toh woh video ek saath nahi aata — chhote-chhote "packets" me aata hai, alag-alag raston se, aur aapke computer par phir jooda jaata hai. Jaise courier company ek bada samaan kayi boxes me bhejti hai alag trucks se.

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.1

**Q1: What is the difference between the Internet and the World Wide Web?**

> The Internet is the global physical and logical network infrastructure that connects millions of computers using TCP/IP. The WWW is an application layer service that runs ON TOP of the Internet, using HTTP/HTTPS to deliver hyperlinked web pages via browsers. Email, FTP, and SSH are other services that also run on the Internet but are not part of the WWW.

**Q2: What is packet switching and why does the Internet use it?**

> Packet switching breaks data into small chunks (packets) that travel independently across the network and are reassembled at the destination. It is preferred over circuit switching because it makes efficient use of network bandwidth, allows multiple users to share the same physical links simultaneously, and provides resilience — if one path fails, packets can take alternative routes.

**Q3: What is Web 2.0 and how does React relate to it?**

> Web 2.0 refers to the era of dynamic, interactive, user-generated web content (2004 onwards). React is a product of Web 2.0 thinking — it enables highly interactive Single Page Applications (SPAs) where users can interact without full page reloads, enabling the rich experiences seen on platforms like Facebook (which created React), Instagram, and YouTube.

## 1.2 Client-Server Architecture

<a id="12-client-server-architecture"></a>

### What is a Client?

A **client** is any device or software that **initiates a request** to a server to access resources or services. The client consumes the service.

> **Hinglish:** Client woh hota hai jo **maangta hai** — jaise aap restaurant me waiter se khana maangते hain. Aapka browser, mobile app — ye sab clients hain.

**Examples of clients:**

- Web browser (Chrome, Firefox, Safari)
- Mobile app (Instagram, Uber)
- Terminal/CLI tools (curl, wget)
- Another server calling an API (Server-to-Server)

### What is a Server?

A **server** is a computer or program that **listens for and responds to requests** from clients. It provides resources, data, or services.

> **Hinglish:** Server woh hota hai jo **deta hai** — jaise restaurant ka kitchen jo khana banata aur deta hai. Server 24/7 requests sunta rehta hai.

**Examples of servers:**

- Web Server (Apache, Nginx) — serves HTML files
- Application Server (Node.js, Django) — business logic
- Database Server (PostgreSQL, MongoDB) — data storage
- File Server — serves files (images, videos)
- CDN Server — delivers cached content closer to the user

---

### Request → Response Cycle — Step by Step

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant User as 👤 User (Browser)
    participant Client as 🌐 Client App
    participant DNS as 📡 DNS Server
    participant Server as 🖥️ Web Server
    participant DB as 🗄️ Database

    User->>Client: Types URL / Clicks link
    Client->>DNS: "What is the IP of amazon.com?"
    DNS-->>Client: "IP: 205.251.242.103"
    Client->>Server: HTTP GET /products (Request)
    Server->>DB: Query: SELECT * FROM products
    DB-->>Server: Returns product data
    Server-->>Client: HTTP 200 OK + HTML/JSON (Response)
    Client->>User: Renders the page
```

**Detailed steps:**

1. **User action** — User types a URL or clicks a link
2. **DNS Lookup** — Client asks DNS to resolve domain to IP address
3. **TCP Connection** — Client establishes a TCP connection to the server
4. **HTTP Request** — Client sends an HTTP request (method, headers, body)
5. **Server Processing** — Server processes the request (authentication, business logic, DB query)
6. **HTTP Response** — Server sends back a response (status code, headers, body)
7. **Rendering** — Client (browser) renders the received content

---

### Peer-to-Peer (P2P) vs Client-Server

| Feature              | Client-Server               | Peer-to-Peer (P2P)                                 |
| -------------------- | --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| Architecture         | Centralized server          | Decentralized (every node is both client & server) |
| Scalability          | Depends on server capacity  | Scales naturally as more peers join                |
| Cost                 | Higher (server maintenance) | Lower (no central server)                          |
| Reliability          | Single point of failure     | More resilient                                     |
| Security             | Easier to control           | Harder to secure                                   |
| Examples             | Amazon, Netflix, Gmail      | BitTorrent, Blockchain, Skype (early)              |
| Use in React/Next.js | All web apps use this       | WebRTC for real-time P2P                           |

> [!NOTE]
> In the context of React and Next.js, you will **always** work with Client-Server architecture. The browser (client) makes HTTP requests to the Next.js server, which responds with HTML, JSON, or other data.

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.2

**Q1: What is the difference between a web server and an application server?**

> A **web server** (like Nginx or Apache) handles HTTP requests and serves static files (HTML, CSS, images). An **application server** (like Node.js or Django) contains business logic, connects to databases, and generates dynamic content. In practice, Nginx often acts as a **reverse proxy** in front of a Node.js application server — Nginx handles static files and TLS, while forwarding dynamic requests to Node.js.

**Q2: Is Next.js a client or a server?**

> Next.js is **both**. On the server side, Next.js renders HTML (SSR/SSG), handles API routes (Route Handlers), and runs Server Components. On the client side, Next.js hydrates the page and handles client-side navigation. This dual nature is what makes Next.js a full-stack framework.

**Q3: What is a reverse proxy and how does it relate to web servers?**

> A **reverse proxy** is a server that sits between the client and the application server. It receives all incoming requests and forwards them to the appropriate backend service. Benefits include load balancing, SSL termination, caching, and security. Nginx is commonly used as a reverse proxy for Next.js apps in production.

## 1.3 What Happens When You Type a URL?

<a id="13-what-happens-when-you-type-a-url"></a>

> [!IMPORTANT]
> This is one of the **most commonly asked interview questions** in frontend and full-stack roles. Know this flow in detail — it tests your understanding of DNS, TCP, HTTP, TLS, and browser rendering all at once.

### The Complete Flow

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A[User types: https://amazon.com] --> B{Browser Cache?}
    B -->|Yes - IP found| F
    B -->|No| C[DNS Resolution]
    C --> C1[Check OS Cache]
    C1 -->|Not found| C2[Check ISP DNS Cache]
    C2 -->|Not found| C3[Recursive DNS Resolver]
    C3 --> C4[Root Name Server]
    C4 --> C5[.com TLD Server]
    C5 --> C6[amazon.com Authoritative DNS]
    C6 -->|IP Address returned| F
    F[TCP 3-Way Handshake\nSYN → SYN-ACK → ACK] --> G
    G[TLS Handshake\nSSL Certificate Exchange] --> H
    H[HTTP GET Request Sent] --> I[Server Processes Request]
    I --> J[HTTP Response Received]
    J --> K[Browser Parses HTML]
    K --> L[DOM + CSSOM Built]
    L --> M[Page Rendered to Screen]
```

---

### Step 1: Browser Checks Cache

Before making any network request, the browser checks its **local cache** to see if it already has a valid copy of the resource.

> **Hinglish:** Browser pehle apni memory check karta hai — "kya maine yeh page pehle load kiya tha?" Agar haan, aur cache expire nahi hua, toh woh seedha wahi se deta hai bina server ke jaaye.

Cache types checked (in order):

1. **Browser memory cache** — current session, fastest
2. **Browser disk cache** — stored on hard drive
3. **Service Worker cache** — PWA/offline support

---

### Step 2: DNS Resolution

**DNS (Domain Name System)** is the internet's phone book — it translates human-readable domain names (`amazon.com`) into machine-readable IP addresses (`205.251.242.103`).

> **Hinglish:** DNS ek phone book ki tarah hai. Aap `amazon.com` likhte hain, DNS bolta hai "iska number hai `205.251.242.103`" — phir browser us number par call karta hai.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Browser] -->|1. Is amazon.com cached?| B[OS DNS Cache]
    B -->|2. Cache miss| C[ISP Recursive Resolver]
    C -->|3. Cache miss| D[Root Name Server\n. ]
    D -->|4. "Ask .com TLD"| C
    C -->|5. Ask .com TLD| E[.com TLD Server]
    E -->|6. "Ask amazon.com NS"| C
    C -->|7. Ask amazon NS| F[amazon.com Authoritative\nName Server]
    F -->|8. IP = 205.251.242.103| C
    C -->|9. IP returned + cached| A
```

**DNS Resolution Types:**

| Type          | Description                                                          |
| ------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Recursive** | The resolver does all the work on behalf of the client (most common) |
| **Iterative** | The resolver refers the client to the next server in the chain       |

**DNS Cache Hierarchy** (checked in this order):

1. Browser cache
2. OS cache (`/etc/hosts`, Windows hosts file)
3. Router/ISP cache
4. Recursive DNS resolver cache
5. Root → TLD → Authoritative DNS (only if all caches miss)

---

### Step 3: TCP/IP Connection — 3-Way Handshake

Once the IP address is known, the browser establishes a **TCP (Transmission Control Protocol)** connection.

> **Hinglish:** TCP connection ek phone call ki tarah hai — pehle aap ring karte hain, doosra uthata hai, phir dono "hello hello" bolte hain confirm karne ke liye ki connection established hai. Yahi 3-way handshake hai.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client (Browser)
    participant S as Server

    C->>S: SYN (Synchronize)\n"I want to connect, seq=x"
    S-->>C: SYN-ACK (Synchronize-Acknowledge)\n"OK, seq=y, ack=x+1"
    C->>S: ACK (Acknowledge)\n"Confirmed, ack=y+1"
    Note over C,S: Connection Established ✅\nHTTP Request can now be sent
```

| Step | Packet      | Meaning                           |
| ---- | ----------- | --------------------------------- |
| 1    | **SYN**     | Client says "I want to connect"   |
| 2    | **SYN-ACK** | Server says "OK, I'm ready"       |
| 3    | **ACK**     | Client confirms "Great, let's go" |

---

### Step 4: TLS/SSL Handshake (HTTPS only)

For **HTTPS** connections, after the TCP handshake, a **TLS (Transport Layer Security)** handshake occurs to establish an encrypted connection.

> **Hinglish:** TLS handshake ek secret code banane ki process hai. Client aur server dono ek key exchange karte hain, jiske baad sab communication encrypted (coded) ho jaata hai — koi bhi beech mein padh nahi sakta.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant S as Server

    C->>S: Client Hello\n(TLS version, supported ciphers, random number)
    S-->>C: Server Hello\n(chosen cipher, SSL certificate, random number)
    C->>C: Verify certificate\n(Is it signed by trusted CA?)
    C->>S: Pre-master secret\n(encrypted with server's public key)
    S->>S: Decrypt pre-master secret\nwith private key
    Note over C,S: Both derive Session Keys\nfrom random numbers + pre-master secret
    C->>S: "Finished" (encrypted with session key)
    S-->>C: "Finished" (encrypted with session key)
    Note over C,S: Encrypted Connection Established ✅
```

---

### Step 5: HTTP Request Sent

```http
GET /products HTTP/1.1
Host: amazon.com
User-Agent: Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0; Win64; x64)
Accept: text/html,application/xhtml+xml
Accept-Encoding: gzip, deflate, br
Accept-Language: en-US,en;q=0.9
Cookie: session-id=123-4567890
Connection: keep-alive
```

---

### Step 6: Server Processes & Responds

```http
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Type: text/html; charset=utf-8
Content-Encoding: gzip
Cache-Control: max-age=3600
X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN
Content-Length: 48320

<!DOCTYPE html>
<html>...page content...</html>
```

---

### Step 7: Browser Renders the Page

(Covered in detail in Section 1.13)

---

### What is a CDN?

A **Content Delivery Network (CDN)** is a geographically distributed network of servers that cache and deliver content to users from the **nearest server location**, reducing latency.

> **Hinglish:** CDN ek chain of stores ki tarah hai. Amazon warehouse sirf ek jagah nahi hota — woh sab cities mein hote hain taki aapka order jaldi pahunche. CDN same kaam karta hai web content ke liye.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    User1[👤 User in Mumbai] -->|Served from| CDN1[CDN Server\nMumbai]
    User2[👤 User in London] -->|Served from| CDN2[CDN Server\nLondon]
    User3[👤 User in New York] -->|Served from| CDN3[CDN Server\nNew York]
    CDN1 & CDN2 & CDN3 -->|Cache miss: fetch from| Origin[🖥️ Origin Server\nUS-East]
```

**How CDN helps in Next.js:**

- Vercel automatically deploys Next.js to a global CDN
- Static assets (`next/image` optimized images, CSS, JS) are cached at edge nodes
- Server Components can be cached at the CDN edge

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.3

**Q1: "What happens when you type google.com and press Enter?" — Explain in detail.**

> 1. **URL Parsing** — Browser parses the URL (protocol: HTTPS, host: google.com, path: /)
> 2. **Cache Check** — Browser checks local cache for a valid cached response
> 3. **DNS Resolution** — OS/ISP/Root DNS resolves google.com → IP (e.g., 142.250.190.78)
> 4. **TCP Handshake** — 3-way handshake (SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK) establishes TCP connection
> 5. **TLS Handshake** — Certificate verification and session key exchange for HTTPS
> 6. **HTTP GET Request** — Browser sends HTTP GET / with headers (User-Agent, Accept, Cookies)
> 7. **Server Processing** — Google's servers process the request, generate HTML
> 8. **HTTP Response** — 200 OK with HTML, headers (Content-Type, Cache-Control, Set-Cookie)
> 9. **Parsing** — Browser parses HTML → DOM, CSS → CSSOM
> 10. **Rendering** — Render tree → Layout → Paint → Composite → Screen

**Q2: What is DNS and why is it needed?**

> DNS (Domain Name System) is a hierarchical distributed database that maps human-readable domain names to IP addresses. It's needed because: (1) IP addresses like `142.250.190.78` are hard for humans to remember, (2) IP addresses of servers can change, but domain names remain stable — DNS handles the mapping, and (3) a distributed system allows DNS to be highly available and fast globally.

**Q3: What is the difference between TCP and UDP?**

> TCP (Transmission Control Protocol) provides **reliable, ordered, error-checked** delivery — every packet is acknowledged, and lost packets are retransmitted. It's used for HTTP, HTTPS, email, and file transfer where data integrity is critical. UDP (User Datagram Protocol) is **faster but unreliable** — packets can be lost, duplicated, or arrive out of order. It's used for video streaming, gaming, DNS, and VoIP where speed matters more than perfection. HTTP/3 (QUIC) actually uses UDP but adds its own reliability mechanisms on top.

**Q4: How does HTTPS protect data in transit?**

> HTTPS uses TLS (Transport Layer Security) to: (1) **Authenticate** the server — the SSL certificate proves the server is who it claims to be (signed by a trusted Certificate Authority), (2) **Encrypt** all data in transit using symmetric encryption (after the asymmetric handshake establishes session keys) — so even if traffic is intercepted, it appears as random bytes, and (3) **Ensure integrity** — any tampering with the data in transit is detected.

**Q5: What is a CDN and how does it benefit a Next.js application?**

> A CDN is a globally distributed network of servers that caches and delivers content from the location closest to the user, reducing latency. For Next.js: static assets (images, CSS, JS bundles) are automatically served from CDN edge nodes on Vercel. SSG pages (statically generated HTML) are also cached at the edge. next/image serves WebP-optimized images from the CDN. This can reduce Time to First Byte (TTFB) from ~200ms (origin) to <10ms (edge CDN).

## 1.4 HTTP & HTTPS — Deep Dive

<a id="14-http--https--deep-dive"></a>

### What is HTTP?

**HTTP (HyperText Transfer Protocol)** is an **application-layer protocol** that defines the rules for how messages are formatted and transmitted between clients and servers on the web.

> **Hinglish:** HTTP ek language/rule set hai jisme browser aur server baat karte hain. Jaise India mein log Hindi mein baat karte hain, browser aur server HTTP mein baat karte hain — ek standard tarika.

**Key characteristics:**

- **Stateless** — Each request is independent; the server does not remember previous requests
- **Text-based** — HTTP/1.x requests/responses are human-readable text
- **Request-Response model** — Always initiated by the client

### What is HTTPS?

**HTTPS (HTTP Secure)** = HTTP + **TLS (Transport Layer Security)**. It adds encryption, authentication, and data integrity to HTTP.

> **Hinglish:** HTTPS = HTTP + Lock 🔒. Sab data encrypted (coded) hota hai — beech mein koi padh nahi sakta. Jaise aap envelope mein letter daalte ho lock karke.

---

### HTTP Versions — Evolution

```mermaid
timeline
    title HTTP Version Evolution
    1991 : HTTP/0.9
         : Only GET method
         : No headers
         : HTML only
    1996 : HTTP/1.0
         : Headers added
         : Status codes added
         : One request per connection
    1997 : HTTP/1.1
         : Persistent connections
         : Pipelining
         : Chunked transfer
         : Host header required
    2015 : HTTP/2
         : Binary protocol
         : Multiplexing
         : Header compression (HPACK)
         : Server Push
         : Single TCP connection
    2022 : HTTP/3
         : Built on QUIC (UDP)
         : 0-RTT connection
         : Better mobile performance
         : No head-of-line blocking
```

---

### HTTP/1.0 vs HTTP/1.1 vs HTTP/2 vs HTTP/3

| Feature                   | HTTP/1.0        | HTTP/1.1                | HTTP/2                      | HTTP/3              |
| ------------------------- | --------------- | ----------------------- | --------------------------- | ------------------- |
| **Released**              | 1996            | 1997                    | 2015                        | 2022                |
| **Transport**             | TCP             | TCP                     | TCP                         | QUIC (UDP)          |
| **Connections**           | One per request | Persistent (keep-alive) | One connection, multiplexed | One QUIC connection |
| **Multiplexing**          | ❌              | ❌ (pipelining only)    | ✅ Full                     | ✅ Full             |
| **Header Compression**    | ❌              | ❌                      | ✅ HPACK                    | ✅ QPACK            |
| **Server Push**           | ❌              | ❌                      | ✅                          | ✅                  |
| **Format**                | Text            | Text                    | Binary                      | Binary              |
| **0-RTT**                 | ❌              | ❌                      | ❌                          | ✅                  |
| **Head-of-line Blocking** | Per connection  | Per connection          | Per connection (TCP level)  | ❌ (solved by QUIC) |

---

### HTTP/1.1 — Persistent Connections Explained

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant S as Server

    Note over C,S: HTTP/1.0 — New connection per request
    C->>S: TCP Handshake
    C->>S: GET /index.html
    S-->>C: 200 OK (HTML)
    C->>S: TCP Handshake (again!)
    C->>S: GET /style.css
    S-->>C: 200 OK (CSS)

    Note over C,S: HTTP/1.1 — Persistent Connection
    C->>S: TCP Handshake (once)
    C->>S: GET /index.html
    S-->>C: 200 OK (HTML)
    C->>S: GET /style.css (same connection!)
    S-->>C: 200 OK (CSS)
    C->>S: GET /script.js (same connection!)
    S-->>C: 200 OK (JS)
```

---

### HTTP/2 — Multiplexing Explained

> **Hinglish:** HTTP/1.1 mein ek lane wali road thi — ek car rukti thi toh peeche sab rukते the. HTTP/2 ne multi-lane highway banaya — kai cars simultaneously chal sakti hain ek hi connection par.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph HTTP1.1["HTTP/1.1 — Sequential (one lane)"]
        R1[Request HTML] --> R2[Request CSS] --> R3[Request JS] --> R4[Request Image]
    end

    subgraph HTTP2["HTTP/2 — Multiplexed (multiple lanes)"]
        direction LR
        M1[Request HTML]
        M2[Request CSS]
        M3[Request JS]
        M4[Request Image]
    end

    Note["All 4 travel simultaneously\non ONE TCP connection"]
```

---

### HTTP vs HTTPS — Comparison

| Feature             | HTTP                          | HTTPS                                                                           |
| ------------------- | ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Port**            | 80                            | 443                                                                             |
| **Security**        | No encryption                 | TLS encrypted                                                                   |
| **Data Privacy**    | Data visible to attackers     | Data encrypted                                                                  |
| **Authentication**  | No server verification        | SSL certificate verifies server                                                 |
| **SEO Impact**      | Penalized by Google           | Preferred by Google                                                             |
| **Browser Warning** | "Not Secure" shown            | Padlock icon shown                                                              |
| **Performance**     | Slightly faster (no TLS)      | TLS overhead (minimal with HTTP/2)                                              |
| **Required for**    | Nothing (avoid in production) | Cookies with `Secure` flag, Service Workers, Geolocation API, Camera/Mic access |

> [!IMPORTANT]
> In Next.js production deployments on Vercel, **HTTPS is enforced automatically**. Service Workers (used for PWA) and several browser APIs **require HTTPS** to function.

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.4

**Q1: What are the main differences between HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2?**

> HTTP/1.1 uses text-based protocol and requires multiple TCP connections for parallelism (typically 6-8 per domain), leading to connection overhead. HTTP/2 introduces: (1) **Binary framing** — messages encoded in binary frames for efficiency, (2) **Multiplexing** — multiple requests/responses over a single TCP connection simultaneously, (3) **Header compression** (HPACK) — reduces redundant header data, (4) **Server Push** — server can proactively send resources the client hasn't requested yet, (5) **Stream prioritization** — important resources loaded first.

**Q2: What is head-of-line blocking and how does HTTP/3 solve it?**

> In HTTP/2, although multiplexing allows multiple streams over one TCP connection, TCP itself has head-of-line blocking at the transport layer — if one TCP packet is lost, all streams on that connection must wait for retransmission. HTTP/3 solves this by running on **QUIC (Quick UDP Internet Connections)**, a UDP-based protocol that handles streams independently — a lost packet only blocks its own stream, not all streams.

**Q3: What is SSL/TLS? What is the difference between SSL and TLS?**

> SSL (Secure Sockets Layer) was the original encryption protocol created by Netscape. TLS (Transport Layer Security) is the modernized, more secure successor to SSL. SSL 3.0 was deprecated in 2015 due to vulnerabilities (POODLE attack). Today, TLS 1.2 and TLS 1.3 are used. Technically, when people say "SSL certificate," they mean a TLS certificate — the term SSL is colloquially still used. TLS 1.3 (2018) significantly simplifies the handshake, requiring only 1 round trip (1-RTT) instead of 2.

## 1.5 HTTP Methods (Verbs) — Complete Reference

<a id="15-http-methods-verbs--complete-reference"></a>

### Overview of HTTP Methods

HTTP methods (also called **verbs**) indicate the **action** to be performed on the resource identified by the URL.

> **Hinglish:** HTTP methods bataate hain ki aap resource ke saath **kya karna chahte hain** — dekhna (GET), banana (POST), replace karna (PUT), delete karna (DELETE). Jaise waiter ko batana — "bhaiya ek chai laao" vs "bhaiya ye chai wapis lo".

---

### Complete HTTP Methods Table

| Method      | Purpose                              | Body?    | Safe? | Idempotent? | Cacheable? |
| ----------- | ------------------------------------ | -------- | ----- | ----------- | ---------- |
| **GET**     | Retrieve resource                    | ❌       | ✅    | ✅          | ✅         |
| **POST**    | Create new resource                  | ✅       | ❌    | ❌          | Rarely     |
| **PUT**     | Replace entire resource              | ✅       | ❌    | ✅          | ❌         |
| **PATCH**   | Partially update resource            | ✅       | ❌    | ❌ (can be) | ❌         |
| **DELETE**  | Remove resource                      | Optional | ❌    | ✅          | ❌         |
| **HEAD**    | Like GET but no body                 | ❌       | ✅    | ✅          | ✅         |
| **OPTIONS** | Get allowed methods / CORS preflight | ❌       | ✅    | ✅          | ❌         |
| **CONNECT** | Establish tunnel (HTTPS proxy)       | ❌       | ❌    | ❌          | ❌         |
| **TRACE**   | Loop-back test (debugging)           | ❌       | ✅    | ✅          | ❌         |

---

### Safe vs Unsafe Methods

**Safe methods** do not modify the server's state. They are read-only operations.

> **Hinglish:** Safe methods sirf "dekhte" hain, kuch change nahi karte — jaise book padhna, usse nuksaan nahi hota.

- ✅ **Safe:** GET, HEAD, OPTIONS, TRACE
- ❌ **Unsafe:** POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE, CONNECT

### Idempotent Methods

An **idempotent** method produces the same result whether called once or multiple times — the server state after 1 call = server state after N calls.

> **Hinglish:** Idempotent ka matlab hai — ek kaam kitni baar bhi karo, result wahi rahega. Jaise light switch off karo ek baar ya teen baar — light off hi rahegi. GET, PUT, DELETE idempotent hain.

| Method | Idempotent?  | Explanation                                                             |
| ------ | ------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| GET    | ✅           | Fetching a user 10 times doesn't change the user                        |
| DELETE | ✅           | Deleting user #5 twice has same effect as once (already deleted)        |
| PUT    | ✅           | Replacing user #5 with same data twice = same result                    |
| POST   | ❌           | Creating a user 10 times creates 10 users                               |
| PATCH  | ❌ (usually) | Depends on implementation. `PATCH /counter increment` is not idempotent |

---

### Each Method — Deep Dive

#### GET — Retrieve Data

```http
GET /api/users/123 HTTP/1.1
Host: api.example.com
Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGci...
Accept: application/json
```

```json
// Response:
{
  "id": 123,
  "name": "Rahul Sharma",
  "email": "rahul@example.com"
}
```

**Use cases:** Fetch user profile, get product list, retrieve blog posts

#### POST — Create Data

```http
POST /api/users HTTP/1.1
Host: api.example.com
Content-Type: application/json
Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGci...

{
  "name": "Priya Mehta",
  "email": "priya@example.com"
}
```

```http
HTTP/1.1 201 Created
Location: /api/users/124
```

**Use cases:** Register user, create order, submit form, upload file

#### PUT — Replace Entire Resource

```http
PUT /api/users/123 HTTP/1.1
Content-Type: application/json

{
  "id": 123,
  "name": "Rahul Kumar",
  "email": "rahul.kumar@example.com",
  "phone": "+91-9999999999"
}
// ALL fields must be sent — missing fields will be set to null/removed
```

#### PATCH — Partial Update

```http
PATCH /api/users/123 HTTP/1.1
Content-Type: application/json

{
  "name": "Rahul Kumar"
}
// Only 'name' is updated. Other fields remain unchanged.
```

#### DELETE — Remove Resource

```http
DELETE /api/users/123 HTTP/1.1
Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGci...
```

```http
HTTP/1.1 204 No Content
// or
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
{ "message": "User deleted successfully" }
```

#### HEAD — Metadata Only

```http
HEAD /api/files/large-video.mp4 HTTP/1.1
```

```http
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Length: 524288000
Content-Type: video/mp4
Last-Modified: Thu, 01 Jan 2024 00:00:00 GMT
// No body sent!
```

**Use case:** Check if file exists without downloading it. Check `Content-Length` before starting a large download.

#### OPTIONS — CORS Preflight

```http
OPTIONS /api/users HTTP/1.1
Origin: https://myfrontend.com
Access-Control-Request-Method: POST
Access-Control-Request-Headers: Content-Type, Authorization
```

```http
HTTP/1.1 204 No Content
Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://myfrontend.com
Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, OPTIONS
Access-Control-Allow-Headers: Content-Type, Authorization
Access-Control-Max-Age: 86400
```

---

### PUT vs PATCH — Visual Comparison

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph Original["Original User Record"]
        O["{ id: 1, name: 'Rahul', email: 'r@ex.com', age: 25 }"]
    end

    subgraph PUT["PUT — Replace Entire Resource"]
        P_REQ["Request: { name: 'Rahul Kumar' }\n⚠️ Only name sent"]
        P_RES["Result: { id: 1, name: 'Rahul Kumar', email: null, age: null }\n❌ email and age LOST"]
    end

    subgraph PATCH["PATCH — Partial Update"]
        PA_REQ["Request: { name: 'Rahul Kumar' }\n✅ Only name sent"]
        PA_RES["Result: { id: 1, name: 'Rahul Kumar', email: 'r@ex.com', age: 25 }\n✅ Only name changed"]
    end

    Original --> PUT
    Original --> PATCH
```

---

### HTTP Methods in REST API Design — Real World (Amazon Example)

```
Resource: /api/products

GET    /api/products           → List all products
POST   /api/products           → Create new product
GET    /api/products/123       → Get product #123
PUT    /api/products/123       → Replace product #123 entirely
PATCH  /api/products/123       → Update product #123 partially (e.g., just price)
DELETE /api/products/123       → Delete product #123
HEAD   /api/products/123       → Check if product #123 exists (no body)
OPTIONS /api/products          → What methods are allowed? (CORS preflight)
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.5

**Q1: What is the difference between PUT and PATCH?**

> **PUT** replaces the **entire resource** with the new data sent in the request body. If a field is missing from the request, it will be set to null or removed. **PATCH** performs a **partial update** — only the fields included in the request body are modified; all other fields remain unchanged. In practice, PATCH is preferred for updates because it's more efficient (less data sent) and safer (no accidental data loss). PUT is idempotent (same result if called multiple times with same data); PATCH is not always idempotent (e.g., `increment counter by 5`).

**Q2: What is the difference between POST and PUT?**

> **POST** creates a **new resource** and typically returns 201 Created with the Location of the new resource. POST is **not idempotent** — calling it N times creates N resources. **PUT** creates or **replaces** a resource at a **specific URL** — the client decides the resource URL. PUT is **idempotent** — calling it N times with the same data results in the same state.

**Q3: Why would you use the HEAD method?**

> HEAD is used when you want the metadata about a resource (headers) without downloading the actual content. Practical uses: (1) Check if a URL exists (response code) without downloading content, (2) Check `Content-Length` before starting a large file download, (3) Check `Last-Modified` or `ETag` to see if content has changed before fetching it, (4) Validate links in a link-checker tool without downloading page content.

**Q4: What does idempotent mean in the context of HTTP methods?**

> An idempotent HTTP method is one where making multiple identical requests has the same effect as making a single request. In other words, the server's state after 1 call is the same as after 10 calls with the same data. GET, PUT, DELETE, and HEAD are idempotent. POST is not (creates multiple resources). This matters for **retry logic** — if a network request fails, it's safe to automatically retry idempotent requests, but not POST (would create duplicates).

**Q5 (Tricky): Is DELETE idempotent?**

> Yes, DELETE is considered idempotent. After the first DELETE call, the resource is gone. After subsequent DELETE calls, the resource is still gone — the server state hasn't changed further. However, note that the **response code might differ** — the first DELETE might return 200 OK (resource deleted), while subsequent calls might return 404 Not Found (resource already gone). The **server state** is the same (no resource), so it is idempotent, even if the response code changes.

## 1.6 HTTP Headers — Complete Reference

<a id="16-http-headers--complete-reference"></a>

### What are HTTP Headers?

HTTP headers are **key-value pairs** sent in both requests and responses that provide **metadata** about the request/response — things like content type, authentication, caching rules, encoding, and more.

> **Hinglish:** HTTP headers ek parcel ke saath aane wali slip ki tarah hain — uspe likha hota hai: andar kya hai, kahan se aaya, kab tak valid hai, kaun bheja, etc. Actual parcel (body) alag hota hai.

### Structure

```http
Header-Name: Header-Value

Content-Type: application/json
Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9...
Cache-Control: max-age=3600
```

---

### Request Headers — Commonly Used

```http
GET /api/products HTTP/1.1

Host: api.amazon.com
# Required in HTTP/1.1. Specifies the domain being requested.

User-Agent: Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0; Win64; x64) AppleWebKit/537.36
# Identifies the client software (browser, version, OS)

Accept: text/html,application/xhtml+xml,application/json;q=0.9,*/*;q=0.8
# Tells server what content types the client can handle
# q= indicates preference (quality value, 0-1)

Accept-Encoding: gzip, deflate, br
# Compression formats client can handle (br = Brotli, most efficient)

Accept-Language: en-US,en;q=0.9,hi;q=0.8
# Preferred languages for response

Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9...
# Authentication credentials (Bearer token, Basic auth, etc.)

Cookie: session_id=abc123; user_pref=dark_mode
# Cookies stored from previous responses, sent with every request

Origin: https://myfrontend.vercel.app
# Indicates where the request originated from (used in CORS)

Referer: https://google.com/search?q=amazon+products
# URL of the page that made the request (note: intentional typo in spec)

Content-Type: application/json
# Type of data in the request body (for POST/PUT/PATCH)

Content-Length: 256
# Size of request body in bytes

Cache-Control: no-cache
# Client's caching preferences (request side)

If-Modified-Since: Thu, 01 Jan 2024 00:00:00 GMT
# Only send response if resource was modified after this date

If-None-Match: "etag-value-abc123"
# Only send response if ETag doesn't match (resource changed)

Connection: keep-alive
# Keep TCP connection open for subsequent requests
```

---

### Response Headers — Commonly Used

```http
HTTP/1.1 200 OK

Content-Type: application/json; charset=utf-8
# Type and encoding of response body

Content-Length: 1024
# Size of response body

Content-Encoding: gzip
# Compression applied to the response body

Cache-Control: public, max-age=3600, s-maxage=86400
# Caching rules:
# public = can be cached by CDN/proxy
# max-age = cache for 3600 seconds (1 hour) on client
# s-maxage = cache for 86400 seconds (1 day) on CDN/shared cache

ETag: "33a64df551425fcc55e4d42a148795d9f25f89d4"
# Unique identifier for this version of the resource
# Used for cache validation (If-None-Match)

Last-Modified: Wed, 15 Jan 2025 10:30:00 GMT
# Last time this resource was modified

Set-Cookie: session_id=abc123; Path=/; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict
# Sets a cookie on the client

Location: /api/users/124
# Used in 3xx redirects and 201 Created responses

Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://myfrontend.vercel.app
# CORS: which origins can access this resource

Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, PUT, DELETE
# CORS: which HTTP methods are allowed

Access-Control-Allow-Headers: Content-Type, Authorization
# CORS: which request headers are allowed

X-Frame-Options: DENY
# Security: prevents page from being embedded in iframes

X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff
# Security: prevents MIME type sniffing

Vary: Accept-Encoding, Accept-Language
# Tells caches which request headers affect the response
# Different Accept-Encoding = different cached response

X-RateLimit-Limit: 1000
X-RateLimit-Remaining: 997
X-RateLimit-Reset: 1737000000
# Rate limiting information (custom headers, X- prefix)
```

---

### Security Headers — Critical for Production

| Header                               | Purpose                                     | Example Value                                           |
| ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Strict-Transport-Security (HSTS)** | Force HTTPS for future visits               | `max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains`                   |
| **Content-Security-Policy (CSP)**    | Prevent XSS by controlling resource sources | `default-src 'self'; script-src 'self' cdn.example.com` |
| **X-Frame-Options**                  | Prevent clickjacking (iframe embedding)     | `DENY` or `SAMEORIGIN`                                  |
| **X-Content-Type-Options**           | Prevent MIME type sniffing                  | `nosniff`                                               |
| **Referrer-Policy**                  | Control Referer header information          | `strict-origin-when-cross-origin`                       |
| **Permissions-Policy**               | Control browser feature access              | `camera=(), microphone=()`                              |

> [!TIP]
> In Next.js, you can add security headers globally in `next.config.js` using the `headers()` function. This is important for production deployments and security audits.

```javascript
// next.config.js
const nextConfig = {
  async headers() {
    return [
      {
        source: "/(.*)",
        headers: [
          { key: "X-Frame-Options", value: "DENY" },
          { key: "X-Content-Type-Options", value: "nosniff" },
          { key: "Referrer-Policy", value: "strict-origin-when-cross-origin" },
          {
            key: "Content-Security-Policy",
            value: "default-src 'self'; script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'",
          },
        ],
      },
    ];
  },
};
```

---

### Cache-Control Header — Deep Dive

```http
Cache-Control: public, max-age=3600, s-maxage=86400, stale-while-revalidate=60
```

| Directive                  | Meaning                                                            |
| -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `public`                   | Can be cached by any cache (CDN, browser, proxy)                   |
| `private`                  | Only the end-user's browser can cache (not CDN)                    |
| `no-cache`                 | Must revalidate with server before using cache (confusingly named) |
| `no-store`                 | Do not cache at all (sensitive data)                               |
| `max-age=N`                | Cache for N seconds (client-side)                                  |
| `s-maxage=N`               | Cache for N seconds (shared/CDN cache, overrides max-age for CDNs) |
| `must-revalidate`          | Once stale, must revalidate before serving                         |
| `stale-while-revalidate=N` | Serve stale content for N seconds while revalidating in background |
| `immutable`                | Resource will never change — don't even check for updates          |

> **Hinglish:** Cache-Control ek expiry date ki tarah hai. `max-age=3600` ka matlab hai "yeh 1 ghante tak fresh hai — dubara server pe mat jaao". `no-store` ka matlab "bilkul mat bachao — hamesha server se maango".

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.6

**Q1: What is the difference between `Cache-Control: no-cache` and `Cache-Control: no-store`?**

> `no-cache` does **not** mean "don't cache" — it means "cache it, but always check with the server for validation before serving from cache." The browser sends a conditional request (with If-None-Match/ETag or If-Modified-Since) — if the server says 304 Not Modified, the cached version is served; otherwise a fresh copy is downloaded. `no-store` means "**never cache** this response at all" — no copy is stored on disk or in memory. Use `no-store` for sensitive data (bank statements, personal medical records). Use `no-cache` when content changes frequently but you still want the efficiency of conditional requests.

**Q2: What is an ETag and how does it work?**

> An ETag (Entity Tag) is a unique identifier (typically a hash of the content) that the server includes in responses. It represents the **version** of a resource. When a client has a cached response with an ETag, it sends the ETag value in the `If-None-Match` header on the next request. If the resource hasn't changed (ETags match), the server returns `304 Not Modified` with no body — saving bandwidth. If it has changed (ETags differ), the server returns `200 OK` with the new content and a new ETag.

**Q3: What is the Authorization header and how is it different from the Cookie header?**

> The `Authorization` header explicitly carries authentication credentials with each request — typically `Bearer <JWT>` for token-based auth or `Basic <base64(user:password)>` for basic auth. The client must explicitly include this header. The `Cookie` header is **automatically** sent by the browser with every request to a matching domain/path — the browser manages this. For security, `HttpOnly` cookies (not accessible via JavaScript) are often preferred for storing session tokens because they prevent XSS attacks from stealing the token.

## 1.7 HTTP Status Codes — Full Reference

<a id="17-http-status-codes--full-reference"></a>

### Overview

HTTP status codes are 3-digit numbers in the response that indicate whether the request was successful, redirected, or resulted in an error.

> **Hinglish:** Status codes response ki "receipt" hain — 200 matlab "ho gaya", 404 matlab "yeh cheez nahi mili", 500 matlab "hamare server mein kuch gadbad ho gayi".

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[HTTP Status Codes] --> B[1xx\nInformational]
    A --> C[2xx\nSuccess]
    A --> D[3xx\nRedirection]
    A --> E[4xx\nClient Error]
    A --> F[5xx\nServer Error]

    B --> B1[100 Continue\n101 Switching Protocols]
    C --> C1[200 OK\n201 Created\n204 No Content]
    D --> D1[301 Moved Permanently\n302 Found\n304 Not Modified]
    E --> E1[400 Bad Request\n401 Unauthorized\n403 Forbidden\n404 Not Found\n429 Too Many Requests]
    F --> F1[500 Internal Server Error\n502 Bad Gateway\n503 Service Unavailable]
```

---

### 1xx — Informational

| Code    | Name                | Meaning                                                          |
| ------- | ------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **100** | Continue            | Server received request headers; client should proceed with body |
| **101** | Switching Protocols | Server agrees to protocol upgrade (e.g., HTTP → WebSocket)       |
| **102** | Processing          | Server is processing, no response yet (WebDAV)                   |

---

### 2xx — Success ✅

| Code    | Name            | Meaning                            | When to use                                  |
| ------- | --------------- | ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| **200** | OK              | Request succeeded                  | Standard success for GET, PUT, PATCH         |
| **201** | Created         | Resource created                   | After successful POST                        |
| **202** | Accepted        | Request received, processing async | Background job started                       |
| **204** | No Content      | Success but no body                | After DELETE, or PUT with no response needed |
| **206** | Partial Content | Partial resource (range request)   | Video streaming, chunked downloads           |

```javascript
// Next.js Route Handler examples:
// 200 OK — standard success
return NextResponse.json({ users: [] }, { status: 200 });

// 201 Created — after creating a resource
return NextResponse.json({ id: 124, name: "Rahul" }, { status: 201 });

// 204 No Content — after delete (no body needed)
return new NextResponse(null, { status: 204 });
```

---

### 3xx — Redirection 🔀

| Code    | Name               | Meaning                           | Permanent? | Method preserved?      |
| ------- | ------------------ | --------------------------------- | ---------- | ---------------------- |
| **301** | Moved Permanently  | Resource moved to new URL forever | ✅         | ❌ (may change to GET) |
| **302** | Found              | Temporary redirect                | ❌         | ❌ (may change to GET) |
| **303** | See Other          | Redirect to GET request           | ❌         | ❌ (always GET)        |
| **304** | Not Modified       | Use cached version (no body)      | —          | —                      |
| **307** | Temporary Redirect | Temporary, preserve method        | ❌         | ✅                     |
| **308** | Permanent Redirect | Permanent, preserve method        | ✅         | ✅                     |

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **Interview Trap:** 301 vs 302 vs 307 vs 308. The key distinction is: **301/308 are permanent** (search engines update their index), **302/307 are temporary**. **301/302 may change POST to GET** on redirect (browsers historically did this), while **307/308 preserve the original method** (POST stays POST after redirect).

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    Q1{Is redirect permanent?} -->|Yes| Q2{Need to preserve HTTP method?}
    Q1 -->|No - Temporary| Q3{Need to preserve HTTP method?}
    Q2 -->|Yes - POST stays POST| A[308 Permanent Redirect]
    Q2 -->|No - POST may become GET| B[301 Moved Permanently]
    Q3 -->|Yes - POST stays POST| C[307 Temporary Redirect]
    Q3 -->|No - POST may become GET| D[302 Found]
```

**Next.js redirect examples:**

```javascript
// next.config.js
async redirects() {
  return [
    // 301 — SEO-friendly permanent redirect
    {
      source: '/old-blog/:slug',
      destination: '/blog/:slug',
      permanent: true,   // → 308 (method preserved) in Next.js
    },
    // 307 — Temporary redirect
    {
      source: '/maintenance',
      destination: '/under-construction',
      permanent: false,  // → 307
    },
  ]
}

// In Server Component or Route Handler:
import { redirect, permanentRedirect } from 'next/navigation'
redirect('/login')           // 307 Temporary
permanentRedirect('/new-url') // 308 Permanent
```

---

### 4xx — Client Errors ❌ (Client's fault)

| Code    | Name                   | Meaning                                    | Real-world Example                            |
| ------- | ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------- |
| **400** | Bad Request            | Malformed request syntax                   | Invalid JSON in body, missing required field  |
| **401** | Unauthorized           | Not authenticated                          | JWT token missing or expired                  |
| **403** | Forbidden              | Authenticated but no permission            | Regular user accessing admin panel            |
| **404** | Not Found              | Resource doesn't exist                     | `/api/users/999` where user 999 doesn't exist |
| **405** | Method Not Allowed     | HTTP method not supported                  | POST to a GET-only endpoint                   |
| **408** | Request Timeout        | Client too slow sending request            | Slow upload connection                        |
| **409** | Conflict               | Conflict with current state                | Registering with an email already in use      |
| **410** | Gone                   | Resource permanently deleted               | Blog post intentionally removed               |
| **413** | Payload Too Large      | Request body exceeds limit                 | Uploading a file bigger than server allows    |
| **415** | Unsupported Media Type | Wrong Content-Type                         | Sending XML when server expects JSON          |
| **422** | Unprocessable Entity   | Syntactically valid but semantically wrong | Valid JSON but failed business validation     |
| **429** | Too Many Requests      | Rate limit exceeded                        | More than 100 API calls/minute                |

---

### 401 vs 403 — The Most Common Confusion

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A[Client makes request] --> B{Are they logged in?}
    B -->|No - No token / Invalid token| C[401 Unauthorized\n'Who are you? Please log in']
    B -->|Yes - Valid token| D{Do they have permission?}
    D -->|No - Wrong role/scope| E[403 Forbidden\n'I know who you are,\nbut you cannot access this']
    D -->|Yes| F[200 OK ✅]
```

> **Hinglish:** 401 = "Pehle login karo, tumhe jaanta bhi nahi" | 403 = "Tum login ho, par yeh area tumhare liye nahi — VIP only zone"

---

### 400 vs 422 — Validation Errors

| Code        | 400 Bad Request                           | 422 Unprocessable Entity                            |
| ----------- | ----------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- |
| **Cause**   | Request is syntactically malformed        | Request is valid syntax but fails validation        |
| **Example** | Invalid JSON, missing Content-Type header | Valid JSON but `age: -5` or `email: "not-an-email"` |
| **When**    | Server can't even parse the request       | Server parsed it, but business rules rejected it    |

```javascript
// 400 — Can't parse the body
POST /api/users
Body: {invalid json...  // Missing closing bracket

// 422 — Parsed fine, but validation failed
POST /api/users
Body: { "email": "not-an-email", "age": -5 }
// → 422 with field-level errors
```

---

### 5xx — Server Errors 💥 (Server's fault)

| Code    | Name                  | Meaning                                      | Real-world Example                                 |
| ------- | --------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| **500** | Internal Server Error | Unhandled exception on server                | Unhandled exception, null pointer, DB query crash  |
| **501** | Not Implemented       | Feature not implemented                      | Server doesn't support the HTTP method             |
| **502** | Bad Gateway           | Upstream server returned invalid response    | Nginx couldn't reach Node.js app                   |
| **503** | Service Unavailable   | Server temporarily unable to handle requests | Server overloaded, maintenance mode                |
| **504** | Gateway Timeout       | Upstream server didn't respond in time       | Database query took too long, microservice timeout |

---

### 502 vs 503 vs 504 — Gateway Errors

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    Client[👤 Client] --> LB[Nginx\nLoad Balancer]
    LB -->|502: App returned garbage| App[Node.js App\n❌ Crashed]
    LB -->|503: App is down| App2[Node.js App\n❌ Offline]
    LB -->|504: App too slow| App3[Node.js App\n⏳ Processing DB query for 60s]
```

> **Hinglish:**
>
> - **502** — Aapne pizza order kiya, delivery boy gaya, lekin restaurant ne kuch ajeeb sa samaan diya (garbage response)
> - **503** — Restaurant band hai aaj (server offline/overloaded)
> - **504** — Restaurant se koi response nahi aaya 30 minute baad (timeout)

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.7

**Q1: What is the difference between 401 and 403?**

> **401 Unauthorized** means the client is **not authenticated** — either no credentials were provided, or the credentials are invalid/expired. The client should authenticate and try again. **403 Forbidden** means the client is **authenticated** (server knows who they are) but does **not have permission** to access the resource. Re-authenticating won't help. Example: A logged-in regular user trying to access `/admin` gets 403, not 401. A user whose JWT token expired gets 401.

**Q2: What is the difference between 301 and 308?**

> Both **301 (Moved Permanently)** and **308 (Permanent Redirect)** indicate that a resource has permanently moved to a new URL, and both tell search engines to update their index to the new URL. The difference is method handling: With **301**, browsers historically convert POST requests to GET on the redirect (the spec doesn't require this but browsers implemented it this way). **308** guarantees the **HTTP method is preserved** — a POST request redirected with 308 remains a POST to the new URL. For REST APIs, use 308 to preserve POST semantics.

**Q3: When would you return 202 Accepted vs 200 OK?**

> Return **200 OK** when the request has been **fully processed synchronously** and you can return the result immediately. Return **202 Accepted** when the server has **received and queued** the request but processing happens **asynchronously** in the background. Example: Sending a bulk email to 10,000 users — you accept the request (202) and process it in the background. The response might include a `Location` header pointing to a status endpoint where the client can poll for completion.

**Q4: What does a 304 response mean and when is it returned?**

> **304 Not Modified** is returned when a client makes a conditional request (using `If-None-Match` with an ETag, or `If-Modified-Since` with a date) and the resource has **not changed since the client's cached version**. The server sends **no body** — just the 304 status — telling the client "your cached copy is still valid, use it." This saves bandwidth (no need to re-download unchanged content) while ensuring the client always has fresh data.

**Q5 (Tricky): If a user tries to access `/admin` and is not logged in, should you return 401 or 403?**

> This is a security consideration. Technically, since the user is not authenticated, 401 is correct. However, from a security perspective, returning 401 reveals that an admin section exists. Some security-conscious applications return **404 Not Found** to unauthenticated users on admin pages, pretending the page doesn't exist to prevent information disclosure. If the user IS authenticated but lacks admin rights, return **403 Forbidden**. The "right" answer depends on your security requirements — both approaches are valid in different contexts.

[Back to Top 🔝](#table-of-contents1)

---

## 1.8 URLs, URIs & URNs

<a id="18-urls-uris--urns"></a>

### Anatomy of a URL

A **URL (Uniform Resource Locator)** is the complete address used to locate a resource on the internet.

```
https://username:password@api.example.com:8080/products/search?q=laptop&sort=price#results
│────┘  │──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────│
scheme  │user:pass│  │──host───────────││port││──────path──────│ │─query params─│ │fragment│
```

| Component           | Example                | Description                                                 |
| ------------------- | ---------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Scheme/Protocol** | `https`                | Protocol to use (http, https, ftp, mailto, ws)              |
| **Username**        | `username`             | Optional auth credentials (rarely used in URLs)             |
| **Password**        | `password`             | Optional (security risk — avoid)                            |
| **Host**            | `api.example.com`      | Domain name or IP address                                   |
| **Port**            | `8080`                 | Port number (optional; default: 80 for HTTP, 443 for HTTPS) |
| **Path**            | `/products/search`     | Resource location on server                                 |
| **Query String**    | `?q=laptop&sort=price` | Key-value pairs after `?`                                   |
| **Fragment**        | `#results`             | Client-side anchor (never sent to server)                   |

---

### URI vs URL vs URN

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    URI["URI\nUniform Resource Identifier\n(Identifies a resource)"] --> URL["URL\nUniform Resource Locator\n(Identifies + tells HOW to access it)\nhttps://example.com/page"]
    URI --> URN["URN\nUniform Resource Name\n(Identifies by name, not location)\nurn:isbn:0-486-27557-4"]
```

| Term    | Full Form                   | Purpose                                     | Example                                                          |
| ------- | --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **URI** | Uniform Resource Identifier | Identifies a resource (superset of both)    | `https://amazon.com/product/123` or `urn:isbn:978-0-596-51774-8` |
| **URL** | Uniform Resource Locator    | Identifies + tells HOW to access (location) | `https://amazon.com/product/123`                                 |
| **URN** | Uniform Resource Name       | Identifies by name, location-independent    | `urn:isbn:978-0-596-51774-8` (a book's ISBN)                     |

> **Hinglish:** URI ek umbrella term hai. URL bolta hai "kahan milega" (address deta hai), URN bolta hai "kaun sa hai" (name deta hai bina location ke). Har URL ek URI hai, lekin har URI URL nahi hai.

---

### URL Encoding

Certain characters have special meaning in URLs (`?`, `&`, `=`, `#`, `/`) and others are not allowed (spaces, `<`, `>`, `{`, `}`). URL encoding replaces these with `%XX` where XX is the hexadecimal ASCII code.

| Character | URL Encoded  | Use case                                            |
| --------- | ------------ | --------------------------------------------------- |
| Space     | `%20` or `+` | Search query with spaces                            |
| `#`       | `%23`        | When `#` appears in a query param (not as fragment) |
| `&`       | `%26`        | When `&` is part of a value, not a separator        |
| `=`       | `%3D`        | When `=` is part of a value                         |
| `/`       | `%2F`        | When `/` is part of a path value                    |
| `@`       | `%40`        | Email in query params                               |
| `+`       | `%2B`        | When `+` is a literal plus (not space)              |

```javascript
// JavaScript URL encoding:
encodeURIComponent("hello world & more?");
// → 'hello%20world%20%26%20more%3F'

encodeURI("https://example.com/path with spaces?q=a&b=c");
// → 'https://example.com/path%20with%20spaces?q=a&b=c'
// encodeURI preserves :, /, ?, &, = etc. (URL structure)
// encodeURIComponent encodes everything (for individual values)

// In Next.js:
const searchParams = new URLSearchParams({
  q: "laptop deals",
  sort: "price asc",
});
const url = `/search?${searchParams.toString()}`;
// → /search?q=laptop+deals&sort=price+asc
```

---

### Query Parameters vs Path Parameters

```
/api/products/123              ← Path parameter (resource ID)
/api/products?category=laptop  ← Query parameter (filter/search)
/api/products/123?fields=name,price ← Both together
```

| Feature       | Path Parameters                                | Query Parameters                             |
| ------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| **Syntax**    | `/users/:id` → `/users/123`                    | `/users?id=123`                              |
| **Purpose**   | Identify a specific resource                   | Filter, sort, search, paginate               |
| **Required?** | Usually required (part of route)               | Optional                                     |
| **URL style** | `/products/electronics/laptops`                | `/products?category=electronics&type=laptop` |
| **Caching**   | Easier (different URL = different cache entry) | Depends on Cache-Control                     |
| **Example**   | `/blog/react-hooks-guide`                      | `/search?q=react&page=2&limit=10`            |

```javascript
// Next.js — reading both:

// Path param: /blog/[slug]/page.tsx
export default function BlogPost({ params }: { params: { slug: string } }) {
  const { slug } = params // 'react-hooks-guide'
}

// Query param: /search?q=react&page=2
export default function SearchPage({ searchParams }: {
  searchParams: { q: string; page: string }
}) {
  const { q, page } = searchParams // 'react', '2'
}
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.8

**Q1: What is the difference between a URI and a URL?**

> A **URI (Uniform Resource Identifier)** is a string that uniquely identifies a resource — it's the **superset**. A **URL (Uniform Resource Locator)** is a type of URI that not only identifies a resource but also specifies **how to access it** (the protocol and location). All URLs are URIs, but not all URIs are URLs. A **URN** (Uniform Resource Name) is another type of URI that identifies a resource by name without specifying location. Example: `https://example.com/page` is a URL (and URI); `urn:isbn:978-0-596-51774-8` is a URN (and URI) but not a URL.

**Q2: What is the difference between `encodeURI()` and `encodeURIComponent()`?**

> `encodeURI()` encodes a **complete URL** and preserves characters that have special meaning in URL structure (`:`, `/`, `?`, `&`, `=`, `#`, `@`). Use it for encoding an entire URL. `encodeURIComponent()` encodes a **component** of a URL (like a query parameter value) and encodes **everything** including `:`, `/`, `?`, `&`, `=`. Use it for encoding individual parameter values. Example: if a query param value contains `&`, use `encodeURIComponent` so it's not interpreted as a parameter separator.

**Q3: Does the URL fragment (#) get sent to the server?**

> **No.** The fragment (everything after `#`) is a **client-side only** concept. Browsers use it for in-page navigation (scrolling to an element with that ID) but **never include it in HTTP requests to the server**. The server never sees `#section`. This is why client-side routing in React SPAs uses hash routes (`/#/about`) or the History API (which changes the URL without server requests).

## 1.9 REST API Fundamentals

<a id="19-rest-api-fundamentals"></a>

### What is an API?

An **API (Application Programming Interface)** is a set of rules and protocols that allows different software applications to communicate with each other.

> **Hinglish:** API ek waiter ki tarah hai jo restaurant (server) aur customer (client) ke beech kaam karta hai. Customer seedha kitchen nahi jaata — waiter se order deta hai. API client aur server ke beech ka intermediary hai.

### What is REST?

**REST (Representational State Transfer)** is an architectural style for designing networked applications, defined by Roy Fielding in his 2000 doctoral dissertation.

> **Hinglish:** REST ek set of rules hai — jab tum en rules follow karte hue API banate ho, toh use RESTful API kehte hain. Yeh rules ensure karte hain ki API predictable, scalable aur easy-to-use ho.

---

### REST Constraints (6 Principles)

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((REST\nConstraints))
    Stateless
      Each request has all needed info
      Server stores no session state
      Scalable and reliable
    Client-Server
      Separation of concerns
      Client handles UI
      Server handles data
    Cacheable
      Responses labeled as cacheable or not
      Reduces server load
      Improves performance
    Uniform Interface
      Resource identification in requests
      Resource manipulation via representations
      Self-descriptive messages
      HATEOAS
    Layered System
      Client unaware of intermediaries
      Load balancers, caches, proxies OK
    Code on Demand
      Optional: server can send executable code
      JavaScript, Java applets
```

| Constraint            | Description                                                                               | Real-world Impact                                              |
| --------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Stateless**         | Server stores no client state between requests; each request must contain all information | Enables horizontal scaling (any server can handle any request) |
| **Client-Server**     | Client and server are separated; client handles UI, server handles data                   | Independent evolution of frontend and backend                  |
| **Cacheable**         | Responses must indicate if they're cacheable                                              | Reduces server load (CDN caching, browser caching)             |
| **Uniform Interface** | Consistent way to interact with resources (HTTP methods + resource URLs)                  | Predictable, self-documenting APIs                             |
| **Layered System**    | Client doesn't need to know about intermediaries (proxies, load balancers)                | Allows scaling through load balancers, CDNs                    |
| **Code on Demand**    | Server can optionally send executable code (JavaScript)                                   | Optional — most APIs don't use this                            |

---

### RESTful API Design — Best Practices

```
✅ Use nouns for resources (not verbs)
   /users        (not /getUsers)
   /products     (not /fetchProducts)
   /orders/123   (not /getOrderById/123)

✅ Use HTTP methods for actions
   GET    /products         → list products
   POST   /products         → create product
   GET    /products/123     → get product 123
   PUT    /products/123     → replace product 123
   PATCH  /products/123     → update product 123 partially
   DELETE /products/123     → delete product 123

✅ Use plural nouns for collections
   /users   (not /user)
   /orders  (not /order)

✅ Nest resources for relationships
   GET /users/456/orders        → orders of user 456
   GET /users/456/orders/789    → order 789 of user 456

✅ Use query params for filtering, sorting, pagination
   GET /products?category=electronics&sort=price&order=asc&page=2&limit=20

✅ Return appropriate status codes
   200 OK, 201 Created, 204 No Content, 400, 401, 403, 404, 422, 500

✅ Version your API
   /api/v1/users
   /api/v2/users

❌ Avoid:
   /getAllUsers
   /createNewProduct
   /deleteUserById/123
   /api/user/get
```

---

### REST vs GraphQL vs gRPC vs WebSocket

| Feature            | REST                | GraphQL            | gRPC                      | WebSocket               |
| ------------------ | ------------------- | ------------------ | ------------------------- | ----------------------- |
| **Protocol**       | HTTP                | HTTP               | HTTP/2                    | TCP (ws://)             |
| **Data Format**    | JSON/XML            | JSON               | Protocol Buffers (binary) | Any (JSON, binary)      |
| **Fetching**       | Fixed endpoints     | Flexible queries   | Fixed methods             | Bidirectional streaming |
| **Over-fetching**  | Common problem      | ✅ Solved          | ❌ Not applicable         | ❌ Not applicable       |
| **Real-time**      | ❌ (polling needed) | Subscriptions      | Server streaming          | ✅ Native               |
| **Type Safety**    | Manual (OpenAPI)    | Schema-defined     | ✅ Strongly typed         | Manual                  |
| **Caching**        | ✅ HTTP caching     | Complex            | ❌ Difficult              | ❌                      |
| **Learning Curve** | Low                 | Medium             | High                      | Medium                  |
| **Best For**       | Public APIs, CRUD   | Complex data needs | Microservices             | Chat, live data         |
| **Examples**       | Twitter v1, Stripe  | GitHub, Shopify    | Google internal           | Slack, trading apps     |

> **Hinglish:**
>
> - **REST** — sabse common, simple, sab samajhte hain — jaise regular mail
> - **GraphQL** — aap exactly maango jo chahiye, zyada ya kam nahi — jaise custom order
> - **gRPC** — bahut fast, binary data, internal services ke liye — jaise walkie-talkie
> - **WebSocket** — do taraf se live baat karo — jaise phone call

---

### API Authentication Methods

| Method             | How it works                                      | Security                          | Use case                          |
| ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- | --------------------------------- |
| **API Keys**       | Static key in header/query                        | Medium (can be stolen if exposed) | Public APIs, server-to-server     |
| **Basic Auth**     | Base64(username:password) in Authorization header | Low (base64 is not encryption)    | Simple internal APIs only         |
| **Session/Cookie** | Server stores session, sends session ID in cookie | Good if httpOnly                  | Traditional web apps              |
| **OAuth 2.0**      | Token delegation (login with Google/GitHub)       | High                              | Social login, third-party access  |
| **JWT**            | Signed token with payload                         | Good                              | SPAs, mobile apps, stateless auth |

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant User
    participant Frontend as Frontend (Next.js)
    participant AuthServer as Auth Server (NextAuth)
    participant API as Protected API

    User->>Frontend: Login with Google
    Frontend->>AuthServer: OAuth 2.0 Authorization Request
    AuthServer->>User: Google Login Page
    User->>AuthServer: Enter credentials
    AuthServer->>Frontend: Authorization Code
    Frontend->>AuthServer: Exchange Code for Access Token
    AuthServer-->>Frontend: JWT Access Token
    Frontend->>API: Request + Authorization: Bearer <JWT>
    API->>API: Verify JWT signature
    API-->>Frontend: Protected Data
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.9

**Q1: What is REST? What are the 6 REST constraints?**

> REST (Representational State Transfer) is an architectural style for distributed hypermedia systems. The 6 constraints are: (1) **Stateless** — no client session stored on server; (2) **Client-Server** — separation of concerns; (3) **Cacheable** — responses must declare cacheability; (4) **Uniform Interface** — consistent resource identification and manipulation; (5) **Layered System** — client can't tell if connected directly to server or intermediary; (6) **Code on Demand** (optional) — server can send executable code.

**Q2: What is statelessness in REST and why is it important?**

> Statelessness means the server stores **no session state** between requests. Every HTTP request must contain all information necessary to process it (authentication tokens, context, etc.). This is crucial for scalability — since any server in a load-balanced cluster can handle any request without needing to share session state, you can add/remove servers dynamically. It also improves reliability — server failures don't lose user sessions.

**Q3: What is the difference between REST and GraphQL?**

> REST uses multiple fixed endpoints (one per resource type) with fixed response structures, which can lead to over-fetching (getting more data than needed) or under-fetching (needing multiple requests for related data). GraphQL uses a **single endpoint** with flexible queries — the client specifies exactly what data it needs, preventing over/under-fetching. GraphQL also has a strong type system and introspection. REST is simpler, better for caching, and more widely understood. GraphQL is better for complex, nested data requirements and rapidly changing frontends.

**Q4: Where should JWTs be stored on the client — localStorage or HttpOnly cookies?**

> This is a critical security question. **localStorage** is accessible via JavaScript, making JWTs stored there vulnerable to **XSS (Cross-Site Scripting)** attacks — if an attacker injects malicious JavaScript, they can steal the token. **HttpOnly cookies** cannot be accessed by JavaScript at all, preventing XSS theft. However, cookies are vulnerable to **CSRF (Cross-Site Request Forgery)** attacks (mitigated with SameSite cookie attribute and CSRF tokens). The recommended best practice is **HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict cookies** for storing authentication tokens.
