## 1.10 CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing)

<a id="110-cors-cross-origin-resource-sharing"></a>

### What is the Same-Origin Policy?

The **Same-Origin Policy** is a browser security mechanism that **restricts web pages from making requests to a different origin** than the one that served the web page.

**Origin = Protocol + Domain + Port**

```
https://amazon.com:443

Protocol: https
Domain:   amazon.com
Port:     443
```

| URL                           | Same origin as `https://amazon.com`? | Reason                      |
| ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------ | --------------------------- |
| `https://amazon.com/products` | ✅ Same                              | Same protocol, domain, port |
| `https://api.amazon.com`      | ❌ Different                         | Different subdomain         |
| `http://amazon.com`           | ❌ Different                         | Different protocol          |
| `https://amazon.com:8080`     | ❌ Different                         | Different port              |
| `https://flipkart.com`        | ❌ Different                         | Different domain            |

> **Hinglish:** Same-Origin Policy ek security guard hai. Browser kehta hai "agar tum `amazon.com` par ho, toh sirf `amazon.com` se data maango — kisi aur domain se nahi." Yeh XSS aur data theft se bachata hai.

---

### What is CORS?

**CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing)** is a browser mechanism that allows **servers to explicitly permit cross-origin requests** by including special HTTP headers.

> **Hinglish:** CORS ek permission system hai. Server bolta hai: "main allow karta hoon sirf `myapp.vercel.app` domain se requests aane doon." Browser phir un requests ko allow karta hai. CORS sirf browser mein hota hai — Postman/curl pe nahi hota.

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **CORS is enforced by the browser, not the server.** When you use Postman or curl, there are no CORS restrictions — only browsers enforce CORS. This is why your API might work in Postman but fail in your React app.

---

### Simple Requests vs Preflight Requests

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A[Cross-Origin Request] --> B{Is it a Simple Request?}
    B -->|Yes| C[Browser sends request directly\nNo OPTIONS preflight]
    B -->|No| D[Browser sends OPTIONS\nPreflight Request first]

    C --> E{Response has correct\nAccess-Control headers?}
    D --> F{Server responds to\nOPTIONS with approval?}

    E -->|Yes| G[✅ Request succeeds]
    E -->|No| H[❌ CORS error in browser]

    F -->|Yes| I[Browser sends actual request]
    F -->|No| J[❌ CORS error - preflight rejected]
    I --> G

    subgraph Simple["Simple Request Conditions"]
        S1[Method: GET, POST, HEAD]
        S2[Headers: only safe headers\nAccept, Accept-Language,\nContent-Language,\nContent-Type: text/plain or\napplication/x-www-form-urlencoded or\nmultipart/form-data]
    end
```

**Simple Request** — Sent directly (no preflight):

- Method: GET, HEAD, POST
- Content-Type: `text/plain`, `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, `multipart/form-data`
- No custom headers

**Preflight Request** — OPTIONS sent first:

- Any method except GET/HEAD/POST
- Content-Type: `application/json`, `application/xml`
- Custom headers like `Authorization`

---

### CORS Headers Explained

```http
-- Preflight Request (browser sends automatically) --
OPTIONS /api/users HTTP/1.1
Origin: https://myfrontend.vercel.app
Access-Control-Request-Method: POST
Access-Control-Request-Headers: Content-Type, Authorization

-- Preflight Response (server must return) --
HTTP/1.1 204 No Content
Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://myfrontend.vercel.app
Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, OPTIONS
Access-Control-Allow-Headers: Content-Type, Authorization
Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true
Access-Control-Max-Age: 86400

-- Actual Request (browser sends after successful preflight) --
POST /api/users HTTP/1.1
Origin: https://myfrontend.vercel.app
Content-Type: application/json
Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGci...

-- Actual Response --
HTTP/1.1 201 Created
Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://myfrontend.vercel.app
```

---

### CORS in Next.js

**CORS in Next.js Route Handlers:**

```typescript
// app/api/users/route.ts
import { NextRequest, NextResponse } from "next/server";

// Option 1: Manual CORS headers
export async function GET(request: NextRequest) {
  const response = NextResponse.json({ users: [] });

  response.headers.set(
    "Access-Control-Allow-Origin",
    "https://myapp.vercel.app",
  );
  response.headers.set(
    "Access-Control-Allow-Methods",
    "GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, OPTIONS",
  );
  response.headers.set(
    "Access-Control-Allow-Headers",
    "Content-Type, Authorization",
  );

  return response;
}

// Handle OPTIONS preflight
export async function OPTIONS(request: NextRequest) {
  return new NextResponse(null, {
    status: 204,
    headers: {
      "Access-Control-Allow-Origin": "https://myapp.vercel.app",
      "Access-Control-Allow-Methods": "GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, OPTIONS",
      "Access-Control-Allow-Headers": "Content-Type, Authorization",
      "Access-Control-Max-Age": "86400",
    },
  });
}
```

```javascript
// Option 2: Global CORS in next.config.js
const nextConfig = {
  async headers() {
    return [
      {
        source: "/api/:path*",
        headers: [
          {
            key: "Access-Control-Allow-Origin",
            value: "https://myapp.vercel.app",
          },
          {
            key: "Access-Control-Allow-Methods",
            value: "GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, OPTIONS",
          },
          {
            key: "Access-Control-Allow-Headers",
            value: "Content-Type, Authorization",
          },
        ],
      },
    ];
  },
};
```

> [!TIP]
> When your Next.js frontend and backend are on the **same domain** (e.g., both on `myapp.vercel.app`), there are **no CORS issues** — same-origin. CORS only matters when the API is on a different origin than the frontend.

---

### How to Fix CORS Errors

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A[CORS Error in Browser] --> B{Who controls the API?}
    B -->|You control the API| C[Add CORS headers to server response]
    B -->|Third-party API| D[Create a proxy in your Next.js app]

    C --> C1[Set Access-Control-Allow-Origin header]
    C --> C2[Handle OPTIONS preflight]

    D --> D1["/api/proxy/route.ts"\nFetch third-party API server-side\nReturn response to your frontend]
    D --> D2["Client → Your Next.js Server → Third-party API\n(No CORS — server-to-server has no CORS)"]
```

```typescript
// Proxy pattern in Next.js:
// app/api/proxy/weather/route.ts

export async function GET(request: Request) {
  const { searchParams } = new URL(request.url);
  const city = searchParams.get("city");

  // Server-to-server request — no CORS!
  const res = await fetch(
    `https://api.openweathermap.org/data/2.5/weather?q=${city}&appid=${process.env.WEATHER_API_KEY}`,
  );
  const data = await res.json();

  // Return to frontend — same origin, no CORS
  return Response.json(data);
}
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.10

**Q1: What is CORS and why does it exist?**

> CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) is a browser security mechanism that controls how web pages in one origin can request resources from a different origin. It exists because of the **Same-Origin Policy** — a critical browser security rule that prevents malicious websites from making unauthorized requests to another site on behalf of a logged-in user. Without SOP/CORS, a malicious site could make authenticated requests to your bank's API using your browser's cookies. CORS allows servers to explicitly **whitelist** trusted origins.

**Q2: Why does a CORS error not occur in Postman but does in a browser?**

> CORS is a **browser security feature** — it is enforced exclusively by web browsers. Postman, curl, server-side code, and mobile apps do not implement CORS. The Same-Origin Policy is designed to protect users from malicious web pages — if you're using Postman, you're making a deliberate direct request, not a web page trying to trick your browser into making unauthorized requests. So when you see a CORS error in your React app but not in Postman, it's because the server isn't returning the required `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` header, and only the browser enforces this restriction.

**Q3: What is a preflight request and when is it triggered?**

> A preflight request is an automatic `OPTIONS` HTTP request that the browser sends **before** the actual request to ask the server: "Can I make this cross-origin request?" It's triggered when the request is **not a simple request** — specifically when: (1) The method is PUT, DELETE, PATCH, or any non-standard method; (2) The Content-Type is `application/json`, `application/xml`, or anything other than the three allowed types; (3) Custom request headers are included (like `Authorization`). The server must respond to the OPTIONS preflight with appropriate `Access-Control-*` headers, or the browser will block the actual request.

**Q4: What is `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials` and when do you need it?**

> When a cross-origin request needs to include **credentials** (cookies, HTTP authentication, TLS certificates), the request must include `credentials: 'include'` in the Fetch API call, AND the server must respond with `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true`. Critically, when credentials are included, `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` **cannot be `*`** — it must specify an exact origin. This prevents any website from making credentialed cross-origin requests.

---

## 1.11 Cookies, Sessions & Tokens

<a id="111-cookies-sessions--tokens"></a>

### The Problem: HTTP is Stateless

HTTP is stateless — each request is independent and the server remembers nothing about previous requests. But web applications need to know **who you are** across multiple requests.

> **Hinglish:** HTTP ek bhulakkad waiter ki tarah hai — har baar aaoge, fresh start. Woh nahi jaanta tum pehle aaye the ya kya order kiya tha. Session/tokens iska solution hain.

---

### What are Cookies?

**Cookies** are small pieces of data (key-value pairs) that servers store on the client's browser. They are automatically sent with every HTTP request to the matching domain.

> **Hinglish:** Cookie ek stamp ki tarah hai jo server aapke browser par lagata hai. Har baar jab aap us site par jaate ho, browser woh stamp automatically bhejta hai — server pehchan leta hai "oh, yeh toh Rahul hai."

```http
-- Server sets a cookie:
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Set-Cookie: session_id=abc123xyz; Path=/; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict; Max-Age=86400

-- Browser automatically sends cookie on next request:
GET /dashboard HTTP/1.1
Host: example.com
Cookie: session_id=abc123xyz
```

### Cookie Attributes — Security Critical

| Attribute  | Purpose                                             | Example                                            |
| ---------- | --------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| `HttpOnly` | JavaScript cannot read this cookie (XSS protection) | `HttpOnly`                                         |
| `Secure`   | Only sent over HTTPS (never HTTP)                   | `Secure`                                           |
| `SameSite` | Controls cross-site sending (CSRF protection)       | `SameSite=Strict`, `SameSite=Lax`, `SameSite=None` |
| `Max-Age`  | Cookie lifetime in seconds                          | `Max-Age=86400` (1 day)                            |
| `Expires`  | Absolute expiration date                            | `Expires=Thu, 31 Dec 2025 23:59:59 GMT`            |
| `Path`     | URL path scope for this cookie                      | `Path=/` (all paths) or `Path=/api`                |
| `Domain`   | Domain scope                                        | `Domain=.example.com` (includes subdomains)        |

### SameSite Attribute Values

| Value    | Behavior                                             | CSRF Protection                              |
| -------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| `Strict` | Cookie only sent for same-site requests              | ✅ Best (may break OAuth redirects)          |
| `Lax`    | Cookie sent for same-site + top-level GET cross-site | ✅ Good balance (default in modern browsers) |
| `None`   | Cookie sent in all cross-site requests               | ❌ No protection (must use `Secure`)         |

### Cookie Types

| Type                   | Expires                   | Storage                                                  |
| ---------------------- | ------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Session Cookie**     | When browser closes       | Browser memory                                           |
| **Persistent Cookie**  | At specified date/Max-Age | Browser disk                                             |
| **Secure Cookie**      | —                         | Only sent over HTTPS                                     |
| **HttpOnly Cookie**    | —                         | Not accessible via JS                                    |
| **Third-party Cookie** | —                         | Set by different domain (ads/tracking, being phased out) |

---

### Session-Based Authentication

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant U as User
    participant B as Browser
    participant S as Server
    participant DB as Session DB (Redis)

    U->>B: Login (email + password)
    B->>S: POST /login {email, password}
    S->>S: Verify credentials
    S->>DB: Store session {userId, roles, expires}
    DB-->>S: session_id = "abc123"
    S-->>B: Set-Cookie: session_id=abc123; HttpOnly; Secure
    B->>B: Cookie stored

    U->>B: Visit dashboard
    B->>S: GET /dashboard\nCookie: session_id=abc123
    S->>DB: Look up session "abc123"
    DB-->>S: {userId: 456, roles: ['user']}
    S-->>B: 200 OK Dashboard HTML

    U->>B: Logout
    B->>S: POST /logout
    S->>DB: Delete session "abc123"
    S-->>B: Clear cookie (Set-Cookie: session_id=; Max-Age=0)
```

---

### Token-Based Authentication (JWT)

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant U as User
    participant B as Browser/App
    participant S as Server

    U->>B: Login (email + password)
    B->>S: POST /login {email, password}
    S->>S: Verify credentials
    S->>S: Create JWT:\n{userId:456, role:'admin', exp:1hr}
    S->>S: Sign with SECRET_KEY
    S-->>B: {accessToken: "eyJ...", refreshToken: "eyJ..."}
    B->>B: Store tokens

    U->>B: Access protected resource
    B->>S: GET /api/data\nAuthorization: Bearer eyJ...
    S->>S: Verify JWT signature\nCheck expiry
    S-->>B: 200 OK Protected Data

    Note over B,S: After 1hr, access token expires

    B->>S: POST /token/refresh\nRefresh token: eyJ...
    S->>S: Verify refresh token
    S-->>B: New access token eyJ...
```

---

### JWT (JSON Web Token) — Structure

```
eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9.eyJ1c2VySWQiOjQ1Niwicm9sZSI6ImFkbWluIiwiZXhwIjoxNzM3MDAwMDAwfQ.XYZ_SIGNATURE

│─────────── Header ────────────│.│────────────── Payload ──────────────│.│─ Signature ─│

Header (base64):    { "alg": "HS256", "typ": "JWT" }
Payload (base64):   { "userId": 456, "role": "admin", "exp": 1737000000 }
Signature:          HMACSHA256(base64(header) + "." + base64(payload), SECRET_KEY)
```

| Part          | Content                    | Visible?                | Trusted?                  |
| ------------- | -------------------------- | ----------------------- | ------------------------- |
| **Header**    | Algorithm type             | ✅ Yes (base64 decoded) | —                         |
| **Payload**   | Claims (userId, role, exp) | ✅ Yes (base64 decoded) | ❌ Don't trust unverified |
| **Signature** | Cryptographic signature    | ✅ Yes                  | ✅ Verifies integrity     |

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **The JWT payload is NOT encrypted — it is only base64 encoded.** Anyone can decode and read it. Never store sensitive data (passwords, SSNs, credit card numbers) in a JWT payload. The **signature** ensures the data hasn't been tampered with, but it doesn't hide the data.

### Access Token vs Refresh Token

| Feature            | Access Token                | Refresh Token                   |
| ------------------ | --------------------------- | ------------------------------- |
| **Lifetime**       | Short (15 minutes — 1 hour) | Long (7 days — 30 days)         |
| **Storage**        | Memory or HttpOnly cookie   | HttpOnly cookie only            |
| **Purpose**        | Authenticate API requests   | Get new access tokens           |
| **Exposed to JS?** | Ideally no                  | Never                           |
| **Sent to**        | Every API request           | Only to /token/refresh endpoint |
| **If compromised** | Low risk (expires soon)     | High risk (long-lived)          |

---

### Session-Based vs Token-Based Authentication

| Feature           | Session-Based                   | Token-Based (JWT)                |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| **Storage**       | Server (Redis/DB)               | Client (cookie/memory)           |
| **Stateless**     | ❌ No (server stores state)     | ✅ Yes                           |
| **Scalability**   | Requires shared session store   | Easier (no server state)         |
| **Revocation**    | ✅ Instant (delete session)     | ❌ Difficult (until expiry)      |
| **Mobile apps**   | ❌ Cookies tricky on mobile     | ✅ Better (Authorization header) |
| **Microservices** | ❌ Needs shared session store   | ✅ Token verified independently  |
| **Security**      | HttpOnly cookie (safe from XSS) | Careful storage needed           |

---

### Where to Store Tokens — Security Comparison

| Location            | XSS Vulnerable?       | CSRF Vulnerable?        | Recommended?                 |
| ------------------- | --------------------- | ----------------------- | ---------------------------- |
| `localStorage`      | ❌ Yes (JS can read)  | ✅ No (not auto-sent)   | ❌ No (XSS risk)             |
| `sessionStorage`    | ❌ Yes (JS can read)  | ✅ No                   | ❌ No (XSS risk)             |
| `HttpOnly Cookie`   | ✅ No (JS can't read) | ❌ Yes (needs SameSite) | ✅ **Yes** (recommended)     |
| `Memory (variable)` | ✅ No                 | ✅ No                   | ✅ Yes (but lost on refresh) |

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.11

**Q1: What is the difference between cookies and localStorage?**

> **Cookies** are small data stores managed by the browser, automatically included in every HTTP request to the matching domain, and can be set with `HttpOnly` (no JS access) and `Secure` flags. They have expiration and can scope to paths/domains. **localStorage** is a browser-side key-value store accessible only via JavaScript, never automatically sent to servers, stores more data (~5MB vs ~4KB for cookies), and persists until explicitly cleared. For authentication: cookies (especially HttpOnly) are more secure because they can't be accessed by malicious JavaScript.

**Q2: What is the difference between session-based and JWT-based authentication?**

> In **session-based auth**, the server stores session data (user ID, roles, expiry) in a database (usually Redis), and sends the client a session ID in a cookie. Every request, the server looks up the session in the DB — stateful. Sessions can be instantly revoked by deleting the DB record. In **JWT-based auth**, the server issues a signed token containing user data. The client stores and sends the token. The server verifies the signature — no DB lookup needed, stateless. JWTs are hard to revoke (must wait for expiry) and require a blacklist mechanism for immediate logout.

**Q3: Why is JWT not suitable for storing in localStorage?**

> localStorage is accessible via JavaScript — any code on your page (including malicious scripts injected via XSS) can read it: `localStorage.getItem('token')`. If your app has ANY XSS vulnerability, an attacker can steal the JWT and make authenticated requests from anywhere. HttpOnly cookies are inaccessible to JavaScript — even if XSS occurs, the cookie cannot be stolen via JS. The recommended pattern is to store JWTs in HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Lax cookies.

**Q4: What is a refresh token and why is it needed?**

> Access tokens have short lifetimes (15 min - 1 hour) to limit damage if compromised. But making users re-login every hour is bad UX. Refresh tokens solve this: they are long-lived tokens stored securely (HttpOnly cookie) and sent only to the `/token/refresh` endpoint to obtain new access tokens — never to regular API endpoints. When an access token expires, the client silently exchanges the refresh token for a new access token without user interaction. If the refresh token is also expired, the user must log in again.

## 1.12 Web Storage APIs

<a id="112-web-storage-apis"></a>

### Overview — Four Browser Storage Options

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    Storage["Browser Storage Options"] --> LC["localStorage\n5-10MB\nPersistent\nSame-origin only"]
    Storage --> SS["sessionStorage\n5-10MB\nPer tab / session\nSame-origin only"]
    Storage --> CK["Cookies\n~4KB\nAuto-sent to server\nExpiration control"]
    Storage --> IDB["IndexedDB\n50MB+\nStructured data\nAsync API"]
```

---

### localStorage

**localStorage** provides persistent key-value storage that survives browser restarts.

> **Hinglish:** localStorage ek permanent locker ki tarah hai — browser band karo, kholo, data wahi milega. Tab close karo, phir kholo, data wahi hai.

```javascript
// localStorage API
localStorage.setItem('theme', 'dark')
localStorage.setItem('user', JSON.stringify({ id: 1, name: 'Rahul' }))

localStorage.getItem('theme')          // 'dark'
JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem('user')) // { id: 1, name: 'Rahul' }

localStorage.removeItem('theme')
localStorage.clear()                   // Remove ALL items

// Check if key exists:
localStorage.getItem('nonExistent')    // null (not undefined)

// Custom hook for React:
function useLocalStorage<T>(key: string, initialValue: T) {
  const [storedValue, setStoredValue] = useState<T>(() => {
    try {
      const item = localStorage.getItem(key)
      return item ? JSON.parse(item) : initialValue
    } catch {
      return initialValue
    }
  })

  const setValue = (value: T) => {
    setStoredValue(value)
    localStorage.setItem(key, JSON.stringify(value))
  }

  return [storedValue, setValue] as const
}

// Usage:
const [theme, setTheme] = useLocalStorage('theme', 'light')
```

---

### sessionStorage

**sessionStorage** is identical to localStorage but data is **cleared when the browser tab is closed**.

> **Hinglish:** sessionStorage ek temporary locker hai — tab band karo, sab khatam. New tab kholo, woh alag sessionStorage hai (shared nahi hota tabs ke beech).

```javascript
sessionStorage.setItem("formData", JSON.stringify(formState));
sessionStorage.getItem("formData");
sessionStorage.removeItem("formData");
sessionStorage.clear();

// Use cases:
// - Multi-step form data within a single session
// - Temporary state that should not persist
// - Shopping cart before checkout confirmation
```

---

### Cookies (Web API)

```javascript
// Reading cookies (all cookies as one string):
document.cookie; // "session_id=abc123; theme=dark; user_pref=en"

// Setting a cookie:
document.cookie = "theme=dark; max-age=86400; path=/";

// Deleting a cookie (set max-age to 0):
document.cookie = "theme=; max-age=0; path=/";

// Note: HttpOnly cookies CANNOT be read or set via document.cookie
// They're only accessible server-side and auto-sent with requests
```

---

### IndexedDB

**IndexedDB** is a low-level API for storing large amounts of structured data (including files and blobs) in the browser.

> **Hinglish:** IndexedDB browser ka full database hai — bahut bada data store kar sakte hain, complex queries kar sakte hain. Usually direct use nahi karte — libraries jaise Dexie.js use karte hain.

```javascript
// Usually used via Dexie.js (wrapper):
import Dexie from "dexie";

const db = new Dexie("MyDatabase");

db.version(1).stores({
  products: "++id, name, price, category",
});

// Add data:
await db.products.add({
  name: "Laptop",
  price: 50000,
  category: "electronics",
});

// Query:
const laptops = await db.products
  .where("category")
  .equals("electronics")
  .toArray();

// Use cases: Offline apps, PWAs, large data sets, file storage
```

---

### Complete Comparison Table

| Feature                  | localStorage       | sessionStorage        | Cookies              | IndexedDB         |
| ------------------------ | ------------------ | --------------------- | -------------------- | ----------------- |
| **Capacity**             | 5-10 MB            | 5-10 MB               | ~4 KB                | 50 MB+            |
| **Persistence**          | Until cleared      | Tab close             | Expiry date          | Until cleared     |
| **Shared between tabs?** | ✅ Yes             | ❌ No                 | ✅ Yes               | ✅ Yes            |
| **Auto-sent to server?** | ❌ No              | ❌ No                 | ✅ Yes               | ❌ No             |
| **Accessible via JS?**   | ✅ Yes             | ✅ Yes                | ✅ (non-HttpOnly)    | ✅ Yes            |
| **Synchronous?**         | ✅ Sync            | ✅ Sync               | ✅ Sync              | ❌ Async          |
| **Data types**           | Strings only       | Strings only          | Strings only         | Any structured    |
| **Server-set?**          | ❌                 | ❌                    | ✅ (Set-Cookie)      | ❌                |
| **Secure for auth?**     | ❌ (XSS risk)      | ❌ (XSS risk)         | ✅ (HttpOnly)        | ❌ (XSS risk)     |
| **Use case**             | Preferences, theme | Form state, temp data | Auth tokens, session | Offline data, PWA |

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.12

**Q1: What is the difference between localStorage and sessionStorage?**

> Both are key-value stores limited to ~5MB, synchronous, and accessible only via JavaScript (same-origin). The key difference is **persistence**: **localStorage** data persists indefinitely — it survives browser restarts, tab/window closes, and is shared across all tabs of the same origin. **sessionStorage** data only lasts for the **current browser tab session** — it's cleared when the tab is closed and is NOT shared between tabs (each tab has its own sessionStorage).

**Q2: When would you use IndexedDB over localStorage?**

> Use IndexedDB when: (1) You need to store **large amounts of data** (>5MB — localStorage's limit), (2) You need to store **structured/complex data** with querying capabilities (localStorage only stores strings), (3) You need to store **files or binary data** (Blobs, ArrayBuffers), (4) You're building an **offline-first PWA** that needs to sync data with a server when back online, (5) You need **transactional operations** (atomic reads/writes). Use localStorage for simple, small key-value preferences like theme, language, or small cached data.

## 1.13 Browser Rendering Pipeline

<a id="113-browser-rendering-pipeline"></a>

### Overview — The Critical Rendering Path

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[HTML\nReceived] --> B[Parse HTML\nBuild DOM]
    A2[CSS\nReceived] --> C[Parse CSS\nBuild CSSOM]
    B --> D[Combine:\nRender Tree]
    C --> D
    D --> E[Layout\nReflow\nCalculate positions & sizes]
    E --> F[Paint\nDraw pixels for each element]
    F --> G[Composite\nLayer composition on GPU]
    G --> H[🖥️ Screen]

    style A fill:#e8f4f8
    style A2 fill:#e8f4f8
    style H fill:#90EE90
```

---

### Step 1: Parse HTML → Build DOM Tree

The browser receives HTML bytes → converts to characters → parses tokens → builds nodes → constructs the **DOM (Document Object Model)** tree.

> **Hinglish:** Browser HTML padhta hai jaise aap ek book padhte hain — line by line, element by element, ek tree structure banata hai jisme sab elements ka parent-child relationship hota hai.

```html
<!-- HTML -->
<html>
  <head>
    <title>My Page</title>
  </head>
  <body>
    <h1>Hello World</h1>
    <p>Welcome to my site</p>
  </body>
</html>
```

```
DOM Tree:
Document
└── html
    ├── head
    │   └── title
    │       └── "My Page"
    └── body
        ├── h1
        │   └── "Hello World"
        └── p
            └── "Welcome to my site"
```

> [!IMPORTANT]
> When the HTML parser encounters a `<script>` tag (without `async` or `defer`), it **pauses HTML parsing** to download and execute the script. This is why placing scripts at the bottom of `<body>` or using `async`/`defer` is important for performance.

---

### Step 2: Parse CSS → Build CSSOM

CSS is parsed and built into a **CSSOM (CSS Object Model)** tree — a map of all CSS rules and their computed values.

> **Hinglish:** CSSOM ek style guide ki tarah hai — CSS rules ko ek tree mein organize karta hai taaki har element ka exact style calculate ho sake.

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **CSS is render-blocking.** The browser cannot construct the Render Tree until the CSSOM is complete — because it needs to know styles before painting. Optimize CSS delivery by minimizing CSS files, inlining critical CSS, and removing unused CSS.

---

### Step 3: DOM + CSSOM → Render Tree

The **Render Tree** combines the DOM and CSSOM. Only **visible** elements are included — `display: none` elements and `<head>` content are excluded.

```
Render Tree (only visible elements with computed styles):
└── body { background: white; }
    ├── h1 { font-size: 32px; color: black; }
    │   └── "Hello World"
    └── p { font-size: 16px; color: gray; }
        └── "Welcome to my site"

Note: display:none elements NOT in render tree
      visibility:hidden elements ARE in render tree (but invisible)
```

---

### Step 4: Layout (Reflow) — Calculate Positions & Sizes

The browser calculates the **exact position and size** of every element on the screen (in pixels).

> **Hinglish:** Layout phase mein browser har element ki exact position aur size calculate karta hai — jaise architect blueprint par measurements likhta hai.

**What triggers reflow (expensive!):**

- Adding/removing DOM elements
- Changing element dimensions (width, height, padding, margin)
- Browser window resize
- Reading layout properties (offsetWidth, getBoundingClientRect)
- Font size changes

---

### Step 5: Paint — Draw Pixels

The browser draws pixels for each visible element — backgrounds, borders, shadows, text, images.

> **Hinglish:** Paint phase mein browser screen par actual pixels draw karta hai — jaise painter canvas par rang bharta hai.

**What triggers repaint (less expensive than reflow):**

- Color changes (background-color, color)
- Visibility changes (visibility: hidden/visible)
- Box shadows, outline changes

---

### Step 6: Composite — GPU Layer Composition

Complex pages are split into **layers** (like Photoshop layers), painted separately, and then **composited** (combined) by the GPU. Compositing is the cheapest operation.

> **Hinglish:** Composite phase mein GPU alag-alag layers ko jodta hai ek final image banane ke liye — jaise Photoshop mein alag layers ko merge karna.

**Properties that trigger compositing (GPU-accelerated, fast!):**

- `transform: translate/scale/rotate`
- `opacity`
- `will-change`
- `filter`

```css
/* ❌ Triggers reflow + repaint (expensive): */
.animate-bad {
  left: 100px; /* triggers layout */
  top: 50px; /* triggers layout */
}

/* ✅ Triggers only compositing (GPU-accelerated, cheap!): */
.animate-good {
  transform: translate(100px, 50px); /* GPU only */
}
```

---

### Reflow vs Repaint — Key Differences

|                          | Reflow                                    | Repaint                                |
| ------------------------ | ----------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- |
| **Also called**          | Layout                                    | Paint                                  |
| **What changes**         | Layout/geometry (size, position)          | Visual appearance only                 |
| **Cost**                 | Very expensive                            | Less expensive than reflow             |
| **Triggers repaint?**    | ✅ Always (layout then paint)             | ❌ Doesn't trigger reflow              |
| **Example triggers**     | width, height, margin, padding, font-size | color, background, outline, visibility |
| **CSS that avoids both** | transform, opacity (composite only)       | —                                      |

> **Hinglish:** Reflow = building ki naap lete hain phir se (mehnga hai). Repaint = building ka colour change karte hain bina naap liye (thoda sasta). Composite = GPU se kaam karte hain (sabse sasta).

---

### `async` vs `defer` on Script Tags

> **Hinglish:** HTML parser ek naive reader ki tarah hai — jab `<script>` milta hai, ruk jaata hai. `async` aur `defer` kehte hain "ruko mat, karo background mein download."

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant P as HTML Parser
    participant N as Network (Download)
    participant E as Script Execution

    Note over P,E: Regular <script> — Blocking
    P->>P: Parse HTML
    P->>N: Start downloading script
    P->>P: STOP parsing (blocked!)
    N-->>E: Script downloaded
    E->>E: Execute script
    P->>P: Resume parsing

    Note over P,E: async <script> — Non-blocking download, blocks on execute
    P->>P: Parse HTML (continues!)
    P->>N: Download script in parallel
    N-->>E: Script downloaded (pause parsing)
    E->>E: Execute script immediately
    P->>P: Resume parsing

    Note over P,E: defer <script> — Non-blocking, execute after HTML parsed
    P->>P: Parse HTML (continues!)
    P->>N: Download script in parallel
    P->>P: HTML fully parsed
    E->>E: Execute script (in order, after parse complete)
```

|                      | Regular `<script>`         | `<script async>`                        | `<script defer>`               |
| -------------------- | -------------------------- | --------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| **Download**         | Blocks HTML parsing        | Parallel (non-blocking)                 | Parallel (non-blocking)        |
| **Execution**        | Immediately (blocks parse) | Immediately when downloaded (may block) | After HTML fully parsed        |
| **Order**            | In document order          | Not guaranteed                          | Guaranteed (document order)    |
| **Best for**         | Avoid!                     | Independent scripts (analytics)         | Most scripts, page-critical JS |
| **DOMContentLoaded** | Blocks                     | Doesn't block                           | Blocks until defer scripts run |

---

### Critical Rendering Path — Optimization

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A[Optimize Critical Rendering Path] --> B[Minimize Render-Blocking Resources]
    A --> C[Minimize Bytes]
    A --> D[Minimize Critical Path Length]

    B --> B1[Move scripts to bottom\nor use async/defer]
    B --> B2[Inline critical CSS]
    B --> B3[Preload important resources\n<link rel=preload>]

    C --> C1[Minify HTML/CSS/JS]
    C --> C2[Compress with gzip/brotli]
    C --> C3[Lazy load non-critical resources]

    D --> D1[Reduce number of\ncritical resources]
    D --> D2[Reduce critical bytes]
    D --> D3[Use CDN for faster delivery]
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 1.13

**Q1: What is the Critical Rendering Path?**

> The Critical Rendering Path (CRP) is the sequence of steps the browser must complete before it can display content on the screen: (1) Build the DOM from HTML, (2) Build the CSSOM from CSS, (3) Combine them into a Render Tree, (4) Run Layout to calculate positions/sizes, (5) Paint pixels to screen, (6) Composite layers. Optimizing the CRP means reducing the number and size of render-blocking resources to get the first visible content on screen as fast as possible. This directly affects metrics like FCP (First Contentful Paint) and LCP (Largest Contentful Paint).

**Q2: What is the difference between reflow and repaint?**

> **Reflow** (also called Layout) occurs when changes require the browser to recalculate the **geometry** of elements — their position, size, and relationship to other elements. It's triggered by changes to width, height, margin, padding, font-size, adding/removing elements. Reflow always triggers a subsequent repaint. **Repaint** occurs when **visual appearance** changes without geometry changes — color, background, visibility, border-radius. It's less expensive than reflow because no layout calculations are needed. **Compositing** (transform, opacity) is cheapest — handled by GPU without reflow or repaint. Always prefer `transform` and `opacity` for animations.

**Q3: What is the difference between `async` and `defer` script attributes?**

> Both `async` and `defer` allow the HTML parser to continue while the script downloads (non-blocking download). The difference is **when** the script executes: `async` scripts execute **immediately** when downloaded — even if HTML parsing isn't complete. They may execute in any order and can interrupt parsing. Best for independent scripts (analytics, ads). `defer` scripts execute only **after HTML parsing is complete** and in document order — guaranteed. Best for scripts that depend on the DOM or depend on each other. For Next.js, `next/script` abstracts this with `afterInteractive` (like async) and `lazyOnload` strategies.

**Q4: Why is CSS render-blocking?**

> CSS is render-blocking because the browser cannot display any content until it has both the DOM (from HTML) and the CSSOM (from CSS) to construct the Render Tree. If the browser rendered content before CSS was loaded, users would see a **Flash of Unstyled Content (FOUC)** — elements appearing without styles, then suddenly reflowing when CSS loads. This would be jarring. To optimize: (1) Minimize CSS file size, (2) Remove unused CSS, (3) Inline critical above-the-fold CSS in `<head>`, (4) Load non-critical CSS with `media="print"` and change to `media="all"` with JavaScript.

**Q5 (Advanced): What is the JavaScript Event Loop and how does it relate to rendering?**

> The browser has a main thread that runs JavaScript AND handles rendering. The event loop processes tasks from the task queue (setTimeout, event handlers) one at a time. Between tasks, the browser gets a chance to re-render. If a JavaScript task takes too long (>16ms for 60fps), the browser can't render a new frame, causing jank. This is why long-running JS should be broken into smaller chunks using `setTimeout`, `requestAnimationFrame`, or `Web Workers`. React's Concurrent Mode (`useTransition`, `useDeferredValue`) helps by breaking rendering work into interruptible chunks, yielding back to the browser between chunks.

### Key Code Examples

```typescript
// app/api/demo/[method]/route.ts
// Demonstrates different HTTP methods

import { NextRequest, NextResponse } from "next/server";

export async function GET(
  request: NextRequest,
  { params }: { params: { method: string } },
) {
  const headers = Object.fromEntries(request.headers.entries());
  const url = new URL(request.url);

  return NextResponse.json({
    method: "GET",
    message: "GET request received — retrieving data",
    requestHeaders: headers,
    queryParams: Object.fromEntries(url.searchParams.entries()),
    timestamp: new Date().toISOString(),
    safe: true,
    idempotent: true,
  });
}

export async function POST(request: NextRequest) {
  const body = await request.json();

  return NextResponse.json(
    {
      method: "POST",
      message: "POST request received — creating resource",
      receivedBody: body,
      created: { id: Math.floor(Math.random() * 1000), ...body },
      safe: false,
      idempotent: false,
    },
    { status: 201 },
  );
}

export async function DELETE(request: NextRequest) {
  return new NextResponse(null, {
    status: 204,
    headers: { "X-Message": "Resource deleted" },
  });
}
```

```typescript
// app/api/status/[code]/route.ts
// Returns the requested HTTP status code

import { NextRequest, NextResponse } from "next/server";

const statusMessages: Record<number, string> = {
  200: "OK — Request succeeded",
  201: "Created — Resource created successfully",
  204: "No Content — Success with no body",
  301: "Moved Permanently — Resource has a new permanent URL",
  400: "Bad Request — Malformed request syntax",
  401: "Unauthorized — Authentication required",
  403: "Forbidden — Authenticated but access denied",
  404: "Not Found — Resource does not exist",
  422: "Unprocessable Entity — Validation failed",
  429: "Too Many Requests — Rate limit exceeded",
  500: "Internal Server Error — Server-side error",
  503: "Service Unavailable — Server temporarily down",
};

export async function GET(
  request: NextRequest,
  { params }: { params: { code: string } },
) {
  const statusCode = parseInt(params.code);

  if (isNaN(statusCode) || statusCode < 100 || statusCode > 599) {
    return NextResponse.json({ error: "Invalid status code" }, { status: 400 });
  }

  const message = statusMessages[statusCode] ?? `HTTP ${statusCode}`;

  if (statusCode === 204) {
    return new NextResponse(null, { status: 204 });
  }

  if (statusCode >= 300 && statusCode < 400) {
    return NextResponse.redirect(new URL("/", request.url), {
      status: statusCode,
    });
  }

  return NextResponse.json(
    { statusCode, message, timestamp: new Date().toISOString() },
    { status: statusCode },
  );
}
```

```typescript
// components/StorageExplorer.tsx
'use client'

import { useState, useEffect } from 'react'

export default function StorageExplorer() {
  const [localData, setLocalData] = useState<Record<string, string>>({})
  const [sessionData, setSessionData] = useState<Record<string, string>>({})
  const [newKey, setNewKey] = useState('')
  const [newValue, setNewValue] = useState('')
  const [storageType, setStorageType] = useState<'local' | 'session'>('local')

  useEffect(() => {
    refreshData()
  }, [])

  const refreshData = () => {
    // Read localStorage
    const local: Record<string, string> = {}
    for (let i = 0; i < localStorage.length; i++) {
      const key = localStorage.key(i)!
      local[key] = localStorage.getItem(key)!
    }
    setLocalData(local)

    // Read sessionStorage
    const session: Record<string, string> = {}
    for (let i = 0; i < sessionStorage.length; i++) {
      const key = sessionStorage.key(i)!
      session[key] = sessionStorage.getItem(key)!
    }
    setSessionData(session)
  }

  const handleSet = () => {
    if (!newKey || !newValue) return
    const storage = storageType === 'local' ? localStorage : sessionStorage
    storage.setItem(newKey, newValue)
    refreshData()
    setNewKey('')
    setNewValue('')
  }

  const handleDelete = (key: string, type: 'local' | 'session') => {
    const storage = type === 'local' ? localStorage : sessionStorage
    storage.removeItem(key)
    refreshData()
  }

  return (
    <div className="p-6">
      <h2 className="text-2xl font-bold mb-4">Web Storage Explorer</h2>

      {/* Add new item */}
      <div className="flex gap-2 mb-6">
        <select
          value={storageType}
          onChange={(e) => setStorageType(e.target.value as 'local' | 'session')}
          className="border rounded px-3 py-2"
        >
          <option value="local">localStorage</option>
          <option value="session">sessionStorage</option>
        </select>
        <input
          placeholder="Key"
          value={newKey}
          onChange={(e) => setNewKey(e.target.value)}
          className="border rounded px-3 py-2 flex-1"
        />
        <input
          placeholder="Value"
          value={newValue}
          onChange={(e) => setNewValue(e.target.value)}
          className="border rounded px-3 py-2 flex-1"
        />
        <button
          onClick={handleSet}
          className="bg-blue-500 text-white px-4 py-2 rounded"
        >
          Set Item
        </button>
      </div>

      {/* Display storage contents */}
      <div className="grid grid-cols-2 gap-6">
        <StorageTable
          title="localStorage (Persistent)"
          data={localData}
          type="local"
          onDelete={handleDelete}
        />
        <StorageTable
          title="sessionStorage (Tab only)"
          data={sessionData}
          type="session"
          onDelete={handleDelete}
        />
      </div>
    </div>
  )
}

function StorageTable({ title, data, type, onDelete }: {
  title: string
  data: Record<string, string>
  type: 'local' | 'session'
  onDelete: (key: string, type: 'local' | 'session') => void
}) {
  return (
    <div className="border rounded-lg p-4">
      <h3 className="font-semibold mb-3">{title}</h3>
      {Object.keys(data).length === 0 ? (
        <p className="text-gray-500 italic">Empty</p>
      ) : (
        <table className="w-full text-sm">
          <thead>
            <tr className="border-b">
              <th className="text-left py-1">Key</th>
              <th className="text-left py-1">Value</th>
              <th></th>
            </tr>
          </thead>
          <tbody>
            {Object.entries(data).map(([key, value]) => (
              <tr key={key} className="border-b last:border-0">
                <td className="py-2 font-mono text-blue-600">{key}</td>
                <td className="py-2 font-mono text-green-600 truncate max-w-[150px]">{value}</td>
                <td>
                  <button
                    onClick={() => onDelete(key, type)}
                    className="text-red-500 hover:text-red-700 text-xs"
                  >
                    Delete
                  </button>
                </td>
              </tr>
            ))}
          </tbody>
        </table>
      )}
    </div>
  )
}
```

### 2 Machine Coding Problems

**MCP 1: HTTP Status Code Explorer**

Build a Next.js page that:

- Shows all HTTP status codes organized by category (1xx, 2xx, 3xx, 4xx, 5xx)
- Each status code is clickable — sends an actual request to `/api/status/[code]` and shows the response
- Color-codes by category (green = 2xx, yellow = 3xx, orange = 4xx, red = 5xx)
- Shows the response headers in a formatted table
- Includes a search/filter functionality
- Shows a brief description of when each code is used

Requirements:

- Must be a Next.js App Router project
- Route handler must return the correct status code
- Client component handles user interaction
- Server component renders the initial status code list

**MCP 2: Web Storage Sync Dashboard**

Build a Next.js page that:

- Has three panels: localStorage, sessionStorage, and Cookie display
- Allows adding/reading/deleting items in each storage type
- Shows real-time character/byte count (localStorage limit awareness)
- Demonstrates that sessionStorage is NOT shared between browser tabs (add an instruction and "Open New Tab" button)
- Shows cookie attributes (HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite) and which are set server-side vs client-side
- Includes a "Clear All" button per storage type with a confirmation
- Persists a "visit count" in localStorage to demonstrate persistence across refreshes

Requirements:

- Use Next.js App Router
- Route handler for setting HttpOnly cookies (server-side)
- Client component for localStorage/sessionStorage (browser-only APIs)
- TypeScript throughout

---

> [!NOTE]
> **Chapter Summary — Key Takeaways:**
>
> 1. The Internet is infrastructure; the WWW is a service on top of it
> 2. DNS resolves domain names to IP addresses through a hierarchical system
> 3. HTTPS = HTTP + TLS; provides encryption, authentication, and integrity
> 4. HTTP methods: GET (read), POST (create), PUT (replace), PATCH (update), DELETE (remove)
> 5. Status codes: 2xx success, 3xx redirect, 4xx client error, 5xx server error
> 6. CORS is enforced by browsers only — it protects against cross-origin requests
> 7. Store auth tokens in HttpOnly cookies, NOT localStorage (XSS protection)
> 8. JWT payload is base64 encoded, NOT encrypted — never store sensitive data in it
> 9. Reflow → Repaint → Composite — avoid reflow with transform/opacity animations
> 10. Use `defer` for most scripts; `async` only for truly independent scripts

## 2.1 JavaScript Engine

<a id="21-javascript-engine"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic                | What You'll Learn                                   |
| ----------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- |
| What is a JS Engine?    | Definition, major engines, which browser uses which |
| Parsing Phase           | Tokenization → AST (Abstract Syntax Tree)           |
| Compilation Phase       | Ignition Interpreter → TurboFan JIT Compiler        |
| Execution Phase         | How bytecode runs on the CPU                        |
| JIT Compilation         | How JS became fast — mixed interpreter + compiler   |
| Interpreter vs Compiler | Key differences and tradeoffs                       |

---

### What is a JavaScript Engine?

A **JavaScript Engine** is a program (or interpreter) that reads, understands, and executes JavaScript code. It translates human-written JavaScript into machine code that the computer's CPU can execute.

> **Hinglish:** JS Engine ek translator ki tarah hai — tumhara JavaScript code (jo human-readable hai) ko computer ki language (machine code) mein convert karta hai. Jaise Hindi-to-English translator.

### Major JavaScript Engines

| Engine                     | Created By | Used In                     | Language Written In |
| -------------------------- | ---------- | --------------------------- | ------------------- |
| **V8**                     | Google     | Chrome, Node.js, Edge, Deno | C++                 |
| **SpiderMonkey**           | Mozilla    | Firefox                     | C++, Rust           |
| **JavaScriptCore (Nitro)** | Apple      | Safari, React Native        | C++                 |
| **Chakra**                 | Microsoft  | Internet Explorer (legacy)  | C++                 |
| **Hermes**                 | Meta       | React Native (Android)      | C++                 |

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **Interview Insight:** V8 is the most widely used engine and the one you'll encounter in both browser (Chrome) and server-side (Node.js) development. React Native uses JavaScriptCore on iOS and Hermes on Android.

---

### Parsing → Compilation → Execution — The Complete Pipeline

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A["📄 JavaScript Source Code\nconst x = 10 + 5"] --> B

    subgraph PARSE["PHASE 1: PARSING"]
        B["Lexer / Tokenizer\nBreaks code into tokens"] --> C
        B_EX["Tokens: [const] [x] [=] [10] [+] [5]"]
        C["Parser\nBuilds Abstract Syntax Tree AST"]
        C_EX["AST: VariableDeclaration\n└── VariableDeclarator\n    ├── id: x\n    └── init: BinaryExpression\n        ├── left: 10\n        ├── op: +\n        └── right: 5"]
    end

    subgraph COMPILE["PHASE 2: COMPILATION"]
        D["Ignition Interpreter\nConverts AST → Bytecode"] --> E
        E{"Hot Code?\nCalled many times?"}
        E -->|Yes: Hot Path| F["TurboFan JIT Compiler\nOptimizes → Machine Code"]
        E -->|No: Cold Path| G["Run as Bytecode\nFaster to start"]
    end

    subgraph EXECUTE["PHASE 3: EXECUTION"]
        H["CPU executes\nMachine Code / Bytecode"]
        I["Result: x = 15"]
    end

    C --> D
    F --> H
    G --> H
    H --> I

    style PARSE fill:#e8f4f8,stroke:#2196F3
    style COMPILE fill:#fff3e0,stroke:#FF9800
    style EXECUTE fill:#e8f5e9,stroke:#4CAF50
```

---

### Phase 1: Parsing — Detailed

**Step 1 — Lexical Analysis (Tokenization):**

The Lexer reads source code character by character and groups them into **tokens** (the smallest meaningful units).

> **Hinglish:** Lexer code ko words mein todta hai — jaise tum ek sentence ko alag-alag words mein tod do. `const x = 10` → `[const]`, `[x]`, `[=]`, `[10]`

```javascript
// Source Code:
const greeting = "Hello" + " World";

// Tokens produced by Lexer:
// [KEYWORD: const] [IDENTIFIER: greeting] [PUNCTUATOR: =]
// [STRING: "Hello"] [PUNCTUATOR: +] [STRING: " World"]
```

**Step 2 — Syntax Analysis (AST Building):**

The Parser takes the token stream and builds an **Abstract Syntax Tree (AST)** — a tree representation of the program structure.

> **Hinglish:** AST ek family tree ki tarah hai — code ka structure dikhata hai ki kaun kiska child hai. `a + b * c` ka AST dikhayega ki `*` pehle hoga (higher precedence).

```javascript
// You can visualize AST at: astexplorer.net

// For: const x = 10 + 5
{
  "type": "VariableDeclaration",
  "kind": "const",
  "declarations": [{
    "type": "VariableDeclarator",
    "id": { "type": "Identifier", "name": "x" },
    "init": {
      "type": "BinaryExpression",
      "operator": "+",
      "left": { "type": "Literal", "value": 10 },
      "right": { "type": "Literal", "value": 5 }
    }
  }]
}
```

> [!NOTE]
> Tools like Babel (used by Next.js to transform JSX), ESLint, and Prettier ALL work by parsing your code into an AST, transforming it, and generating new code. Understanding AST helps you understand how these tools work.

---

### Phase 2: Compilation — JIT Deep Dive

#### Interpreter vs Compiler — Core Difference

| Feature             | Interpreter                          | Compiler                                     |
| ------------------- | ------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------- |
| **How it works**    | Reads and executes code line by line | Translates entire code to machine code first |
| **Start time**      | Fast (no pre-compilation)            | Slow (must compile first)                    |
| **Execution speed** | Slower (no optimization)             | Faster (fully optimized)                     |
| **Example**         | Early JavaScript engines             | C, C++, Go, Rust                             |
| **Error detection** | At runtime (line by line)            | Before execution (compile time)              |

> **Hinglish:** Interpreter ek live translator ki tarah hai — ek ek line padho, ek ek line translate karo. Compiler poori book pehle translate karta hai, phir read karo — slow start, fast run.

#### JIT (Just-In-Time) Compilation — The Best of Both Worlds

JavaScript started as a purely interpreted language (slow). Modern engines use **JIT compilation** — a hybrid approach that combines interpreter speed-to-start with compiler optimization.

> **Hinglish:** JIT ka matlab hai "sahi waqt par compile karo." Pehle interpreter se chalao (fast start), phir jo code baar baar use ho raha hai usse compile karke optimize karo (fast run).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph V8["V8 Engine — JIT Pipeline"]
        A["AST"] --> B

        subgraph IGNITION["Ignition — Interpreter"]
            B["AST → Bytecode\nFast to generate\nStarts execution immediately"]
        end

        subgraph PROFILER["Profiler / Monitor"]
            C["Watches execution\nCounts function calls\nIdentifies hot functions"]
        end

        subgraph TURBOFAN["TurboFan — Optimizing Compiler"]
            D["Hot Bytecode → Machine Code\nInline caching\nType specialization\nDead code elimination"]
        end

        subgraph DEOPT["Deoptimization"]
            E["If type assumption wrong\nFall back to bytecode\nRe-optimize if needed"]
        end

        B --> C
        C -->|"Hot: called >1000 times"| D
        D --> E
        E -->|"Re-profile"| C
    end

    style IGNITION fill:#e3f2fd
    style TURBOFAN fill:#e8f5e9
    style DEOPT fill:#fff3e0
```

**How JIT Optimization Works in Practice:**

```javascript
// V8 sees this function being called thousands of times:
function add(a, b) {
  return a + b;
}

// First few calls: interpreted as bytecode
add(1, 2); // V8: "numbers — I see a pattern"
add(3, 4); // V8: "still numbers"
add(5, 6); // V8: "definitely a number-adding function"

// V8 OPTIMIZES: assumes a and b are always numbers
// Generates machine code: ADD_INT r1, r2 (very fast!)

// If you break the assumption:
add("hello", "world"); // DEOPTIMIZATION!
// V8: "I was wrong — falling back to bytecode, re-profiling"
```

> [!TIP]
> **Performance Tip for React:** Avoid mixing types in functions that are called frequently (like render functions or array mappers). Type consistency allows V8 to keep its JIT optimizations active. This is one reason TypeScript improves runtime performance indirectly — it encourages type consistency.

---

### Real-World Analogy

> **Amazon's Recommendation Engine:** The first time Amazon shows you recommendations, it uses a general algorithm (interpreter — fast start). As it collects more data about your behavior (profiling), it optimizes the recommendations specifically for you (JIT compilation — hot path optimization). If your tastes change suddenly (deoptimization), it adjusts.

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 2.1

**Q1: What is a JavaScript Engine and how does it differ from a JavaScript Runtime?**

> A **JavaScript Engine** is the core component that parses and executes JavaScript code — it handles lexing, parsing, compilation, and execution. V8 (Chrome/Node.js), SpiderMonkey (Firefox), and JavaScriptCore (Safari) are examples. A **JavaScript Runtime** is the complete environment that provides context for JS execution — it includes the engine PLUS additional APIs: Web APIs (browser) or built-in modules (Node.js), Event Loop, and the Callback/Task queues. The engine alone can only run pure JavaScript; the runtime adds I/O capabilities, timers, network access, etc.

**Q2: What is JIT compilation and why does it make JavaScript fast?**

> JIT (Just-In-Time) compilation is a hybrid execution strategy that combines the fast start-up of interpretation with the fast execution speed of compilation. The engine first interprets code into bytecode (fast to start), then profiles execution to identify "hot" code paths (functions called frequently). Hot code is compiled to highly optimized native machine code at runtime. This means JavaScript avoids the slow startup of full ahead-of-time compilation while still achieving near-native speeds for frequently executed code.

**Q3: What is deoptimization in V8 and how can you avoid it?**

> Deoptimization occurs when V8's JIT compiler made assumptions about data types during optimization that turn out to be incorrect. For example, V8 might optimize a function assuming arguments are always integers, generating fast integer-specific machine code. If the function is then called with a string, V8 must throw away the optimized code and fall back to slower bytecode interpretation, then re-profile. Avoid deoptimization by: maintaining consistent argument types in frequently called functions, avoiding `try/catch` in hot functions (V8 cannot optimize them well), and not using `arguments` object in performance-critical code.

**Q4: What is an AST and where is it used in the React/Next.js ecosystem?**

> An AST (Abstract Syntax Tree) is a tree-based representation of the syntactic structure of source code, generated during the parsing phase. Each node represents a construct in the code (variable declaration, function call, expression, etc.). In React/Next.js: **Babel** parses JSX into an AST and transforms it to `React.createElement()` calls. **ESLint** parses code into AST to find pattern-based issues. **Prettier** parses to AST and regenerates formatted code. **Next.js SWC compiler** (written in Rust) performs AST transformations for code splitting and optimization.

**Q5: What is the difference between Ignition and TurboFan in V8?**

> **Ignition** is V8's interpreter — it takes the AST and generates compact bytecode, then begins executing it immediately. This provides fast startup because no full compilation is needed. **TurboFan** is V8's optimizing JIT compiler — it takes hot bytecode (code that runs frequently, identified by the profiler) and compiles it to highly optimized machine code using techniques like inline caching, type specialization, loop unrolling, and dead code elimination. Together they form V8's two-tier execution system: Ignition for fast start, TurboFan for sustained performance.

## 2.2 JavaScript Runtime Environment

<a id="22-javascript-runtime-environment"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic                   | What You'll Learn                                    |
| -------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- |
| Browser vs Node.js Runtime | What each runtime provides beyond the JS engine      |
| Call Stack                 | LIFO structure, stack frames, stack overflow         |
| Memory Heap                | Where objects live, garbage collection basics        |
| Web APIs                   | setTimeout, fetch, DOM — provided by browser, not JS |
| Callback Queue             | Macrotask queue — where timer callbacks wait         |
| Microtask Queue            | Promise queue — higher priority than macrotask queue |

---

### What is the JavaScript Runtime Environment?

The **JavaScript Runtime Environment** is the complete ecosystem in which JavaScript code executes. It consists of:

1. **JavaScript Engine** (V8, SpiderMonkey) — executes JS code
2. **Call Stack** — tracks function calls
3. **Memory Heap** — stores objects and data
4. **Web APIs / Node APIs** — provided by the host environment (not JS itself)
5. **Event Loop** — coordinates asynchronous execution
6. **Callback/Task Queues** — hold pending callbacks

> **Hinglish:** Runtime Environment ek complete workshop ki tarah hai. Engine sirf ek machine hai — runtime mein aur bhi cheezein hain: raw material storage (heap), kaam ki list (call stack), bahar se kaam aane ki jagah (Web APIs), aur queue manager (Event Loop).

---

### Browser Runtime vs Node.js Runtime

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph BROWSER["🌐 Browser Runtime"]
        BE["V8 / SpiderMonkey\nJS Engine"]
        BCS["Call Stack"]
        BMH["Memory Heap"]
        BWAPI["Web APIs\n• DOM API\n• setTimeout / setInterval\n• fetch / XMLHttpRequest\n• localStorage / sessionStorage\n• Geolocation / Camera\n• Canvas / WebGL\n• Notifications"]
        BCQ["Callback Queue\n(Macrotask)"]
        BMQ["Microtask Queue"]
        BEL["Event Loop"]

        BE --- BCS
        BE --- BMH
        BCS <--> BEL
        BWAPI --> BCQ
        BCQ <--> BEL
        BMQ <--> BEL
    end

    subgraph NODE["🟢 Node.js Runtime"]
        NE["V8 JS Engine"]
        NCS["Call Stack"]
        NMH["Memory Heap"]
        NAPI["Node.js APIs\n• fs (file system)\n• http / https\n• path / os\n• crypto\n• child_process\n• streams\n• buffer"]
        NCQ["Callback Queue\n(libuv event loop)"]
        NMQ["Microtask Queue"]
        NEL["Event Loop\n(libuv)"]
        NPM["npm packages"]

        NE --- NCS
        NE --- NMH
        NCS <--> NEL
        NAPI --> NCQ
        NCQ <--> NEL
        NMQ <--> NEL
        NPM --> NE
    end

    style BROWSER fill:#e3f2fd,stroke:#1565C0
    style NODE fill:#e8f5e9,stroke:#2E7D32
```

| Feature              | Browser Runtime                        | Node.js Runtime                     |
| -------------------- | -------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------- |
| **JS Engine**        | V8 (Chrome), SpiderMonkey (Firefox)    | V8                                  |
| **Host APIs**        | DOM, Web APIs, localStorage, fetch     | fs, http, crypto, path, os          |
| **Event Loop**       | Browser's built-in event loop          | libuv (C++ library)                 |
| **Global Object**    | `window`                               | `global` / `globalThis`             |
| **Module System**    | ES Modules (native in modern browsers) | CommonJS + ES Modules               |
| **Timer**            | `setTimeout` (Web API)                 | `setTimeout` (libuv)                |
| **File Access**      | ❌ Not allowed (sandboxed)             | ✅ `fs` module                      |
| **Network**          | `fetch`, `XMLHttpRequest`              | `http`, `https`, `fetch` (Node 18+) |
| **Use case**         | Web UI, client-side interaction        | Server, CLI, build tools            |
| **`this` in global** | `window`                               | `{}` (empty object in modules)      |

---

### Call Stack — How Function Calls Work

The **Call Stack** is a **LIFO (Last In, First Out)** data structure that tracks the currently executing functions. When a function is called, a **stack frame** is pushed onto the stack. When it returns, the frame is popped off.

> **Hinglish:** Call Stack ek stack of plates ki tarah hai. Jab function call hota hai, ek plate upar rakhte hain. Jab function return karta hai, woh plate hata lete hain. Hamesha sabse upar wali plate pehle hategi — LIFO.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant CS as Call Stack
    participant Code as Code Execution

    Code->>CS: main() starts → push main
    Note over CS: [main]
    Code->>CS: greet() called → push greet
    Note over CS: [main, greet]
    Code->>CS: getName() called → push getName
    Note over CS: [main, greet, getName]
    CS->>Code: getName() returns "Rahul" → pop getName
    Note over CS: [main, greet]
    CS->>Code: greet() returns "Hello Rahul" → pop greet
    Note over CS: [main]
    CS->>Code: main() completes → pop main
    Note over CS: []
```

```javascript
function getName() {
  return "Rahul"; // Stack: [main, greet, getName]
}

function greet() {
  const name = getName(); // Stack: [main, greet] → calls getName
  return `Hello ${name}`;
}

function main() {
  const message = greet(); // Stack: [main] → calls greet
  console.log(message);
}

main(); // Entry point → pushed to stack first
// Output: "Hello Rahul"
```

**Stack Overflow — What is it?**

```javascript
// ❌ Infinite recursion → Stack Overflow
function infiniteRecurse() {
  return infiniteRecurse(); // Keeps pushing, never popping
}

infiniteRecurse();
// RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded

// ✅ Recursion with base case (safe)
function factorial(n) {
  if (n <= 1) return 1; // Base case: stop recursion
  return n * factorial(n - 1); // Recursive case
}

factorial(5); // 120 — stack: [factorial(5), factorial(4), factorial(3), factorial(2), factorial(1)]
```

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **Single-Threaded:** JavaScript has **ONE call stack** — it can only do **one thing at a time**. This is why long-running synchronous operations (like processing a huge array) block the entire UI. This is where asynchronous programming (Event Loop) becomes critical.

---

### Memory Heap — Where Objects Live

The **Memory Heap** is a large unstructured region of memory where JavaScript stores **objects, arrays, and functions** (reference types). Primitive values (`number`, `string`, `boolean`, etc.) are stored in the stack (or engine-optimized locations).

> **Hinglish:** Memory Heap ek bada warehouse hai jahan sab objects rakhte hain. Har object ka ek address hota hai (reference). Stack mein sirf yeh address store hota hai, actual cheez warehouse (heap) mein hoti hai.

```javascript
// Primitives — stored by VALUE (in stack/register):
let a = 10;
let b = a; // b gets a COPY of value 10
b = 20;
console.log(a); // 10 — a is unchanged (value copy)

// Objects — stored by REFERENCE (in heap):
let obj1 = { name: "Rahul" }; // heap: { name: "Rahul" } @ address 0x1234
let obj2 = obj1; // obj2 gets SAME ADDRESS 0x1234 (not a copy!)
obj2.name = "Priya";
console.log(obj1.name); // "Priya" — obj1 ALSO changed! (same reference)
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph STACK["Call Stack"]
        S1["a = 10"]
        S2["b = 20"]
        S3["obj1 → 0x1234"]
        S4["obj2 → 0x1234"]
    end

    subgraph HEAP["Memory Heap"]
        H1["0x1234: { name: 'Priya' }"]
        H2["0xABCD: [1, 2, 3]"]
        H3["0xEF01: function greet(){}"]
    end

    S3 -->|reference| H1
    S4 -->|same reference| H1

    style STACK fill:#fff3e0
    style HEAP fill:#e8f5e9
```

**Garbage Collection:**

V8 uses a **Mark and Sweep** garbage collector. Objects that are no longer reachable from any reference are automatically freed.

> **Hinglish:** Garbage collector ek cleaner ki tarah hai — jo objects kisi ke kaam nahi aa rahe (koi reference nahi hai unka), unhe memory se hata deta hai automatically.

```javascript
function createUser() {
  let user = { name: "Rahul", age: 25 }; // Created in heap
  return user.name; // Only returns the string
  // 'user' object goes out of scope → no references left
  // Garbage Collector will free this memory
}

let name = createUser(); // name = "Rahul"
// { name: "Rahul", age: 25 } object is now eligible for GC
```

---

### Web APIs — Not Part of JavaScript!

**Web APIs** are provided by the **browser** (not the JS engine). They allow JavaScript to interact with the browser and the outside world.

> **Hinglish:** Web APIs browser ka gift hai JavaScript ko — JavaScript khud timer nahi baana sakti, DOM access nahi kar sakti, network request nahi bhej sakti. Yeh sab browser provide karta hai Web APIs ke through.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    JS["JavaScript Code\n(V8 Engine)"] -->|calls| WEBAPI

    subgraph WEBAPI["Web APIs (Browser Provided)"]
        T["⏱️ setTimeout / setInterval\nTimer API"]
        F["🌐 fetch / XMLHttpRequest\nNetwork API"]
        D["📄 DOM API\ndocument.getElementById\naddEventListener"]
        S["💾 Storage API\nlocalStorage / sessionStorage"]
        G["📍 Geolocation API\nnavigator.geolocation"]
        N["🔔 Notifications API"]
        W["👷 Web Workers API"]
    end

    T -->|"callback after delay"| CQ["Callback Queue"]
    F -->|"callback after response"| CQ
    D -->|"event handler callback"| CQ

    style WEBAPI fill:#e3f2fd
    style JS fill:#fff9c4
```

**Key Insight — setTimeout is NOT JavaScript:**

```javascript
// setTimeout is a WEB API call — not a JS function
setTimeout(() => {
  console.log("I'm a Web API callback!");
}, 1000);

// What ACTUALLY happens:
// 1. JS calls setTimeout → hands off to Browser's Timer API
// 2. Browser starts 1000ms timer (outside JS engine)
// 3. JS engine continues with next code (NOT blocked!)
// 4. After 1000ms, browser puts callback in Callback Queue
// 5. Event Loop moves callback to Call Stack when stack is empty
```

---

### Callback Queue (Macrotask Queue)

The **Callback Queue** (also called **Task Queue** or **Macrotask Queue**) holds callbacks from Web APIs that are ready to execute — like timer callbacks, I/O callbacks, and UI events.

> **Hinglish:** Callback Queue ek waiting room ki tarah hai — Web API ka kaam ho gaya, ab callback yahan wait karta hai jab tak Call Stack free ho.

**Sources of Macrotasks:**

- `setTimeout()` callback
- `setInterval()` callback
- `setImmediate()` (Node.js only)
- I/O callbacks (`fs.readFile`, network responses)
- UI rendering events
- `MessageChannel` callbacks

---

### Microtask Queue

The **Microtask Queue** holds callbacks from Promises and other high-priority async operations. It is processed **completely before** any macrotask is taken from the Callback Queue.

> **Hinglish:** Microtask Queue ek VIP queue hai — jab bhi Event Loop kuch bhi karne se pehle, pehle yeh queue khali karni padti hai. Promise callbacks hamesha setTimeout se pehle chalte hain.

**Sources of Microtasks:**

- `Promise.then()` / `Promise.catch()` / `Promise.finally()`
- `async/await` (internally uses Promises)
- `queueMicrotask(fn)`
- `MutationObserver` callbacks
- `queueReactionJob` (used by Proxy)

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 2.2

**Q1: Why is JavaScript called single-threaded? What are the implications?**

> JavaScript is single-threaded because it has exactly **one Call Stack** — it can execute only one piece of code at any given moment. Implications: (1) Long-running synchronous operations (large loops, complex calculations) **block the entire UI** — the page freezes and cannot respond to user input, (2) This is why asynchronous programming is critical — operations like network requests must be non-blocking, (3) No race conditions for shared data (unlike multi-threaded languages), (4) For CPU-intensive tasks, **Web Workers** can be used to run code in separate threads.

**Q2: What is the difference between the Stack and the Heap in JavaScript memory?**

> The **Call Stack** stores primitive values, function parameters, local variables, and references (pointers to heap objects). It's structured (LIFO), fast, and has limited size (causing Stack Overflow if exceeded). The **Heap** is a large, unstructured memory region where objects, arrays, and functions are stored. It's managed by the garbage collector. When you create an object, it's stored in the heap, and the stack holds a reference (memory address) to it. This is why objects are "passed by reference" — you're passing the heap address, not a copy of the object.

**Q3: If `setTimeout` is not part of JavaScript, where does it come from?**

> `setTimeout` is provided by the **host environment** (browser or Node.js) as a **Web API** (browser) or **libuv API** (Node.js). When JavaScript calls `setTimeout(fn, delay)`, the JS engine hands the callback and delay to the browser's Timer API, then immediately continues executing the next line — the timer runs in a separate thread managed by the browser. After the delay, the browser places the callback in the **Callback Queue**. The JavaScript engine never directly executes the timer — it only executes the callback when the Event Loop moves it to the Call Stack.

**Q4: What is a Memory Leak in JavaScript and how can you prevent it?**

> A memory leak occurs when objects that are no longer needed remain reachable and thus cannot be garbage collected, causing memory usage to grow over time. Common causes: (1) **Global variables** — accidentally creating global variables that hold references, (2) **Forgotten event listeners** — `addEventListener` without corresponding `removeEventListener`, (3) **Closures holding references** — a closure keeping a reference to a large object that should be freed, (4) **setInterval without clearInterval** — keeps running and holding references. Prevention: Use `WeakMap`/`WeakSet` for object caches, always clean up event listeners in React's `useEffect` cleanup, and clear intervals/timeouts.

**Q5: What is the difference between the Microtask Queue and the Callback Queue?**

> The **Microtask Queue** (Promise queue) has **higher priority** than the **Callback Queue** (Macrotask queue). After every task (from Callback Queue) completes, AND after the Call Stack empties, the Event Loop **first drains the entire Microtask Queue before picking the next macrotask**. This means all pending Promise callbacks run before any `setTimeout` callback. If a microtask creates another microtask, that also runs before any macrotask — the microtask queue can potentially starve macrotasks if it never empties.
