## 2.3 The Event Loop — Complete Deep Dive

<a id="23-the-event-loop--complete-deep-dive"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic                  | What You'll Learn                                           |
| ------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- |
| What is the Event Loop?   | Definition, purpose, why it exists                          |
| Execution Order           | Call Stack → Microtasks → Macrotasks rule                   |
| Microtask Sources         | Promise.then, async/await, queueMicrotask, MutationObserver |
| Macrotask Sources         | setTimeout, setInterval, setImmediate, I/O, UI events       |
| Step-by-Step Walkthroughs | Visualized execution of complex async code                  |
| 15+ Output Questions      | Interview-focused tricky questions                          |

---

### What is the Event Loop?

The **Event Loop** is a continuous loop that monitors the **Call Stack** and the **queues**, moving callbacks from the queues to the Call Stack when the stack is empty.

> **Hinglish:** Event Loop ek security guard ki tarah hai — woh dekhta rehta hai: "kya Call Stack khali hai? Agar haan, toh pehle Microtask Queue se kaam lo, phir Callback Queue se." Yahi kadam baar baar repeat hota hai — isliye loop kehte hain.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph RUNTIME["JavaScript Runtime"]
        subgraph ENGINE["JS Engine"]
            CS["📚 Call Stack\n(Executing code)"]
            HEAP["🗄️ Memory Heap"]
        end

        subgraph QUEUES["Task Queues"]
            MQ["⚡ Microtask Queue\n(Priority 1)\nPromise.then\nasync/await\nqueueMicrotask\nMutationObserver"]
            CQ["📋 Callback Queue\n(Priority 2)\nsetTimeout callback\nsetInterval callback\nI/O callbacks\nUI events"]
        end

        subgraph WEBAPI["Web APIs"]
            WA["Browser APIs\n• Timer\n• Network\n• DOM Events"]
        end

        EL["🔄 Event Loop\nChecks: Is Call Stack empty?\n1. Yes → drain Microtask Queue\n2. Then → take 1 from Callback Queue\n3. Repeat forever"]
    end

    CS -->|"Web API calls\ne.g., setTimeout"| WA
    WA -->|"Macrotask callback ready"| CQ
    CS -->|"Promise resolved"| MQ

    EL -->|"Move microtask to stack\nwhen stack is empty"| CS
    MQ -->|"Event Loop moves"| CS
    CQ -->|"Event Loop moves\n(after microtasks drained)"| CS

    style CS fill:#FFE082
    style MQ fill:#A5D6A7
    style CQ fill:#90CAF9
    style EL fill:#F48FB1
```

---

### The Event Loop Algorithm — Step by Step

```
Event Loop Algorithm (simplified):

1. Execute all synchronous code in Call Stack until empty
2. Check Microtask Queue:
   a. If not empty → move first microtask to Call Stack → execute
   b. After execution → check Microtask Queue AGAIN (repeat until empty)
   c. A microtask can add more microtasks — all run before macrotasks
3. Check Callback Queue (Macrotask):
   a. If not empty → move FIRST macrotask to Call Stack → execute
   b. After ONE macrotask → go back to step 2 (check microtasks again!)
4. Render (browser): browser may perform DOM updates here
5. Go to step 2 and repeat forever
```

> **Hinglish:** Algorithm: pehle saari synchronous code chalao → phir poori Microtask Queue khali karo → phir ek Macrotask lo → phir phir Microtask Queue khali karo → phir ek aur Macrotask → yahi loop chalti rehti hai.

---

### Visualized Example 1 — Basic Event Loop

```javascript
console.log("1: Start"); // Synchronous

setTimeout(() => {
  console.log("4: setTimeout"); // Macrotask
}, 0);

Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log("3: Promise")); // Microtask

console.log("2: End"); // Synchronous

// Output Order: 1 → 2 → 3 → 4
```

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant CS as Call Stack
    participant MQ as Microtask Queue
    participant CQ as Callback Queue
    participant EL as Event Loop

    CS->>CS: console.log("1: Start") → executes → "1: Start" printed
    CS->>CS: setTimeout(...) → registers timer with Web API
    Note over CS: setTimeout callback sent to Web API
    CS->>CS: Promise.resolve().then(...) → promise resolves immediately
    Note over MQ: Promise callback added to Microtask Queue
    CS->>CS: console.log("2: End") → executes → "2: End" printed
    Note over CS: Call Stack is now EMPTY

    EL->>MQ: Microtask Queue has items? YES
    MQ->>CS: Move Promise callback to Call Stack
    CS->>CS: console.log("3: Promise") → executes → "3: Promise" printed
    Note over CS: Call Stack empty again

    EL->>MQ: Microtask Queue empty? YES
    EL->>CQ: Callback Queue has items? YES
    CQ->>CS: Move setTimeout callback to Call Stack
    CS->>CS: console.log("4: setTimeout") → executes → "4: setTimeout" printed
```

---

### Visualized Example 2 — async/await Deep Dive

```javascript
console.log("1: Sync Start");

async function fetchData() {
  console.log("3: Inside async function");
  const result = await Promise.resolve("data"); // Pauses here
  console.log("5: After await"); // Microtask
  return result;
}

console.log("2: Before async call");
fetchData();
console.log("4: After async call (sync continues)");

// Output: 1 → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5
```

> **Hinglish:** `await` ek pause button ki tarah hai. Jab `await` milta hai, function ruk jaata hai, control wapas caller ko chala jaata hai, aur baaki synchronous code chalti rehti hai. Jab awaited promise resolve ho jaati hai, function ka remaining part Microtask Queue mein jaata hai.

---

### Visualized Example 3 — Microtask Starvation

```javascript
// ⚠️ This can starve macrotasks!
function recursiveMicrotask() {
  Promise.resolve().then(() => {
    console.log("Microtask running...");
    recursiveMicrotask(); // Adds another microtask immediately!
  });
}

recursiveMicrotask();
setTimeout(() => console.log("setTimeout — will this ever run?"), 0);

// "setTimeout" will NEVER run — Microtask Queue never empties!
// Each microtask adds another microtask — infinite loop
```

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **Microtask Starvation:** If the Microtask Queue never empties (because each microtask adds another microtask), the Event Loop can never pick up macrotasks — `setTimeout` callbacks, UI updates, and I/O never run. This is a real performance bug pattern.

---

### Complete Event Loop Execution Order Reference

```
Priority Order (highest to lowest):
1. Synchronous code (Call Stack)
2. Microtasks (completely drained):
   - Promise.then / .catch / .finally
   - async/await continuations
   - queueMicrotask()
   - MutationObserver callbacks
3. ONE Macrotask at a time:
   - setTimeout(fn, 0) or setTimeout(fn, n)
   - setInterval callbacks
   - setImmediate() [Node.js only — after I/O]
   - I/O callbacks
   - UI event handlers (click, scroll)
4. Back to step 2 (check microtasks after each macrotask)
5. Render (browser checks if repaint needed)
```

---

### 🎯 15+ Event Loop Interview Questions

**Q1: What is the output of this code?**

```javascript
console.log("A");

setTimeout(() => console.log("B"), 0);
setTimeout(() => console.log("C"), 0);

Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log("D"));
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log("E"));

console.log("F");
```

> **Output:** A → F → D → E → B → C
>
> **Explanation:** A and F are synchronous (run first). D and E are microtasks (run before macrotasks, in order). B and C are macrotasks (run after all microtasks, in order of registration).

---

**Q2: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log("1");

setTimeout(() => {
  console.log("2");
  Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log("3"));
}, 0);

Promise.resolve().then(() => {
  console.log("4");
  setTimeout(() => console.log("5"), 0);
});

console.log("6");
```

> **Output:** 1 → 6 → 4 → 2 → 3 → 5
>
> **Explanation:**
>
> - Sync: "1", "6"
> - Microtask queue: Promise → "4" (also registers setTimeout for "5" as macrotask)
> - Macrotask: setTimeout "2" runs → then its inner Promise microtask "3" runs
> - Macrotask: setTimeout "5" runs last

---

**Q3: What is the output?**

```javascript
async function a() {
  console.log("A1");
  await b();
  console.log("A2");
}

async function b() {
  console.log("B1");
}

console.log("Start");
a();
console.log("End");
```

> **Output:** Start → A1 → B1 → End → A2
>
> **Explanation:** Sync: Start, then a() starts: A1, then b() is called: B1, b() returns a resolved Promise, `await` suspends a() and returns control → End prints. Then microtask: A2 prints.

---

**Q4: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log("1");

queueMicrotask(() => console.log("2"));

setTimeout(() => console.log("3"), 0);

Promise.resolve()
  .then(() => console.log("4"))
  .then(() => console.log("5"));

console.log("6");
```

> **Output:** 1 → 6 → 2 → 4 → 5 → 3
>
> **Explanation:** Sync: 1, 6. Microtasks (in registration order): queueMicrotask("2") → Promise.then("4") → chained .then("5"). Macrotask: setTimeout("3").

---

**Q5: What is the output?**

```javascript
Promise.resolve()
  .then(() => {
    console.log("1");
    return Promise.resolve("2");
  })
  .then((val) => console.log(val));

Promise.resolve()
  .then(() => console.log("3"))
  .then(() => console.log("4"));
```

> **Output:** 1 → 3 → 4 → 2
>
> **Explanation:** This is tricky! When a `.then` callback returns a Promise (not a plain value), the next `.then` must wait for that inner Promise to resolve — this adds **extra microtask ticks**. "1" and "3" are first microtasks. "4" is next (chained to "3"). Then "2" resolves (was waiting for the inner `Promise.resolve("2")` which takes extra microtask ticks).

---

**Q6: What is the output?**

```javascript
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i), 0);
}
```

> **Output:** 3 → 3 → 3
>
> **Explanation:** `var` is function-scoped (not block-scoped). The loop completes (i becomes 3) before any setTimeout callback runs. All three callbacks close over the SAME `i` variable, which is now 3. Fix: use `let` (block-scoped — each iteration gets its own `i`) or IIFE.

---

**Q7: Fix the above — make it print 0, 1, 2**

```javascript
// Fix 1: Use let (block scope creates new binding per iteration)
for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i), 0); // 0, 1, 2
}

// Fix 2: IIFE to capture current value
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  ((j) => setTimeout(() => console.log(j), 0))(i);
}

// Fix 3: bind
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  setTimeout(console.log.bind(null, i), 0);
}
```

---

**Q8: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log("script start");

async function async1() {
  await async2();
  console.log("async1 end");
}

async function async2() {
  console.log("async2 start");
  return Promise.resolve("async2 end");
}

async1();

setTimeout(() => console.log("setTimeout"), 0);
console.log("script end");
```

> **Output:** script start → async2 start → script end → async1 end → setTimeout
>
> **Explanation:** Sync: "script start", async1() starts → reaches await → async2() is called: "async2 start" → async1 suspends → "script end". Microtasks: async1 resumes: "async1 end". Macrotask: "setTimeout".

---

**Q9: What is the output?**

```javascript
const p = new Promise((resolve) => {
  console.log("1: Promise executor");
  resolve("resolved");
  console.log("2: After resolve");
});

p.then((val) => console.log("3:", val));

console.log("4: Synchronous end");
```

> **Output:** 1 → 2 → 4 → 3
>
> **Explanation:** The **Promise executor function runs synchronously** immediately. "1" and "2" both print before the `.then` callback (which is async/microtask). After sync code ends ("4"), the microtask "3: resolved" runs.

---

**Q10: What is the output?**

```javascript
setTimeout(() => console.log("timeout 1"), 0);

Promise.resolve()
  .then(() => setTimeout(() => console.log("timeout 2"), 0))
  .then(() => console.log("then 1"));

setTimeout(() => console.log("timeout 3"), 0);

console.log("sync");
```

> **Output:** sync → then 1 → timeout 1 → timeout 3 → timeout 2
>
> **Explanation:** Sync: "sync". Microtasks: first .then registers "timeout 2" as macrotask → second .then prints "then 1". Macrotasks in queue order: "timeout 1" (registered first), "timeout 3" (registered second), "timeout 2" (registered by microtask — after timeout 1 and 3).

---

**Q11: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log("start");

setTimeout(function () {
  console.log("timeout");
}, 0);

Promise.resolve(1)
  .then(function (x) {
    return x + 1;
  })
  .then(function (x) {
    throw new Error("err");
  })
  .catch(function (x) {
    console.log("caught", x.message);
  })
  .then(function () {
    console.log("after catch");
  });

console.log("end");
```

> **Output:** start → end → caught err → after catch → timeout
>
> **Explanation:** Sync: start, end. Promise chain (all microtasks): x+1=2 → throws error → catch logs "caught err" → after catch logs → Then macrotask: timeout.

---

**Q12 (Tricky): What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log("1");

setTimeout(() => console.log("2"), 1000);
setTimeout(() => console.log("3"), 0);

new Promise((res) => {
  console.log("4");
  res();
})
  .then(() => {
    console.log("5");
    setTimeout(() => console.log("6"), 0);
  })
  .then(() => console.log("7"));

console.log("8");
```

> **Output:** 1 → 4 → 8 → 5 → 7 → 3 → 6 → 2
>
> **Explanation:** Sync: 1, 4 (executor sync), 8. Microtasks: "5" (then 1) → registers timeout 6 → "7" (then 2). Macrotasks in queue order: "3" (registered early) → "6" (registered by microtask, comes after 3) → "2" (1000ms delay, definitely last).

---

**Q13: Will `finally` always run?**

```javascript
async function test() {
  try {
    return await Promise.reject("error");
  } finally {
    console.log("finally ran");
  }
}

test().catch((err) => console.log("caught:", err));
```

> **Output:** finally ran → caught: error
>
> **Explanation:** `finally` always runs, even when the Promise is rejected. It runs before the `catch` handler. Note: if `finally` throws or returns a value, it overrides the original rejection/resolution.

---

**Q14: What is the output?**

```javascript
async function getData() {
  const x = await 1; // await non-Promise: wraps in Promise.resolve(1)
  const y = await Promise.resolve(2);
  return x + y;
}

getData().then((val) => console.log(val));
console.log("sync");
```

> **Output:** sync → 3
>
> **Explanation:** `await 1` is equivalent to `await Promise.resolve(1)` — it still creates a microtask pause. "sync" prints before getData resolves. Then microtasks run and val=3 is logged.

---

**Q15: Node.js specific — setImmediate vs setTimeout**

```javascript
// In Node.js:
setTimeout(() => console.log("setTimeout"), 0);
setImmediate(() => console.log("setImmediate"));

// Output: NOT guaranteed! Could be either order.
// BUT inside an I/O callback, setImmediate ALWAYS runs before setTimeout:

const fs = require("fs");
fs.readFile(__filename, () => {
  setTimeout(() => console.log("setTimeout"), 0);
  setImmediate(() => console.log("setImmediate"));
  // setImmediate ALWAYS first inside I/O callback
});
```

> **Explanation:** At the top level, the order of `setTimeout(0)` vs `setImmediate` is non-deterministic (depends on system timer resolution). Inside an I/O callback, `setImmediate` always runs first because the Event Loop is already in the "check" phase after I/O, which is where `setImmediate` callbacks are processed.

## 2.4 Execution Context & Hoisting

<a id="24-execution-context--hoisting"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic                      | What You'll Learn                           |
| ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- |
| Global Execution Context      | What runs before any function is called     |
| Function Execution Context    | Created per function call                   |
| Creation Phase                | Variable hoisting, function hoisting        |
| Execution Phase               | Code runs line by line                      |
| Variable Environment          | Where variables are stored                  |
| Lexical Environment           | Outer environment reference (closure)       |
| var hoisting                  | Hoisted + initialized as `undefined`        |
| let/const hoisting            | Hoisted but NOT initialized (TDZ)           |
| Function declaration hoisting | Fully hoisted (name + body)                 |
| Function expression hoisting  | Only variable hoisted (not the function)    |
| Class hoisting                | Hoisted but in TDZ (like let/const)         |
| Temporal Dead Zone (TDZ)      | What it is, why it exists, errors it causes |

---

### What is an Execution Context?

An **Execution Context** is the environment in which JavaScript code is evaluated and executed. Every time code runs, it runs inside an execution context.

> **Hinglish:** Execution Context ek room ki tarah hai jahan JavaScript code chalti hai. Har room mein uske apne variables, functions aur bahar ka reference hota hai. Jab bhi naya function call hota hai, nayi room banti hai.

**Three Types of Execution Context:**

| Type                                 | When Created        | Examples                        |
| ------------------------------------ | ------------------- | ------------------------------- |
| **Global Execution Context (GEC)**   | Program starts      | Code outside any function       |
| **Function Execution Context (FEC)** | Every function call | `function foo() {}` when called |
| **Eval Execution Context**           | `eval()` is called  | Rare — avoid `eval()`           |

---

### Execution Context — Internal Structure

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph EC["Execution Context"]
        subgraph VE["Variable Environment"]
            VE1["Variable declarations (var)"]
            VE2["Function declarations"]
            VE3["arguments object (FEC only)"]
        end

        subgraph LE["Lexical Environment"]
            LE1["Block scope (let, const)"]
            LE2["Outer Environment Reference\n(points to parent scope)"]
        end

        TC["ThisBinding\n• GEC: window (browser) / global (Node)\n• FEC: depends on call site\n• Arrow fn: inherits from parent"]
    end

    style EC fill:#fff9c4
    style VE fill:#e3f2fd
    style LE fill:#e8f5e9
```

---

### Phase 1: Creation Phase (Memory Phase)

Before any code runs, JavaScript creates the execution context in two phases. The **Creation Phase** happens first — JS scans the code and allocates memory.

> **Hinglish:** Creation Phase ek blueprint banane ki tarah hai — code run karne se pehle, JS pehle dekhta hai "kya-kya variables aur functions hain" aur unke liye memory reserve karta hai. Yahi hoisting hai.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A["JavaScript file loaded"] --> B["CREATION PHASE\n(Before any code runs)"]

    subgraph CREATION["Creation Phase — Memory Allocation"]
        B --> C["Scan for var declarations\n→ Allocate memory\n→ Initialize as undefined"]
        B --> D["Scan for function declarations\n→ Allocate memory\n→ Store complete function body"]
        B --> E["Scan for let/const declarations\n→ Allocate memory\n→ Mark as UNINITIALIZED (TDZ)"]
        B --> F["Scan for class declarations\n→ Allocate memory\n→ Mark as UNINITIALIZED (TDZ)"]
        B --> G["Set up ThisBinding\n→ Outer environment reference"]
    end

    H["EXECUTION PHASE\n(Code runs line by line)"] --> I["Assign actual values\nto variables"]
    H --> J["Execute function calls"]
    H --> K["Evaluate expressions"]

    CREATION --> H

    style CREATION fill:#fff3e0
    style H fill:#e8f5e9
```

---

### Hoisting — Complete Reference

**Hoisting** is JavaScript's behavior of moving declarations to the top of their scope during the Creation Phase (conceptually — physically the code doesn't move; memory is allocated before execution).

> **Hinglish:** Hoisting ka matlab hai JavaScript code run karne se pehle variables aur functions ko "upar le jaata hai" (conceptually). Aslaan code move nahi hota — sirf memory pehle allocate ho jaati hai.

#### var — Hoisted + Initialized as `undefined`

```javascript
// What you write:
console.log(name); // ❓ What happens?
var name = "Rahul";
console.log(name);

// What JavaScript "sees" (conceptually after hoisting):
var name = undefined; // ← hoisted + initialized as undefined
console.log(name); // undefined (no error!)
name = "Rahul"; // assignment happens at original line
console.log(name); // "Rahul"

// Output: undefined → "Rahul"
```

#### let & const — Hoisted but NOT Initialized (Temporal Dead Zone)

```javascript
// What you write:
console.log(age); // ❓
let age = 25;
console.log(age);

// What JavaScript "sees":
// let age → hoisted to top but in TEMPORAL DEAD ZONE (TDZ)
// Accessing before initialization → ReferenceError!

console.log(age); // ReferenceError: Cannot access 'age' before initialization
let age = 25; // Initialization: exits TDZ
console.log(age); // 25
```

#### Function Declaration — Fully Hoisted

```javascript
// You can call a function BEFORE its declaration!
sayHello(); // ✅ Works! Output: "Hello!"

function sayHello() {
  console.log("Hello!");
}

// Entire function (name + body) is hoisted to top of scope
// JavaScript sees:
function sayHello() {
  console.log("Hello!");
} // Hoisted completely
sayHello(); // Called after hoisting
```

#### Function Expression — Only Variable Hoisted

```javascript
// ❌ Cannot call before declaration
greet(); // TypeError: greet is not a function (var) or
// ReferenceError: Cannot access 'greet' before init (let/const)

var greet = function () {
  // var is hoisted as undefined
  console.log("Hi!"); // function body is NOT hoisted
};

// What JS sees:
var greet = undefined; // ← hoisted (var only, not the function!)
greet(); // TypeError: greet is not a function (it's undefined!)
greet = function () {
  console.log("Hi!");
}; // assigned later
```

#### Class Declaration — Hoisted but in TDZ

```javascript
// ❌ Cannot use class before declaration
const obj = new MyClass(); // ReferenceError: Cannot access 'MyClass' before init

class MyClass {
  constructor() {
    this.value = 42;
  }
}

// Classes behave like let/const — hoisted to TDZ, not usable before declaration
```

---

### Complete Hoisting Comparison Table

| Declaration Type       | Hoisted?      | Initialized?      | Value Before Declaration   | Error Type       |
| ---------------------- | ------------- | ----------------- | -------------------------- | ---------------- |
| `var x`                | ✅ Yes        | ✅ As `undefined` | `undefined`                | No error         |
| `let x`                | ✅ Yes        | ❌ TDZ            | TDZ                        | `ReferenceError` |
| `const x`              | ✅ Yes        | ❌ TDZ            | TDZ                        | `ReferenceError` |
| `function f(){}`       | ✅ Yes        | ✅ Full function  | Callable                   | No error         |
| `var f = function(){}` | ✅ (var only) | ✅ As `undefined` | `undefined` (not callable) | `TypeError`      |
| `let f = function(){}` | ✅ (let only) | ❌ TDZ            | TDZ                        | `ReferenceError` |
| `class MyClass {}`     | ✅ Yes        | ❌ TDZ            | TDZ                        | `ReferenceError` |

---

### Temporal Dead Zone (TDZ) — Deep Dive

The **Temporal Dead Zone** is the period between when a `let`/`const`/`class` variable is hoisted (memory allocated) and when it is initialized with a value (reaches its declaration in code execution).

> **Hinglish:** TDZ ek "forbidden zone" hai — variable ka naam already JS ko pata hai (hoisted), lekin use access karna allowed nahi hai ab tak jab tak line execute na ho. Jaise ek seat book hai lekin passenger abhi baitha nahi.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph TIMELINE["Code Execution Timeline"]
        A["Scope starts\n(function/block begins)"] --> B
        B["TDZ begins\nlet age hoisted\nbut NOT initialized\nAccessing → ReferenceError"] --> C
        C["let age = 25\nDeclaration reached\nTDZ ENDS\nage = 25"] --> D
        D["age is accessible\nage → 25"]
    end

    style B fill:#ffcdd2
    style C fill:#c8e6c9
    style D fill:#e8f5e9
```

```javascript
{
  // TDZ for 'value' starts here
  console.log(typeof value); // ReferenceError! (even typeof doesn't save you with let/const)
  // ↑ With var, typeof returns "undefined" — safe
  // ↑ With let/const, typeof throws ReferenceError in TDZ

  let value = 42; // TDZ ends here
  console.log(value); // 42
}

// typeof with var (safe):
console.log(typeof undeclaredVar); // "undefined" — no error (var behavior)

// typeof with let in TDZ (throws!):
console.log(typeof myLet); // ReferenceError
let myLet = 5;
```

---

### Execution Context — Complete Visual Example

```javascript
var globalVar = "I am global";
let globalLet = "Global let";

function outer() {
  var outerVar = "I am outer";

  function inner() {
    var innerVar = "I am inner";
    console.log(globalVar); // "I am global" — scope chain
    console.log(outerVar); // "I am outer" — scope chain
    console.log(innerVar); // "I am inner" — local
  }

  inner();
}

outer();
```

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph GEC["Global Execution Context"]
        GV["globalVar = 'I am global'\nglobalLet = 'Global let'\nouter = [function]"]
        GOUTER["outer environment ref: null"]
    end

    subgraph OEC["outer() Execution Context"]
        OV["outerVar = 'I am outer'\ninner = [function]"]
        OINNER["outer environment ref: → GEC"]
    end

    subgraph IEC["inner() Execution Context"]
        IV["innerVar = 'I am inner'"]
        IINNER["outer environment ref: → OEC"]
    end

    IEC -->|"Scope Chain:\nLook for globalVar"| OEC
    OEC -->|"Not found here\nKeep looking up"| GEC
    GEC -->|"Found! globalVar = 'I am global'"| IEC

    style GEC fill:#fff9c4
    style OEC fill:#e3f2fd
    style IEC fill:#e8f5e9
```

---

### 🎯 15+ Hoisting Interview Output Questions

**Q1: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log(x);
var x = 5;
console.log(x);
```

> **Output:** undefined → 5
> **Reason:** `var x` is hoisted and initialized as `undefined`. Assignment happens at runtime.

---

**Q2: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log(x);
let x = 5;
```

> **Output:** ReferenceError: Cannot access 'x' before initialization
> **Reason:** `let` is hoisted to TDZ — accessing before declaration throws ReferenceError.

---

**Q3: What is the output?**

```javascript
foo();
bar();

function foo() {
  console.log("foo");
}

var bar = function () {
  console.log("bar");
};
```

> **Output:** "foo" → TypeError: bar is not a function
> **Reason:** `foo` is a function declaration — fully hoisted. `bar` is a var (hoisted as `undefined`) — calling `undefined()` throws TypeError.

---

**Q4: What is the output?**

```javascript
var x = 1;
function test() {
  console.log(x);
  var x = 2;
  console.log(x);
}
test();
console.log(x);
```

> **Output:** undefined → 2 → 1
> **Reason:** Inside `test()`, `var x` is hoisted — local `x` shadows global `x`. At first log, local `x` is `undefined` (hoisted but not yet assigned). After assignment, `x = 2`. Global `x` remains 1.

---

**Q5: What is the output?**

```javascript
let x = 1;

function outer() {
  let x = 2;
  function inner() {
    console.log(x); // Which x?
  }
  inner();
}

outer();
```

> **Output:** 2
> **Reason:** Lexical scope — `inner` has no own `x`, looks up scope chain, finds `x = 2` in `outer`. Never reaches global `x = 1`.

---

**Q6: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log(typeof foo);
console.log(typeof bar);
console.log(typeof baz);

function foo() {}
var bar = 1;
let baz = 2;
```

> **Output:** "function" → "undefined" → ReferenceError
> **Reason:** `foo` (function declaration) is fully hoisted: `typeof` returns "function". `bar` (var) is hoisted as `undefined`: `typeof` returns "undefined". `baz` (let) is in TDZ: `typeof` throws ReferenceError (note: `typeof` does NOT save you from TDZ with let/const).

---

**Q7: What is the output?**

```javascript
function hoistTest() {
  console.log(a); // ?
  console.log(b); // ?
  var a = 10;
  function b() {
    return 20;
  }
}
hoistTest();
```

> **Output:** undefined → [function: b]
> **Reason:** `a` (var) hoisted as `undefined`. `b` (function declaration) fully hoisted — its value is the function itself.

---

**Q8: What is the output?**

```javascript
var a = 1;

function outer() {
  var a = 2;
  return function inner() {
    var a = 3;
    console.log(a);
  };
}

var innerFn = outer();
innerFn();
console.log(a);
```

> **Output:** 3 → 1
> **Reason:** `inner` accesses its own `a = 3`. Global `a` is 1 (unchanged).

---

**Q9: What is the output?**

```javascript
{
  var x = 1;
  let y = 2;
}
console.log(x);
console.log(y);
```

> **Output:** 1 → ReferenceError: y is not defined
> **Reason:** `var` ignores block scope — `x` is accessible globally. `let` is block-scoped — `y` is not accessible outside the block.

---

**Q10: What is the output?**

```javascript
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i), 0);
}

for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log(j), 0);
}
```

> **Output:** 3, 3, 3 (from var loop) → 0, 1, 2 (from let loop)
> **Reason:** `var i` is function-scoped, shared across iterations. `let j` creates a new binding per iteration (block-scoped).

---

**Q11: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log(a());
console.log(b());
console.log(c());

function a() {
  return "A";
}
var b = function () {
  return "B";
};
const c = () => "C";
```

> **Output:** "A" → TypeError: b is not a function → ReferenceError: Cannot access 'c' before initialization
> **Reason:** `a` is fully hoisted. `b` (var) is hoisted as `undefined` — calling `undefined()` → TypeError. `c` (const) is in TDZ → ReferenceError.

---

**Q12: What is the output?**

```javascript
var x = "global";
function test() {
  console.log(x); // ?
  var x = "local";
  console.log(x); // ?
}
test();
```

> **Output:** undefined → "local"
> **Reason:** Inside `test`, `var x` is hoisted. At first log, `x` is `undefined` (local var shadows global but not yet assigned). After assignment, `x = "local"`.

---

**Q13: What is the output?**

```javascript
let x = 10;

if (true) {
  console.log(x); // ?
  let x = 20;
}
```

> **Output:** ReferenceError: Cannot access 'x' before initialization
> **Reason:** `let x = 20` inside the `if` block is hoisted to the top of that block and enters TDZ. Accessing `x` before the `let` declaration (even though outer `x = 10` exists) causes ReferenceError. The inner `x` shadows the outer one and is in TDZ.

---

**Q14: What is the output?**

```javascript
function test() {
  return a;
  var a = 1;
}
console.log(test());
```

> **Output:** undefined
> **Reason:** `var a` is hoisted and initialized as `undefined` during the creation phase. The function returns before `a = 1` is assigned, so returns `undefined`.

---

**Q15: What is the output?**

```javascript
const obj = {
  value: 42,
  getValue: function () {
    return this.value;
  },
  getValueArrow: () => {
    return this.value;
  },
};

console.log(obj.getValue()); // ?
console.log(obj.getValueArrow()); // ?
```

> **Output:** 42 → undefined (in strict mode) or window.value (browser non-strict)
> **Reason:** `getValue` is a regular function — `this` refers to `obj` at call time. `getValueArrow` is an arrow function — `this` is lexically inherited from the surrounding context (where `obj` is defined — the global scope), not `obj`. Global `this` has no `value` property.

---

**Q16: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log(foo);

var foo = "bar";

console.log(foo);

function foo() {
  return "I'm a function";
}

console.log(foo);
```

> **Output:** [function: foo] → "bar" → "bar"
> **Reason:** Function declarations take priority over var declarations during hoisting — `foo` is initialized as the function. Then `foo = "bar"` assigns the string. Both subsequent logs show "bar".

## 2.5 Scope & Scope Chain

<a id="25-scope--scope-chain"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic             | What You'll Learn                             |
| -------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| Global Scope         | Variables accessible everywhere               |
| Function Scope       | Variables only in that function               |
| Block Scope          | Variables in `{}` blocks (let/const only)     |
| Lexical/Static Scope | Scope determined at write time, not call time |
| Scope Chain          | How JS looks up variables upward              |
| Variable Shadowing   | Inner variable hiding outer variable          |
| Illegal Shadowing    | `let` cannot shadow `var` in same scope       |

---

### What is Scope?

**Scope** determines the **visibility and accessibility** of variables — where a variable can be read and written.

> **Hinglish:** Scope matlab "kahan access hai" — ek variable sirf wahan available hoti hai jahan uska scope hai. Jaise office ka ID card sirf office ke andar kaam karta hai, bahar nahi.

---

### Types of Scope

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    GS["🌍 Global Scope\nAccessible everywhere\nvar x = 1 (outside functions)"]

    subgraph FS["🏠 Function Scope"]
        FV["Variables declared\ninside a function\n\nfunction foo() {\n  var y = 2  // only in foo\n}"]
    end

    subgraph BS["📦 Block Scope (ES6+)"]
        BV["Variables declared inside\n{} blocks using let/const\n\nif(true) {\n  let z = 3  // only in this block\n}"]
    end

    GS -->|"Accessible inside"| FS
    GS -->|"Accessible inside"| BS
    FS -->|"var ignores block scope!"| BS

    style GS fill:#fff9c4
    style FS fill:#e3f2fd
    style BS fill:#e8f5e9
```

---

### Global Scope

Variables declared outside any function or block are in the **Global Scope** — accessible from anywhere in the code.

```javascript
var globalVar = "I'm global"; // Global scope
let globalLet = "I'm global"; // Global scope
const globalConst = "I'm global"; // Global scope

function test() {
  console.log(globalVar); // ✅ Accessible
  console.log(globalLet); // ✅ Accessible
  console.log(globalConst); // ✅ Accessible
}

// In browser: var creates property on window
console.log(window.globalVar); // "I'm global" ✅ (var only!)
console.log(window.globalLet); // undefined ❌ (let doesn't attach to window)
```

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **Global Scope Pollution:** Accidentally creating global variables is a common bug. Always declare variables with `const`/`let`. In strict mode (`'use strict'`), assigning to an undeclared variable throws a `ReferenceError`.

---

### Function Scope

Variables declared with `var` inside a function are **function-scoped** — accessible anywhere within that function, but not outside.

```javascript
function calculateTax() {
  var taxRate = 0.18; // Function scoped
  let total = 1000; // Also function scoped
  const GST = "18%"; // Also function scoped

  return total * taxRate;
}

calculateTax();
console.log(taxRate); // ❌ ReferenceError — taxRate not accessible outside
console.log(total); // ❌ ReferenceError
console.log(GST); // ❌ ReferenceError
```

---

### Block Scope

`let` and `const` are **block-scoped** — they are limited to the `{}` block in which they are declared. `var` ignores block scope.

> **Hinglish:** Block scope matlab `{}` ke andar hi accessible hai. `let`/`const` block-scoped hain. `var` block ko ignore karta hai — sirf function ya global scope maanta hai.

```javascript
{
  var a = 1; // var: ignores block, becomes function/global scoped
  let b = 2; // let: block-scoped
  const c = 3; // const: block-scoped
}

console.log(a); // 1   ✅ (var ignores block scope)
console.log(b); // ReferenceError ❌ (let is block-scoped)
console.log(c); // ReferenceError ❌ (const is block-scoped)

// Real impact in if/for:
if (true) {
  var x = "I leak out!";
  let y = "I stay in";
}
console.log(x); // "I leak out!" (var leaks from if block)
console.log(y); // ReferenceError (let stays in block)
```

---

### Lexical Scope (Static Scope)

**Lexical scope** means that the scope of a variable is determined by its **position in the source code** (where it's written), not where the function is called from.

> **Hinglish:** Lexical scope ka matlab hai scope code likhte waqt define hoti hai, function call karte waqt nahi. Jahan function LIKHA gaya hai woh matter karta hai, jahan se CALL kiya woh nahi.

```javascript
let x = "global";

function outer() {
  let x = "outer";

  function inner() {
    // inner is DEFINED inside outer → lexically scoped to outer
    console.log(x); // "outer" — reads from where it's DEFINED
  }

  return inner;
}

let innerFn = outer();

// Even though called in global scope, 'x' refers to outer's x
innerFn(); // "outer" — NOT "global"!

// This is lexical scope — scope determined at DEFINITION time
```

---

### Scope Chain — How Variable Lookup Works

When JavaScript looks for a variable, it searches the **scope chain** — starting from the current scope and moving outward until it finds the variable or reaches the global scope.

> **Hinglish:** Scope chain ek ladder ki tarah hai. JS pehle apne ghar (current scope) mein dhundhta hai. Nahi mila? Parent ke ghar jaata hai. Phir dada ke ghar. Agar global tak nahi mila? ReferenceError.

```mermaid
flowchart BT
    subgraph INNER["Inner Function Scope"]
        I1["Looking for: x"]
        I2["Own vars: z = 3"]
        I3["x not found here..."]
    end

    subgraph MIDDLE["Middle Function Scope"]
        M1["Own vars: y = 2, x = 'middle'"]
        M2["x FOUND HERE! = 'middle'"]
    end

    subgraph GLOBAL["Global Scope"]
        G1["Own vars: x = 'global', a = 1"]
    end

    INNER -->|"Not found → look up"| MIDDLE
    MIDDLE -->|"If not found → look up"| GLOBAL
    GLOBAL -->|"If not found → ReferenceError"| ERROR["❌ ReferenceError"]

    style INNER fill:#e8f5e9
    style MIDDLE fill:#e3f2fd
    style GLOBAL fill:#fff9c4
```

```javascript
const x = "global";

function level1() {
  const y = "level1";

  function level2() {
    const z = "level2";

    function level3() {
      // Looking for x: not in level3 → look up → not in level2
      // → look up → not in level1 → look up → found in global!
      console.log(x); // "global" (scope chain lookup)
      console.log(y); // "level1" (found in level1)
      console.log(z); // "level2" (found in level2)
      // console.log(w) // ReferenceError: w not found anywhere in chain
    }
    level3();
  }
  level2();
}
level1();
```

---

### Variable Shadowing

When an inner scope declares a variable with the **same name** as an outer scope variable, the inner variable **shadows** (hides) the outer one within its scope.

> **Hinglish:** Shadowing tab hota hai jab andar ki variable bahar ki variable ka naam chura le — andar wali "shadow" ban jaati hai. Bahar ki visible nahi hoti andar se.

```javascript
let name = "Rahul"; // Outer scope

function greet() {
  let name = "Priya"; // Shadows outer 'name' inside greet
  console.log(name); // "Priya" — inner shadows outer
}

greet();
console.log(name); // "Rahul" — outer unchanged

// Block scope shadowing:
let count = 0;
{
  let count = 100; // Shadows outer count in this block
  console.log(count); // 100
}
console.log(count); // 0 — outer unchanged
```

---

### Illegal Shadowing

You **cannot** shadow a `let` variable (declared in an outer scope) with a `var` in an inner scope that belongs to the same function scope. But the reverse (shadowing `var` with `let`) is allowed.

> **Hinglish:** Illegal shadowing = `var` se `let` ko shadow nahi kar sakte same scope mein. Yeh isliye hai kyunki `var` function scope mein jaati hai aur conflict create karti hai.

```javascript
// ❌ Illegal: var cannot shadow let in same function scope
let x = 10;
function test() {
  var x = 20; // SyntaxError! var 'x' would conflict with let 'x' in same scope
}

// ✅ Legal: var can shadow let in a block (different function scope)
let y = 10;
{
  var y = 20; // ❌ Still illegal! var is function-scoped, sees the same y
}

// ✅ Legal: let CAN shadow var
var a = 1;
function test2() {
  let a = 2; // ✅ let can shadow var
  console.log(a); // 2
}

// ✅ Legal: let can shadow let in inner block
let b = 1;
{
  let b = 2; // ✅ let can shadow let in inner block
  console.log(b); // 2
}
console.log(b); // 1
```

---

### Scope in Real-World React/Next.js

```javascript
// React Component — Scope in practice:
import { useState } from "react";

const API_KEY = process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_API_KEY; // Module scope (global within module)

function ProductList() {
  // Function scope begins
  const [products, setProducts] = useState([]); // Function-scoped state
  const BASE_URL = "/api"; // Function-scoped constant

  async function fetchProducts(category) {
    // Nested function scope
    // Scope chain: category → products/setProducts/BASE_URL → API_KEY
    const url = `${BASE_URL}/products?cat=${category}`; // Accesses parent scope
    const res = await fetch(url);
    const data = await res.json();
    setProducts(data); // Closure over setProducts
  }

  return (
    <div>
      {products.map((product) => {
        // Block scope per iteration (let in map)
        const price = product.price * 1.18; // Block scoped to map callback
        return <div key={product.id}>{price}</div>;
      })}
    </div>
  );
}
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 2.5

**Q1: What is the difference between function scope and block scope?**

> **Function scope** means a variable is accessible anywhere within the function it was declared in — regardless of `{}` blocks within the function. `var` is function-scoped. **Block scope** means a variable is only accessible within the specific `{}` block it was declared in. `let` and `const` are block-scoped. Example: a `var` declared inside an `if` statement is accessible outside the `if` but inside the function. A `let` declared in the same `if` is only accessible within that `if` block.

**Q2: What is lexical scope? How does it differ from dynamic scope?**

> **Lexical scope** (also called static scope) means that the scope of a variable is determined by its **physical location in the source code** (where it's written) — not where the function is called from. JavaScript uses lexical scoping. **Dynamic scope** (used in some other languages like Bash, older Lisps) means scope is determined at **runtime** based on the call stack — a function can access variables from the function that called it. In lexical scope, a function can access variables from the function that **defined** it. This distinction is critical for closures.

**Q3: What is the scope chain and how does variable lookup work?**

> The scope chain is the ordered list of scopes that JavaScript searches when looking up a variable. When a variable is accessed, JavaScript: (1) Looks in the **current scope** first. (2) If not found, moves to the **parent scope** (outer function). (3) Continues upward until the **global scope**. (4) If still not found, throws `ReferenceError`. The chain is established at function creation time (lexical scope) — each function stores a reference to its outer scope, forming a linked chain of environments.

**Q4: What is variable shadowing? Give an example where it causes a bug.**

> Variable shadowing occurs when an inner scope declares a variable with the same name as an outer scope, hiding the outer variable within the inner scope. Bug example:
>
> ```javascript
> let total = 0;
> function addItems(items) {
>   items.forEach(function (item) {
>     let total = item.price; // BUG! Accidentally shadows outer total
>     // Intended to add to outer total, but created a new local total
>   });
>   return total; // Returns 0 — outer total was never modified!
> }
> ```
>
> Fix: use a different variable name, or access outer `total` without re-declaring it.

**Q5: Why does `let` prevent accidental global variable creation?**

> `var` has function scope — if you forget to declare a variable with `var` inside a function, it automatically becomes a global variable (in non-strict mode). `let` and `const` have block scope and are strictly scoped — accessing an undeclared `let` or trying to declare it without the `let` keyword throws a `ReferenceError`. Additionally, `let`/`const` don't attach to the `window` object even when declared globally, preventing namespace pollution. This makes `let`/`const` safer and helps catch bugs at development time rather than runtime.

## 🧪 Mini Project: JavaScript Runtime Visualizer

<a id="-mini-project-javascript-runtime-visualizer"></a>

### Problem Statement

Build a **"JS Runtime Visualizer"** — a Next.js interactive tool that visually demonstrates the JavaScript Event Loop, Call Stack, Memory Heap, and execution order to help developers understand async JavaScript behavior.

### Architecture

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph APP["Next.js App"]
        UI["React UI\n(Client Component)"] --> |User inputs JS code snippet| ENGINE
        ENGINE["Code Analyzer\nRoute Handler\n(Server Component)"] --> |Execution trace| UI

        subgraph VISUALIZER["Visualizer Components"]
            CS_VIZ["CallStackVisualizer\nAnimates push/pop"]
            EL_VIZ["EventLoopVisualizer\nAnimates loop steps"]
            MQ_VIZ["MicrotaskQueueViz\nShows promise queue"]
            CQ_VIZ["CallbackQueueViz\nShows timer queue"]
            OUTPUT["OutputConsole\nShows execution output"]
        end

        UI --> VISUALIZER
    end

    style APP fill:#f5f5f5
    style VISUALIZER fill:#e3f2fd
```

### File Structure

```
js-runtime-visualizer/
├── app/
│   ├── layout.tsx
│   ├── page.tsx                      # Main visualizer page
│   └── api/
│       └── analyze/route.ts          # Code analysis endpoint
├── components/
│   ├── visualizer/
│   │   ├── CallStackPanel.tsx        # Animated call stack
│   │   ├── MicrotaskQueuePanel.tsx   # Microtask queue visualization
│   │   ├── CallbackQueuePanel.tsx    # Macrotask queue visualization
│   │   ├── EventLoopIndicator.tsx    # Rotating event loop animation
│   │   └── ConsoleOutput.tsx         # Execution output panel
│   ├── CodeEditor.tsx                # Monaco editor or textarea
│   └── SnippetSelector.tsx           # Pre-built code examples
├── lib/
│   ├── executionTracer.ts            # Traces execution steps
│   └── snippets.ts                   # Pre-built demo snippets
└── hooks/
    └── useEventLoopSimulation.ts     # Simulation logic
```

### Key Code Examples

```typescript
// lib/snippets.ts — Pre-built demonstration snippets

export const snippets = {
  basicEventLoop: {
    name: "Basic Event Loop",
    code: `
console.log("1: Sync Start")
setTimeout(() => console.log("4: Timeout"), 0)
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log("3: Promise"))
console.log("2: Sync End")
    `,
    expectedOutput: [
      "1: Sync Start",
      "2: Sync End",
      "3: Promise",
      "4: Timeout",
    ],
    explanation: "Sync code → Microtasks → Macrotasks",
  },

  asyncAwait: {
    name: "async/await Order",
    code: `
async function main() {
  console.log("2: async start")
  await Promise.resolve()
  console.log("4: after await")
}
console.log("1: before async")
main()
console.log("3: after async call")
    `,
    expectedOutput: [
      "1: before async",
      "2: async start",
      "3: after async call",
      "4: after await",
    ],
    explanation: "await creates a microtask pause",
  },

  hoistingDemo: {
    name: "Hoisting Behavior",
    code: `
console.log(typeof varTest)
console.log(funcTest())
// console.log(letTest) // Would throw ReferenceError

var varTest = "hello"
function funcTest() { return "function hoisted!" }
let letTest = "world"
    `,
    expectedOutput: ["undefined", "function hoisted!"],
    explanation: "var hoisted as undefined, function fully hoisted",
  },
};
```

```typescript
// components/visualizer/CallStackPanel.tsx
'use client'

import { useState } from 'react'

interface StackFrame {
  id: string
  name: string
  type: 'global' | 'function' | 'async'
}

interface CallStackPanelProps {
  frames: StackFrame[]
  isAnimating: boolean
}

export default function CallStackPanel({ frames, isAnimating }: CallStackPanelProps) {
  return (
    <div className="bg-yellow-50 border-2 border-yellow-300 rounded-lg p-4 min-h-64">
      <h3 className="font-bold text-yellow-800 mb-3">
        📚 Call Stack
        {isAnimating && (
          <span className="ml-2 text-sm font-normal animate-pulse">executing...</span>
        )}
      </h3>

      <div className="flex flex-col-reverse gap-2 min-h-48">
        {frames.length === 0 ? (
          <div className="text-gray-400 italic text-center mt-8">
            Stack is empty
          </div>
        ) : (
          frames.map((frame, index) => (
            <div
              key={frame.id}
              className={`
                p-3 rounded border-2 font-mono text-sm
                transition-all duration-300
                ${frame.type === 'global'
                  ? 'bg-blue-100 border-blue-300 text-blue-800'
                  : frame.type === 'async'
                  ? 'bg-purple-100 border-purple-300 text-purple-800'
                  : 'bg-yellow-100 border-yellow-400 text-yellow-800'
                }
                ${index === frames.length - 1 ? 'ring-2 ring-orange-400' : ''}
              `}
            >
              <div className="flex justify-between items-center">
                <span>{frame.name}()</span>
                {index === frames.length - 1 && (
                  <span className="text-xs bg-orange-400 text-white px-2 py-0.5 rounded">
                    ACTIVE
                  </span>
                )}
              </div>
            </div>
          ))
        )}
      </div>
    </div>
  )
}
```

```typescript
// hooks/useEventLoopSimulation.ts
"use client";

import { useState, useCallback } from "react";

interface ExecutionStep {
  type: "sync" | "microtask" | "macrotask";
  description: string;
  callStack: string[];
  microtaskQueue: string[];
  callbackQueue: string[];
  output: string | null;
}

export function useEventLoopSimulation(snippetKey: string) {
  const [currentStep, setCurrentStep] = useState(0);
  const [steps, setSteps] = useState<ExecutionStep[]>([]);
  const [isPlaying, setIsPlaying] = useState(false);
  const [outputs, setOutputs] = useState<string[]>([]);

  const generateSteps = useCallback((code: string): ExecutionStep[] => {
    // In a real implementation, this would use a custom parser/tracer
    // For demo purposes, returning pre-computed steps for known snippets

    return [
      {
        type: "sync",
        description: "Execute synchronous code",
        callStack: ["main()", 'console.log("1: Sync Start")'],
        microtaskQueue: [],
        callbackQueue: [],
        output: "1: Sync Start",
      },
      {
        type: "sync",
        description: "setTimeout registered with Web API",
        callStack: ["main()", "setTimeout(callback, 0)"],
        microtaskQueue: [],
        callbackQueue: [],
        output: null,
      },
      {
        type: "sync",
        description: "Promise resolves → callback queued as microtask",
        callStack: ["main()"],
        microtaskQueue: ["Promise.then callback"],
        callbackQueue: ["setTimeout callback"],
        output: null,
      },
      {
        type: "sync",
        description: "Execute last sync line",
        callStack: ["main()", 'console.log("2: Sync End")'],
        microtaskQueue: ["Promise.then callback"],
        callbackQueue: ["setTimeout callback"],
        output: "2: Sync End",
      },
      {
        type: "microtask",
        description: "Call Stack empty → Process Microtask Queue",
        callStack: ["Promise callback"],
        microtaskQueue: [],
        callbackQueue: ["setTimeout callback"],
        output: "3: Promise",
      },
      {
        type: "macrotask",
        description: "Microtask Queue empty → Process Callback Queue",
        callStack: ["setTimeout callback"],
        microtaskQueue: [],
        callbackQueue: [],
        output: "4: Timeout",
      },
    ];
  }, []);

  const play = useCallback(
    (code: string) => {
      const newSteps = generateSteps(code);
      setSteps(newSteps);
      setCurrentStep(0);
      setOutputs([]);
      setIsPlaying(true);

      // Auto-advance through steps
      newSteps.forEach((step, index) => {
        setTimeout(() => {
          setCurrentStep(index);
          if (step.output) {
            setOutputs((prev) => [...prev, step.output!]);
          }
          if (index === newSteps.length - 1) {
            setIsPlaying(false);
          }
        }, index * 1500); // 1.5 seconds per step
      });
    },
    [generateSteps],
  );

  const reset = useCallback(() => {
    setCurrentStep(0);
    setSteps([]);
    setOutputs([]);
    setIsPlaying(false);
  }, []);

  return {
    currentStep,
    steps,
    isPlaying,
    outputs,
    play,
    reset,
    currentState: steps[currentStep] ?? null,
  };
}
```

}

// Test:
myPromiseAll([Promise.resolve(1), Promise.resolve(2), Promise.resolve(3)]).then(

); // [1, 2, 3]

myPromiseAll([

function memoize(fn) {

  // Value: cached result
}

const expensiveAdd = (a, b) => {
  console.log("Computing...");
  return a + b;
};

const memoizedAdd = memoize(expensiveAdd);
memoizedAdd(1, 2); // Logs "Computing..." → 3
memoizedAdd(1, 2); // Uses cache → 3 (no "Computing..." log)
memoizedAdd(3, 4); // Logs "Computing..." → 7

### 5 Theory Questions

**T1: Explain the complete lifecycle of a JavaScript function call — from the moment it's invoked to when it returns, covering execution context creation, call stack, memory allocation, and cleanup.**

**T2: What is the Temporal Dead Zone (TDZ)? Why did the JavaScript specification designers choose to make `let` and `const` throw `ReferenceError` instead of returning `undefined` like `var` does?**

**T3: Explain why microtasks (Promises) have higher priority than macrotasks (setTimeout). What design decision led to this, and what practical implications does it have for web application performance?**

**T4: In React, the `useEffect` cleanup function and the component lifecycle relate to the JavaScript event loop and async execution. Explain how React's rendering cycle interacts with the JavaScript Event Loop, and why `useEffect` callbacks run after the browser paint.**

**T5: Explain JIT (Just-In-Time) compilation, deoptimization, and how writing "type-stable" JavaScript leads to better performance. How does TypeScript indirectly improve runtime performance beyond just providing type safety?**

---

### 2 Machine Coding Problems

**MCP 1: Build an Async Task Scheduler**

Build a `TaskScheduler` class that:

- Accepts tasks with a `priority` (microtask or macrotask) and `delay`
- Executes tasks in the correct Event Loop order (microtasks before macrotasks)
- Supports task cancellation
- Provides a queue size getter
- Logs execution order with timestamps

```typescript
interface Task {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  priority: "micro" | "macro";
  delay: number; // ms (only for macro)
  callback: () => void;
}

class TaskScheduler {
  addMicroTask(name: string, callback: () => void): string;
  addMacroTask(name: string, delay: number, callback: () => void): string;
  cancel(id: string): boolean;
  getQueueSize(): { micro: number; macro: number };
}

// Usage:
const scheduler = new TaskScheduler();
scheduler.addMacroTask("fetch data", 0, () => console.log("fetched"));
scheduler.addMicroTask("update state", () => console.log("state updated"));
// Output: "state updated" → "fetched" (microtask first!)
```

Requirements:

- TypeScript implementation
- Proper use of `queueMicrotask()` for microtasks
- `setTimeout()` for macrotasks
- Unique ID generation for cancellation
- Must demonstrate correct Event Loop priority

---

**MCP 2: Build a Scope & Hoisting Analyzer**

Build a Next.js API route that:

- Accepts a JavaScript code snippet (as a string)
- Analyzes it to identify:
  - All `var`, `let`, `const`, `function` declarations
  - Which variables will be hoisted and how
  - Potential TDZ issues (accessing let/const before declaration)
  - Scope level of each declaration (global, function, block)
  - Variable shadowing occurrences
- Returns a structured analysis report

```typescript
// POST /api/analyze-scope
// Body: { code: string }
// Response:
{
  declarations: [
    {
      name: string
      type: 'var' | 'let' | 'const' | 'function' | 'class'
      scope: 'global' | 'function' | 'block'
      hoisted: boolean
      initializedAs: 'undefined' | 'TDZ' | 'full-function' | 'null'
      line: number
      potentialTDZAccess: boolean
    }
  ],
  shadowings: [
    {
      name: string
      outerScope: string
      innerScope: string
      line: number
    }
  ],
  warnings: string[]
}
```

Requirements:

- Next.js App Router with Route Handler
- Basic parsing using regex or a simple parser (no need for full AST)
- TypeScript
- Return meaningful warnings (TDZ access, accidental globals, shadowing)
- Frontend form to input code and display the analysis visually

---

> [!NOTE]
> **Chapter 2 Summary — Key Takeaways:**
>
> 1. V8 uses Ignition (interpreter) + TurboFan (JIT compiler) for fast execution
> 2. The JavaScript Runtime = Engine + Call Stack + Heap + Web APIs + Event Loop + Queues
> 3. JavaScript is single-threaded — one call stack, one thing at a time
> 4. Event Loop order: Sync Code → Drain ALL Microtasks → ONE Macrotask → Repeat
> 5. Promises (microtasks) ALWAYS run before setTimeout (macrotask) — even setTimeout(0)
> 6. `var` is hoisted + initialized as `undefined`; `let`/`const` are hoisted into TDZ
> 7. Function declarations are fully hoisted (name + body); function expressions are not
> 8. Scope is determined LEXICALLY (where code is written), not dynamically (where it's called)
> 9. Scope chain: looks from inner to outer, stops at first match or throws ReferenceError
> 10. `var` ignores block scope; `let`/`const` are block-scoped

## 3.1 Variables — var, let, const

<a id="31-variables--var-let-const"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| `var` | Function-scoped, hoisted as `undefined`, re-declarable, mutable |
| `let` | Block-scoped, TDZ, not re-declarable, mutable |
| `const` | Block-scoped, TDZ, must initialize, reference immutable |
| `const` with Objects/Arrays | Why `.push()` works on a const array |
| When to Use What | Modern best practice — prefer `const` → `let` → avoid `var` |

---

### What Are Variables?

A **variable** is a named container that stores a value in memory. In JavaScript, variables can be declared using three keywords: `var`, `let`, and `const` — each with different behavior around scope, hoisting, and mutability.

> **Hinglish:** Variable ek labelled dabba hai jisme aap koi bhi cheez rakh sakte hain. `var`, `let`, `const` teen alag types ke dabbe hain — har ek ke apne rules hain ki kitne area mein dikhega, kab use hoga, aur kya andar ki cheez badal sakti hai.

---

### Complete Comparison Table

| Feature | `var` | `let` | `const` |
|---------|-------|-------|---------|
| **Scope** | Function-scoped | Block-scoped | Block-scoped |
| **Hoisting** | ✅ Hoisted as `undefined` | ✅ Hoisted to TDZ | ✅ Hoisted to TDZ |
| **Before declaration** | Returns `undefined` | `ReferenceError` | `ReferenceError` |
| **Re-declaration** | ✅ Allowed | ❌ Not allowed | ❌ Not allowed |
| **Re-assignment** | ✅ Allowed | ✅ Allowed | ❌ Not allowed |
| **Must initialize** | ❌ Optional | ❌ Optional | ✅ Required |
| **Global object prop** | ✅ Yes (`window.x`) | ❌ No | ❌ No |
| **Temporal Dead Zone** | ❌ No TDZ | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes |
| **Use in modern code** | ❌ Avoid | ✅ For mutable | ✅ Prefer always |

---

### `var` — Full Behavior

```javascript
// 1. Function-scoped (not block-scoped)
function varDemo() {
  if (true) {
    var x = 10  // var ignores {} block scope
  }
  console.log(x)  // 10 ✅ — var leaked out of if block
}

// 2. Hoisted as undefined
console.log(y)  // undefined (no error!)
var y = 5
console.log(y)  // 5

// 3. Re-declarable (dangerous!)
var name = "Rahul"
var name = "Priya"  // ✅ No error — silently overwrites!
console.log(name)   // "Priya"

// 4. Attaches to global window object (browser)
var globalVar = "I'm on window"
console.log(window.globalVar)  // "I'm on window" (browser only)

// 5. Classic closure bug with var in loops
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i), 0)
}
// Output: 3, 3, 3 (NOT 0, 1, 2)
// Because var i is shared across all iterations
```

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **Why `var` is dangerous:** The re-declaration feature means you can accidentally overwrite a variable declared earlier without any error. In large codebases, this silently causes bugs. Always use `let` or `const` in modern JavaScript.

---

### `let` — Full Behavior

```javascript
// 1. Block-scoped
function letDemo() {
  if (true) {
    let x = 10   // Only exists inside this {} block
  }
  console.log(x) // ❌ ReferenceError: x is not defined
}

// 2. Temporal Dead Zone (TDZ)
console.log(age) // ❌ ReferenceError: Cannot access 'age' before initialization
let age = 25
console.log(age) // 25

// 3. NOT re-declarable in same scope
let city = "Mumbai"
let city = "Delhi"  // ❌ SyntaxError: Identifier 'city' has already been declared

// 4. Re-assignable (mutable)
let score = 0
score = 10   // ✅ Allowed
score++      // ✅ Allowed
console.log(score) // 11

// 5. Loop fix — let creates new binding per iteration
for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i), 0)
}
// Output: 0, 1, 2 ✅ Each iteration has its own i
```

---

### `const` — Full Behavior

```javascript
// 1. Must be initialized at declaration
const PI           // ❌ SyntaxError: Missing initializer in const declaration
const PI = 3.14   // ✅ Correct

// 2. Cannot re-assign
const MAX_SIZE = 100
MAX_SIZE = 200     // ❌ TypeError: Assignment to constant variable.

// 3. Block-scoped (same as let)
{
  const blockConst = "only here"
}
console.log(blockConst)  // ❌ ReferenceError

// 4. TDZ — same as let
console.log(TAX)  // ❌ ReferenceError
const TAX = 0.18

// 5. Does NOT mean immutable — only the BINDING is constant!
const arr = [1, 2, 3]
arr.push(4)           // ✅ Allowed — mutating content, not rebinding
arr[0] = 99           // ✅ Allowed
arr = [4, 5, 6]       // ❌ TypeError — rebinding is not allowed

const obj = { name: "Rahul" }
obj.name = "Priya"    // ✅ Allowed — mutating property
obj.age = 25          // ✅ Allowed — adding property
obj = { name: "X" }  // ❌ TypeError — rebinding not allowed
```

---

### `const` with Objects & Arrays — Deep Dive

> **Hinglish:** `const` ka matlab hai "dabba badlo mat" (reference mat badlo), andar ki cheez badal sakte ho. Array mein `push` karna allowed hai, lekin `arr = newArray` nahi kar sakte.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph STACK["Stack Memory"]
        C["const arr\nReference: 0x1234"]
    end

    subgraph HEAP["Heap Memory"]
        H["0x1234:\n[1, 2, 3, 4]"]
    end

    C -->|"points to"| H

    style STACK fill:#fff3e0
    style HEAP fill:#e8f5e9
```

```javascript
const user = {
  name: "Rahul",
  address: {
    city: "Mumbai"
  }
}

// Allowed — modifying existing properties:
user.name = "Priya"            // ✅
user.address.city = "Delhi"    // ✅ (nested object is also mutable)
user.email = "p@example.com"   // ✅ (adding new property)

// NOT allowed — changing what user points to:
user = { name: "Priya" }       // ❌ TypeError

// To truly freeze an object (prevent mutations):
const frozen = Object.freeze({ value: 42 })
frozen.value = 100    // Silently fails (strict mode: throws TypeError)
console.log(frozen.value)  // 42 — unchanged

// Note: Object.freeze() is SHALLOW — nested objects are NOT frozen
const data = Object.freeze({ inner: { x: 1 } })
data.inner.x = 99   // ✅ Still works! inner is not frozen
```

---

### When to Use What — Modern Best Practice

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A["Declaring a variable"] --> B{"Will the value\never be reassigned?"}
    B -->|"No — value is fixed"| C["✅ Use const\n(preferred default)"]
    B -->|"Yes — value changes"| D{"Is it a counter,\nloop variable, or\nmutable state?"}
    D -->|"Yes"| E["✅ Use let"]
    D -->|"Unsure"| F["Start with const\nChange to let if needed"]
    C --> G["❌ Never use var"]
    E --> G

    style C fill:#c8e6c9
    style E fill:#bbdefb
    style G fill:#ffcdd2
```

**Real-world React Pattern:**

```javascript
// ✅ Modern React — const everywhere by default
const App = () => {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0)    // const for state variable
  const MAX_COUNT = 100                     // const for fixed value
  const handleClick = () => setCount(c => c + 1) // const for function

  // let only when truly needed:
  let message = "Loading..."
  if (count > 0) message = `Count: ${count}`

  return <div onClick={handleClick}>{message}</div>
}
```

---

### 🎯 10+ Interview Questions — Section 3.1

**Q1: What is the difference between `var`, `let`, and `const`?**

> `var` is function-scoped, hoisted as `undefined`, and can be re-declared and re-assigned. It's considered legacy and error-prone. `let` is block-scoped, enters the TDZ when hoisted (accessing before declaration throws `ReferenceError`), cannot be re-declared in the same scope, but can be re-assigned. `const` behaves like `let` (block-scoped, TDZ) but additionally requires initialization at declaration and prevents re-assignment of the binding. However, `const` does not make the value immutable — objects and arrays declared with `const` can still have their contents mutated.

---

**Q2: What is the output?**

```javascript
function test() {
  console.log(a)   // ?
  console.log(b)   // ?
  var a = 1
  let b = 2
}
test()
```

> **Output:** `undefined` → `ReferenceError: Cannot access 'b' before initialization`
> `var a` is hoisted and initialized as `undefined`. `let b` is hoisted to TDZ — accessing it before its declaration line throws `ReferenceError`.

---

**Q3: What is the output?**

```javascript
var x = 1
let y = 1

if (true) {
  var x = 2   // same var x (function scoped)
  let y = 2   // new y (block scoped)
  console.log(x, y)  // ?
}

console.log(x, y)  // ?
```

> **Output:** `2 2` → `2 1`
> Inside the `if`: `var x = 2` overwrites the function-scoped `x`. `let y = 2` creates a NEW block-scoped `y`. Outside the `if`: `x` is still `2` (var is function-scoped, same variable). `y` is `1` (outer `let y` unchanged).

---

**Q4: Can you re-assign a property of a `const` object? Can you prevent it?**

> Yes — `const` only prevents re-assignment of the **variable binding** (the reference), not the contents of the object. `obj.prop = newValue` is allowed. To prevent property mutations, use `Object.freeze(obj)`, which prevents adding, deleting, or modifying properties. However, `Object.freeze()` is shallow — nested objects require deep freezing. For deep immutability, use libraries like Immer (used in Redux Toolkit) or recursive freezing.

---

**Q5: What is the output?**

```javascript
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log("var:", i), 0)
}

for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log("let:", j), 0)
}
```

> **Output:** `var: 3, var: 3, var: 3` → `let: 0, let: 1, let: 2`
> With `var`, the loop creates ONE shared `i` — by the time callbacks execute, the loop has finished and `i = 3`. With `let`, each iteration creates a NEW block-scoped binding for `j`, so each callback captures its own value.

---

**Q6: What is the output?**

```javascript
const arr = [1, 2, 3]
arr.push(4)
console.log(arr)
arr = [5, 6, 7]
```

> **Output:** `[1, 2, 3, 4]` is logged, then `TypeError: Assignment to constant variable.`
> `arr.push(4)` mutates the array content — allowed with `const`. `arr = [5, 6, 7]` attempts to rebind the const variable — throws `TypeError`.

---

**Q7: Is there any difference between `let` in a for loop vs outside?**

```javascript
let i = 0
for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  // inner i
}
console.log(i) // ?
```

> **Output:** `0` — The `let i` inside the `for` loop creates a **new block-scoped** binding, completely separate from the outer `let i = 0`. The outer `i` is never modified by the loop.

---

**Q8: Why was `let` introduced if `var` already existed?**

> `var` has several problems: (1) **Function scope** — `var` declared inside an `if` or `for` block leaks out to the function scope, causing unintended access. (2) **Re-declaration** — silently overwriting previously declared variables leads to hard-to-find bugs. (3) **Hoisting confusion** — `var` being initialized as `undefined` masks bugs where code accesses variables before declaration. `let` (and `const`) were introduced in ES6 to solve these issues with block scoping, TDZ errors, and no re-declaration — making JavaScript code more predictable and maintainable.

---

**Q9: What is the output?**

```javascript
let x = 'outer'
{
  console.log(x)  // ?
  let x = 'inner'
}
```

> **Output:** `ReferenceError: Cannot access 'x' before initialization`
> The inner `let x = 'inner'` is hoisted to the top of the block and enters TDZ. Even though the outer `x = 'outer'` exists, the inner `x` shadows it within the block — and accessing it before the declaration line causes `ReferenceError`. This is a classic TDZ + shadowing trap.

---

**Q10: What does `const` guarantee and what does it NOT guarantee?**

> `const` guarantees: (1) The variable **must be initialized** at declaration. (2) The **binding** (the reference/memory address the variable holds) cannot be changed — no re-assignment. `const` does NOT guarantee: (1) The **value** is immutable — objects and arrays can still be mutated. (2) Deep immutability — nested objects are fully mutable. `const` is about the **pointer** being constant, not the data it points to.

---

**Q11: What is the output?**

```javascript
const obj = Object.freeze({ a: 1, b: { c: 2 } })
obj.a = 100          // silently fails
obj.b.c = 200        // succeeds! (shallow freeze)
console.log(obj.a)   // ?
console.log(obj.b.c) // ?
```

> **Output:** `1` → `200`
> `Object.freeze()` is shallow — it freezes only the top-level properties. `obj.a = 100` silently fails (in strict mode throws TypeError). `obj.b` is a reference to a nested object that is NOT frozen, so `obj.b.c = 200` succeeds.
