## 3.2 Data Types — Primitive vs Reference

<a id="32-data-types--primitive-vs-reference"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Primitive Types | 7 types stored by value on Stack |
| Reference Types | Objects stored by reference on Heap |
| `typeof` Operator | All return values and historical quirks |
| `instanceof` Operator | Prototype chain checking |
| `Object.prototype.toString.call()` | Reliable type checking method |

---

### What Are Data Types?

JavaScript is a **dynamically typed** language — variables do not have fixed types; types are determined at runtime and can change.

> **Hinglish:** JavaScript mein variable ko pehle type declare nahi karna padta — runtime par automatically pata chalta hai. Ek variable pehle number ho sakta hai, baad mein string — yahi dynamic typing hai.

---

### Primitive Types — Stored by Value

**Primitive types** are immutable values stored directly in the **Call Stack**. When you assign a primitive to another variable, a complete **copy** is made.

> **Hinglish:** Primitive types "original cheez" store karte hain — jaise ek number `5`. Jab dusre variable mein assign karo, copy ho jaata hai. Dono independent hote hain.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph PRIMITIVES["7 Primitive Types"]
        S["string\n'hello', \"world\", \`template\`"]
        N["number\n42, 3.14, -10, Infinity, NaN"]
        BI["bigint\n9007199254740991n"]
        B["boolean\ntrue, false"]
        U["undefined\nDeclared but not assigned"]
        NULL["null\nIntentional absence of value"]
        SY["symbol\nSymbol('unique identifier')"]
    end

    style PRIMITIVES fill:#fff9c4
    style S fill:#e3f2fd
    style N fill:#e8f5e9
    style BI fill:#fce4ec
    style B fill:#f3e5f5
    style U fill:#e0f7fa
    style NULL fill:#fff3e0
    style SY fill:#e8eaf6
```

```javascript
// Pass by VALUE — copies are independent
let a = 10
let b = a   // b gets a COPY of 10
b = 20
console.log(a)  // 10 — unchanged
console.log(b)  // 20 — independent copy

// String immutability:
let str = "hello"
str[0] = "H"    // silently fails — strings are immutable primitives
console.log(str) // "hello" — unchanged

// String operations return NEW strings:
let upper = str.toUpperCase()  // returns new string "HELLO"
console.log(str)   // "hello" — original unchanged
console.log(upper) // "HELLO" — new string
```

---

### Reference Types — Stored by Reference

**Reference types** are stored in the **Heap**. The Stack holds a **reference (memory address)** pointing to the heap location. Assigning to another variable copies the reference — both variables point to the **same object**.

> **Hinglish:** Reference types ka actual data Heap mein hota hai. Stack mein sirf address hota hai. Jab dusre variable mein assign karo, address copy hota hai — dono ek hi jagah point karte hain. Ek badlao toh dono mein change dikhe.

```javascript
// Pass by REFERENCE — shared reference
let obj1 = { name: "Rahul", age: 25 }
let obj2 = obj1   // obj2 gets the SAME reference (address)

obj2.name = "Priya"
console.log(obj1.name)  // "Priya" — obj1 also changed! Same object in heap.

// Arrays — same behavior:
let arr1 = [1, 2, 3]
let arr2 = arr1
arr2.push(4)
console.log(arr1)  // [1, 2, 3, 4] — arr1 also changed!

// Functions — also reference types:
function greet() { return "hi" }
let fn = greet   // fn holds reference to same function object
fn === greet     // true — same reference

// Reference types in JavaScript:
// object {}, array [], function(){}, Date, RegExp, Map, Set, WeakMap, WeakSet
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph STACK["Stack Memory"]
        A["let a = 10\n(value: 10)"]
        B["let b = a\n(value: 10 — COPY)"]
        O1["let obj1\n(ref: 0x1234)"]
        O2["let obj2\n(ref: 0x1234 — SAME!)"]
    end

    subgraph HEAP["Heap Memory"]
        H1["0x1234:\n{ name: 'Priya', age: 25 }"]
    end

    O1 -->|"points to"| H1
    O2 -->|"points to SAME"| H1

    style STACK fill:#fff3e0
    style HEAP fill:#e8f5e9
```

---

### Complete Type Reference Table

| Type | Category | `typeof` result | Example | Truthy/Falsy |
|------|----------|----------------|---------|--------------|
| `string` | Primitive | `"string"` | `"hello"`, `""` | `""` → falsy, rest truthy |
| `number` | Primitive | `"number"` | `42`, `NaN`, `Infinity` | `0`, `NaN` → falsy |
| `bigint` | Primitive | `"bigint"` | `42n`, `9007199254740991n` | `0n` → falsy |
| `boolean` | Primitive | `"boolean"` | `true`, `false` | `false` → falsy |
| `undefined` | Primitive | `"undefined"` | `undefined` | Falsy |
| `null` | Primitive | `"object"` ⚠️ | `null` | Falsy |
| `symbol` | Primitive | `"symbol"` | `Symbol("id")` | Truthy |
| `object` | Reference | `"object"` | `{}`, `[]`, `null` | `{}` truthy, `null` falsy |
| `function` | Reference | `"function"` ⚠️ | `function(){}` | Truthy |

---

### `typeof` Operator — Quirks You MUST Know

> **Hinglish:** `typeof` operator variable ka type batata hai — lekin iske kuch quirks hain jo interviews mein trap karte hain.

```javascript
// Normal results:
typeof "hello"      // "string"
typeof 42           // "number"
typeof true         // "boolean"
typeof undefined    // "undefined"
typeof Symbol("x")  // "symbol"
typeof 42n          // "bigint"

// ⚠️ QUIRK 1: null returns "object" (historical bug in JS — cannot be fixed)
typeof null         // "object" — NOT "null"!
// Checking for null: value === null (use strict equality)

// ⚠️ QUIRK 2: Arrays return "object" (not "array")
typeof []           // "object"
typeof {}           // "object"
typeof new Date()   // "object"

// ⚠️ QUIRK 3: Functions return "function" (special case)
typeof function(){} // "function"
typeof (() => {})   // "function"
typeof class{}      // "function" (classes are special functions!)

// ⚠️ QUIRK 4: Undeclared variable — typeof is SAFE (no ReferenceError)
typeof undeclaredVar  // "undefined" (safe!)
undeclaredVar         // ❌ ReferenceError

// Correct ways to check types:
Array.isArray([])         // true ✅
value === null            // true for null ✅
typeof value === "string" // for strings ✅
```

---

### `instanceof` Operator — Prototype Chain Check

`instanceof` checks whether an object has a specific constructor's `prototype` anywhere in its prototype chain.

> **Hinglish:** `instanceof` poochta hai "kya yeh cheez iss class ka instance hai?" — prototype chain mein check karta hai.

```javascript
// Basic usage:
[] instanceof Array         // true
{} instanceof Object        // true (technically — see below)
new Date() instanceof Date  // true
/regex/ instanceof RegExp   // true

function Person(name) { this.name = name }
const p = new Person("Rahul")
p instanceof Person         // true
p instanceof Object         // true (Person.prototype inherits from Object.prototype)

// ⚠️ GOTCHA: instanceof with primitive wrappers
"hello" instanceof String   // false! (primitive, not String object)
new String("hello") instanceof String  // true (String object)

// ⚠️ instanceof fails across iframes/realms:
// An Array from a different iframe won't be instanceof Array
// Solution: Array.isArray() works across realms
```

---

### `Object.prototype.toString.call()` — The Reliable Type Checker

For reliable type checking of ALL values (especially arrays, null, and built-in types), use `Object.prototype.toString.call()`:

> **Hinglish:** Yeh sabse reliable type checker hai — `typeof` ke quirks se bachne ke liye. Yeh exact type string return karta hai jaise `[object Array]`, `[object Null]`.

```javascript
Object.prototype.toString.call("hello")     // "[object String]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(42)          // "[object Number]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(true)        // "[object Boolean]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(null)        // "[object Null]" ✅ (not "object")
Object.prototype.toString.call(undefined)   // "[object Undefined]"
Object.prototype.toString.call([])          // "[object Array]" ✅ (not "object")
Object.prototype.toString.call({})          // "[object Object]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(function(){})// "[object Function]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(new Date())  // "[object Date]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(/regex/)     // "[object RegExp]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(new Map())   // "[object Map]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(new Set())   // "[object Set]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(Symbol())    // "[object Symbol]"

// Building a reliable type checker function:
function getType(value) {
  return Object.prototype.toString.call(value).slice(8, -1).toLowerCase()
}

getType(null)        // "null"
getType([])          // "array"
getType({})          // "object"
getType(new Date())  // "date"
getType(undefined)   // "undefined"
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 3.2

**Q1: What is the difference between primitive and reference types?**

> **Primitive types** (string, number, bigint, boolean, undefined, null, symbol) are immutable values stored directly in the Call Stack. When assigned to another variable, a complete copy is made — changes to one don't affect the other. **Reference types** (objects, arrays, functions) are stored in the Heap; the Stack holds a reference (memory address). When assigned, the reference is copied — both variables point to the same heap object, so mutations through one variable are visible through the other.

**Q2: Why does `typeof null === 'object'`?**

> This is a historical bug in JavaScript that has existed since its first version in 1995 and cannot be fixed due to backward compatibility concerns. In the original implementation, JavaScript values were stored as a type tag + value. The type tag for objects was `000`. `null` was represented as the null pointer `0x00`, which also starts with `000` — so the type-checking code incorrectly identified it as an object. The fix was proposed (making `typeof null === 'null'`) but rejected to avoid breaking existing code. The safe way to check for `null` is `value === null`.

**Q3: What is the difference between `instanceof` and `typeof`?**

> `typeof` is a unary operator that returns a string indicating the primitive type category of a value. It works on primitives and functions but has quirks (null → "object", arrays → "object"). `instanceof` checks whether an object's prototype chain contains the `prototype` property of a specific constructor — it's designed for reference types and class instances. Key differences: `typeof` works on primitives; `instanceof` requires an object. `typeof` doesn't need a class to check against; `instanceof` does. `instanceof` can fail across iframes/realms; `Array.isArray()` doesn't.

**Q4: How would you reliably check if a value is an array?**

> The most reliable method is `Array.isArray(value)` — it works correctly across different JavaScript realms (iframes, Web Workers) where `instanceof Array` can fail. Other methods: `Object.prototype.toString.call(value) === '[object Array]'` also works reliably. Avoid `typeof value === 'object'` (returns true for objects too) and `value instanceof Array` (can fail across frames).

**Q5 (Tricky): What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log(typeof typeof 42)
```

> **Output:** `"string"`
> `typeof 42` evaluates to `"number"` (a string). `typeof "number"` evaluates to `"string"`. So the final result is `"string"`. This is a common interview trick — `typeof` always returns a string, so `typeof typeof anything` is always `"string"`.

## 3.3 Type Coercion & Type Checking

<a id="33-type-coercion--type-checking"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Implicit Coercion | Automatic type conversion by JS engine |
| Explicit Conversion | Manual conversion using Number(), String(), Boolean() |
| Truthy & Falsy Values | The 8 falsy values and everything else |
| `==` vs `===` | Abstract equality vs strict equality |
| `Object.is()` | Special cases: NaN and -0 |
| 20+ Output Questions | Every coercion trap tested in interviews |

---

### What is Type Coercion?

**Type coercion** is JavaScript's automatic (implicit) or manual (explicit) conversion of a value from one type to another.

> **Hinglish:** Coercion matlab "type badlna." Implicit coercion automatic hota hai — JS khud type badal deta hai bina tumhare kaha. Explicit coercion manually karna padta hai — jaise `Number("42")` likhna.

---

### Implicit Coercion — How It Works

```javascript
// String coercion (+ with string):
"5" + 1         // "51" (number 1 → string "1")
"5" + true      // "5true"
"5" + null      // "5null"
"5" + undefined // "5undefined"
"5" + {}        // "5[object Object]"
"5" + []        // "5" ([] → "" → "5" + "" = "5")

// Numeric coercion (arithmetic operators - * / %):
"5" - 1         // 4  (string "5" → number 5)
"5" * 2         // 10
"5" / 2         // 2.5
"5" - "3"       // 2
true + 1        // 2  (true → 1)
false + 1       // 1  (false → 0)
null + 1        // 1  (null → 0)
undefined + 1   // NaN (undefined → NaN)
[] + []         // "" (both [] → "")
[] + {}         // "[object Object]" ([] → "", {} → "[object Object]")
{} + []         // 0 in some contexts! ({} treated as block, +[] = 0)

// Boolean coercion (in conditionals):
if (1) {}       // truthy — block runs
if ("") {}      // falsy — block skipped
if ([]) {}      // truthy! (empty array is truthy)
if ({}) {}      // truthy! (empty object is truthy)
if (null) {}    // falsy
```

---

### Truthy & Falsy Values — Complete Reference

> **Hinglish:** Truthy matlab "true ki tarah kaam karta hai" condition mein. Falsy matlab "false ki tarah kaam karta hai." Sirf 8 falsy values hain — baaki sab truthy hain.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph FALSY["❌ Falsy Values (Only 8)"]
        F1["false"]
        F2["0"]
        F3["-0"]
        F4["0n (BigInt zero)"]
        F5["'' or \"\" or `` (empty string)"]
        F6["null"]
        F7["undefined"]
        F8["NaN"]
    end

    subgraph TRUTHY["✅ Truthy — Everything Else"]
        T1["true"]
        T2["Any non-zero number (1, -1, 3.14)"]
        T3["Any non-empty string ('0', 'false', ' ')"]
        T4["[] (empty array!)"]
        T5["{} (empty object!)"]
        T6["function(){}"]
        T7["Infinity, -Infinity"]
        T8["new Date()"]
    end

    style FALSY fill:#ffcdd2
    style TRUTHY fill:#c8e6c9
```

```javascript
// Common truthy/falsy traps:
Boolean([])          // true  ⚠️ empty array is TRUTHY!
Boolean({})          // true  ⚠️ empty object is TRUTHY!
Boolean("0")         // true  ⚠️ string "0" is TRUTHY!
Boolean("false")     // true  ⚠️ string "false" is TRUTHY!
Boolean(0)           // false
Boolean(-0)          // false ⚠️ negative zero is FALSY!
Boolean(0n)          // false ⚠️ BigInt zero is FALSY!
Boolean(NaN)         // false
Boolean(null)        // false
Boolean(undefined)   // false
Boolean("")          // false

// React rendering gotcha:
const count = 0
return <div>{count && <Component />}</div>
// ⚠️ Renders "0"! because 0 is falsy but renders as text
// Fix: return <div>{count > 0 && <Component />}</div>
// OR: return <div>{Boolean(count) && <Component />}</div>
```

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **React Interview Trap:** `{0 && <Component />}` renders `0` on screen (not nothing) because `0` is falsy but React still renders the number `0`. Use `{count > 0 && <Component />}` or `{!!count && <Component />}` to avoid this.

---

### Explicit Conversion

```javascript
// To Number:
Number("42")        // 42
Number("3.14")      // 3.14
Number("")          // 0
Number("  ")        // 0 (whitespace only)
Number(null)        // 0
Number(undefined)   // NaN
Number(true)        // 1
Number(false)       // 0
Number([])          // 0  ([] → "" → 0)
Number([1])         // 1  ([1] → "1" → 1)
Number([1,2])       // NaN ([1,2] → "1,2" → NaN)
Number({})          // NaN ({} → "[object Object]" → NaN)
Number("abc")       // NaN

// parseInt vs Number for strings:
parseInt("42px")    // 42  (stops at non-numeric)
Number("42px")      // NaN (fails completely)
parseInt("0x1A")    // 26  (hex parsing)
parseInt("010")     // 10  (octal in some environments)

// To String:
String(42)          // "42"
String(null)        // "null"
String(undefined)   // "undefined"
String(true)        // "true"
String([1,2,3])     // "1,2,3"
String({})          // "[object Object]"
(42).toString()     // "42"
(255).toString(16)  // "ff" (hexadecimal)
(8).toString(2)     // "1000" (binary)

// To Boolean:
Boolean(42)         // true
Boolean(0)          // false
Boolean("hello")    // true
Boolean("")         // false
Boolean(null)       // false
Boolean(undefined)  // false
!!value             // Double negation — idiomatic boolean conversion
```

---

### `==` vs `===` — The Most Common Interview Topic

**Abstract Equality (`==`)** performs type coercion before comparing.
**Strict Equality (`===`)** compares value AND type — no coercion.

> **Hinglish:** `==` type badal ke compare karta hai — "loose comparison." `===` type aur value dono exactly same chahiye — "strict comparison." Modern code mein hamesha `===` use karo.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A["== (Abstract Equality)"] --> B{"Same type?"}
    B -->|"Yes"| C["Compare values directly\n(like ===)"]
    B -->|"No"| D{"Special cases?"}
    D -->|"null == undefined"| E["✅ true (only each other)"]
    D -->|"String == Number"| F["Convert string to number\nthen compare"]
    D -->|"Boolean == anything"| G["Convert boolean to number\nthen compare"]
    D -->|"Object == Primitive"| H["Call valueOf/toString\nconvert object to primitive"]
    D -->|"null/undefined == other"| I["❌ false\n(not equal to 0, false, '')"]

    style A fill:#ffcdd2
    style E fill:#c8e6c9
```

```javascript
// == coercion rules:
null == undefined    // true  (special rule)
null == false        // false (null only equals null and undefined)
null == 0            // false
undefined == false   // false

1 == "1"             // true  (string → number: "1" → 1)
1 == true            // true  (boolean → number: true → 1)
0 == false           // true  (boolean → number: false → 0)
0 == ""              // true  (string → number: "" → 0)
0 == null            // false (special rule: null only == undefined)
"" == false          // true  ("" → 0, false → 0)

// The infamous coercion chain:
"" == 0              // true
0 == false           // true
"" == false          // true
// All three "equal" 0 but "" does NOT equal false... wait:
"" == false          // true! (both convert to 0)

// Object coercion:
[] == false          // true  ([] → "" → 0, false → 0)
[] == 0              // true  ([] → "" → 0)
[] == ""             // true  ([] → "")
[[]] == ""           // true  ([[]] → [""] → "" via toString)
{} == "[object Object]"  // true ({}.toString() = "[object Object]")

// === (strict equality — no coercion):
1 === "1"     // false (different types)
1 === true    // false
0 === false   // false
null === undefined  // false
```

---

### `Object.is()` — Handles NaN and -0

```javascript
// Object.is() vs ===:
NaN === NaN           // false (NaN is not equal to itself!)
Object.is(NaN, NaN)  // true ✅

0 === -0              // true (=== doesn't distinguish)
Object.is(0, -0)     // false ✅ (Object.is does distinguish)

// For all other cases, Object.is() behaves like ===:
Object.is(1, 1)       // true
Object.is(1, 2)       // false
Object.is(null, null) // true
Object.is("a", "a")  // true

// Why does this matter?
// React uses Object.is() internally for:
// - useState: determining if state actually changed (to trigger re-render)
// - useEffect: comparing dependency array values
// - useMemo/useCallback: checking if deps changed
```

> [!TIP]
> **React Internals:** React uses `Object.is()` (not `===`) to compare previous and new state/props values. This means `NaN` as state will prevent re-renders (NaN is same as NaN per Object.is), and `-0` and `0` are treated as different values. Understanding this helps debug subtle re-render issues.

---

### 🎯 20+ Coercion Output Interview Questions

**Q1:** `console.log(1 + "2" + 3)`
> **Output:** `"123"` — `1 + "2"` = `"12"` (string concat), then `"12" + 3` = `"123"`

**Q2:** `console.log(1 + 2 + "3")`
> **Output:** `"33"` — `1 + 2` = `3` (numeric), then `3 + "3"` = `"33"` (string concat)

**Q3:** `console.log("3" - 1)`
> **Output:** `2` — `-` triggers numeric coercion: `"3"` → `3`, then `3 - 1 = 2`

**Q4:** `console.log(true + true)`
> **Output:** `2` — `true → 1`, `1 + 1 = 2`

**Q5:** `console.log(null + 1)`
> **Output:** `1` — `null → 0`, `0 + 1 = 1`

**Q6:** `console.log(undefined + 1)`
> **Output:** `NaN` — `undefined → NaN`, `NaN + 1 = NaN`

**Q7:** `console.log([] + [])`
> **Output:** `""` — both arrays → `""`, `"" + "" = ""`

**Q8:** `console.log([] + {})`
> **Output:** `"[object Object]"` — `[]` → `""`, `{}` → `"[object Object]"`, concat

**Q9:** `console.log({} + [])`
> **Output:** `0` OR `"[object Object]"` (context-dependent!)
> As a statement: `{}` is parsed as an empty block, `+[]` = `+""` = `0`
> In an expression: `{}` + `[]` = `"[object Object]"` + `""` = `"[object Object]"`

**Q10:** `console.log(+"3")`
> **Output:** `3` — unary `+` converts to number

**Q11:** `console.log(+"")`
> **Output:** `0` — empty string → 0

**Q12:** `console.log(+null)`
> **Output:** `0` — null → 0

**Q13:** `console.log(+undefined)`
> **Output:** `NaN`

**Q14:** `console.log(+[])`
> **Output:** `0` — `[]` → `""` → `0`

**Q15:** `console.log(+{})`
> **Output:** `NaN` — `{}` → `"[object Object]"` → `NaN`

**Q16:** `console.log(false == "")`
> **Output:** `true` — both coerce to `0`

**Q17:** `console.log(null == undefined)`
> **Output:** `true` — special rule in spec

**Q18:** `console.log(null == false)`
> **Output:** `false` — null only equals null/undefined

**Q19:** `console.log(NaN == NaN)`
> **Output:** `false` — NaN is never equal to anything, including itself

**Q20:** `console.log(Object.is(NaN, NaN))`
> **Output:** `true` — Object.is handles this correctly

**Q21:** `console.log(0 == "0")`
> **Output:** `true` — `"0"` → `0`

**Q22:** `console.log(0 == [])`
> **Output:** `true` — `[]` → `""` → `0`

**Q23:** `console.log("0" == [])`
> **Output:** `false` — `"0"` (string) vs `[]` (object), `[]` → `""`, `"0" !== ""`

**Q24:** `console.log(!!null + !!undefined + !!NaN)`
> **Output:** `0` — all three are falsy, `false + false + false` = `0 + 0 + 0 = 0`

**Q25 (Tricky):**

```javascript
console.log(1 < 2 < 3)   // ?
console.log(3 > 2 > 1)   // ?
```

> **Q25 Output:** `true` → `false`
> `1 < 2` = `true`, `true < 3` → `1 < 3` = `true` ✅
> `3 > 2` = `true`, `true > 1` → `1 > 1` = `false` ❌ (trap!)

## 3.4 Strings — Template Literals & Methods

<a id="34-strings--template-literals--methods"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Template Literals | Backtick syntax, interpolation, multiline |
| Tagged Templates | Advanced use — styled-components, GraphQL, i18n |
| String Immutability | Why strings can't be mutated |
| String Methods | All 25+ important methods with examples |

---

### Template Literals

```javascript
// Basic interpolation:
const name = "Rahul"
const age = 25

// Old way (string concatenation):
const old = "Hello, " + name + "! You are " + age + " years old."

// Template literal (modern, preferred):
const modern = `Hello, ${name}! You are ${age} years old.`

// Expressions inside ${}:
const result = `${2 + 3} is five`          // "5 is five"
const check = `${age >= 18 ? 'Adult' : 'Minor'}` // "Adult"
const fn = `${name.toUpperCase()}`          // "RAHUL"
const obj = `User: ${JSON.stringify({name, age})}` // "User: {"name":"Rahul","age":25}"

// Multiline strings:
const html = `
  <div class="card">
    <h1>${name}</h1>
    <p>Age: ${age}</p>
  </div>
`
// Preserves whitespace and newlines exactly as written

// Nested template literals:
const items = ['React', 'Next.js', 'TypeScript']
const list = `Skills:\n${items.map((item, i) => `  ${i + 1}. ${item}`).join('\n')}`
```

---

### Tagged Template Literals

**Tagged templates** allow you to parse template literals with a function — the function receives the string parts and interpolated values separately.

> **Hinglish:** Tagged templates ek advanced feature hai jisme template literal ko ek function ke through process karte hain. Styled-components CSS-in-JS mein yahi use hota hai.

```javascript
// Basic tagged template:
function highlight(strings, ...values) {
  // strings = array of string parts
  // values = array of interpolated values

  return strings.reduce((result, str, i) => {
    const value = values[i - 1]
    return result + (value !== undefined ? `<strong>${value}</strong>` : '') + str
  })
}

const name = "React"
const version = 18
const tagged = highlight`Learning ${name} version ${version} today!`
// "Learning <strong>React</strong> version <strong>18</strong> today!"

// Real-world usage — Styled Components:
const Button = styled.button`
  background: ${props => props.primary ? 'blue' : 'white'};
  padding: 0.5em 1em;
  border-radius: 4px;
`

// Real-world usage — GraphQL queries:
const query = gql`
  query GetUser($id: ID!) {
    user(id: $id) {
      name
      email
    }
  }
`

// SQL (sql-template-strings library):
const result = await db.query(sql`
  SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = ${userId} AND active = ${true}
`)
// Automatically handles SQL injection prevention!
```

---

### String Immutability

Strings in JavaScript are **immutable** — you cannot change individual characters.

```javascript
let str = "hello"
str[0] = "H"           // Silently fails (non-strict) / TypeError (strict)
console.log(str)       // "hello" — unchanged!

// String operations always return NEW strings:
const upper = str.toUpperCase()    // "HELLO" (new string)
const sliced = str.slice(0, 3)    // "hel" (new string)
console.log(str)                   // "hello" — original unchanged

// In React — this matters for state:
// ✅ Correct: replace entire string
setState(prevStr => prevStr + " world")

// String "mutations" just create new strings
```

---

### Complete String Methods Reference

```javascript
const str = "Hello, World! JavaScript is awesome"
const arr = ["React", "Next.js", "TypeScript"]

// ── LENGTH ──────────────────────────────────────────
str.length                     // 35

// ── ACCESS ──────────────────────────────────────────
str.charAt(0)                  // "H"
str[0]                         // "H" (same as charAt)
str.charCodeAt(0)              // 72 (Unicode code point)
str.at(0)                      // "H" (supports negative index)
str.at(-1)                     // "e" (last char) ✅ new in ES2022

// ── SEARCH ──────────────────────────────────────────
str.indexOf("o")               // 4 (first occurrence)
str.lastIndexOf("o")           // 20 (last occurrence)
str.indexOf("xyz")             // -1 (not found)
str.includes("World")          // true
str.includes("world")          // false (case-sensitive!)
str.startsWith("Hello")        // true
str.startsWith("World", 7)     // true (start search at index 7)
str.endsWith("awesome")        // true
str.endsWith("Java", 21)       // true (check first 21 chars)

// ── EXTRACT ──────────────────────────────────────────
str.slice(0, 5)                // "Hello"
str.slice(-7)                  // "awesome" (negative: from end)
str.slice(7, 12)               // "World"
str.substring(0, 5)            // "Hello" (like slice but no negatives)
// slice vs substring: slice supports negatives, substring doesn't

// ── CASE ────────────────────────────────────────────
str.toUpperCase()              // "HELLO, WORLD!..."
str.toLowerCase()              // "hello, world!..."

// ── TRIM ────────────────────────────────────────────
"  hello  ".trim()             // "hello"
"  hello  ".trimStart()        // "hello  " (removes leading whitespace)
"  hello  ".trimEnd()          // "  hello" (removes trailing whitespace)

// ── SPLIT & JOIN ─────────────────────────────────────
"a,b,c".split(",")             // ["a", "b", "c"]
"hello".split("")              // ["h", "e", "l", "l", "o"]
"hello".split("", 3)           // ["h", "e", "l"] (limit)
arr.join(" | ")                // "React | Next.js | TypeScript"

// ── REPLACE ──────────────────────────────────────────
str.replace("World", "React")  // "Hello, React! JavaScript..."
// Only replaces FIRST occurrence:
"aaa".replace("a", "b")        // "baa" ⚠️
"aaa".replaceAll("a", "b")    // "bbb" ✅ ES2021

// With regex:
str.replace(/javascript/i, "TypeScript")  // case-insensitive
str.replace(/o/g, "0")         // replace all 'o' with '0'

// ── REPEAT & PAD ─────────────────────────────────────
"ha".repeat(3)                 // "hahaha"
"5".padStart(4, "0")           // "0005" (pad to length 4)
"5".padEnd(4, "0")             // "5000"
"42".padStart(6, " ")          // "    42" (right-align with spaces)

// ── MATCH & REGEX ─────────────────────────────────────
const email = "test@example.com"
email.match(/@/)               // Match result object (first match)
"aabbcc".match(/[a-z]/g)      // ["a","a","b","b","c","c"] (all matches)
"test@example.com".matchAll(/[a-z]+/g)  // Iterator of all matches (ES2020)

// ── TEMPLATE PATTERNS ─────────────────────────────────
// Combining methods for common tasks:

// Truncate with ellipsis:
const truncate = (str, n) => str.length > n ? str.slice(0, n) + '...' : str
truncate("Hello World", 8)    // "Hello Wo..."

// Slugify a string:
const slugify = str =>
  str.toLowerCase().trim().replace(/\s+/g, '-').replace(/[^\w-]/g, '')
slugify("Hello World! React")  // "hello-world-react"

// Capitalize first letter:
const capitalize = str => str.charAt(0).toUpperCase() + str.slice(1)
capitalize("hello")            // "Hello"
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 3.4

**Q1: What is the difference between `slice()` and `substring()`?**

> Both extract a portion of a string. Key differences: (1) **Negative indices:** `slice()` supports negative indices (counts from end), `substring()` treats negatives as `0`. (2) **Argument swapping:** If `start > end`, `substring()` swaps them; `slice()` returns empty string. (3) Usage: `slice()` is generally preferred in modern code because of negative index support. Example: `"hello".slice(-3)` = `"llo"`, `"hello".substring(-3)` = `"hello"` (negative treated as 0).

**Q2: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log("hello".charAt(10))     // ?
console.log("hello"[10])            // ?
```

> `charAt(10)` returns `""` (empty string) for out-of-bounds. `"hello"[10]` returns `undefined`. This is a subtle difference between `charAt()` and bracket notation.

**Q3: What is a tagged template literal and where is it used in production?**

> A tagged template is a function call where the function receives the template literal's string parts and interpolated values as separate arguments. The function can process and return any value. Real-world uses: **styled-components** uses tagged templates (`styled.div\`color: red;\``) to write CSS-in-JS. **GraphQL clients** (Apollo, urql) use `gql\`query { user { name } }\`` for type-safe query parsing. **SQL libraries** use tagged templates to safely interpolate user data and prevent SQL injection.

## 3.5 Numbers & Math

<a id="35-numbers--math"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| IEEE 754 Double-Precision | Why JS numbers are floats internally |
| 0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3 | The famous floating-point problem |
| NaN & Infinity | Special numeric values and their behavior |
| Number methods | toFixed, toPrecision, parseInt, parseFloat |
| Math object | All important Math methods |

---

### IEEE 754 — How Numbers Are Stored

JavaScript uses **IEEE 754 double-precision floating-point** format for ALL numbers (integers and decimals). Numbers are stored in 64 bits: 1 sign bit, 11 exponent bits, 52 mantissa bits.

> **Hinglish:** JS mein har number 64-bit binary mein store hota hai — chahe woh integer ho ya decimal. Yahi wajah hai ki kuch decimal calculations exactly nahi hote.

```javascript
// Safe integer range:
Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER    // 9007199254740991 (2^53 - 1)
Number.MIN_SAFE_INTEGER    // -9007199254740991
Number.MAX_VALUE           // 1.7976931348623157e+308
Number.MIN_VALUE           // 5e-324 (smallest positive number)
Number.EPSILON             // 2.220446049250313e-16

// BigInt for numbers beyond safe range:
9007199254740991 + 1       // 9007199254740992 ✅
9007199254740991 + 2       // 9007199254740992 ⚠️ wrong! (precision lost)
9007199254740991n + 2n     // 9007199254740993n ✅ BigInt is safe
```

---

### 0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3 — The Famous Bug

```javascript
0.1 + 0.2                  // 0.30000000000000004
0.1 + 0.2 === 0.3          // false ❌

// Why? 0.1 in binary is 0.0001100110011... (repeating)
// Cannot be represented exactly in IEEE 754 binary format

// Solutions:
// 1. Round to fixed decimals:
(0.1 + 0.2).toFixed(1) === (0.3).toFixed(1)  // true ✅
parseFloat((0.1 + 0.2).toFixed(10)) === 0.3   // true ✅

// 2. Use Number.EPSILON comparison:
Math.abs(0.1 + 0.2 - 0.3) < Number.EPSILON   // true ✅

// 3. For financial calculations — use integers (cents):
const price = 0.10  // $0.10
const tax = 0.20    // $0.20
// ❌ Don't: price + tax = 0.30000000000000004
// ✅ Do: work in cents
const priceCents = 10
const taxCents = 20
const totalCents = priceCents + taxCents   // 30 (exact!)
const total = totalCents / 100             // $0.30

// 4. For production: use libraries like decimal.js or big.js
```

---

### NaN — Not a Number

```javascript
// NaN — result of invalid numeric operations:
0 / 0              // NaN
parseInt("abc")    // NaN
Math.sqrt(-1)      // NaN
undefined + 1      // NaN
"abc" - 1          // NaN

// NaN's unusual property: NOT equal to itself!
NaN === NaN        // false ⚠️
NaN == NaN         // false ⚠️

// Checking for NaN:
isNaN("hello")        // true  ⚠️ (converts to number first: isNaN(NaN) = true)
isNaN(undefined)      // true  ⚠️ (converts undefined to NaN first)
isNaN("123")          // false (converts "123" to 123 first)
Number.isNaN("hello") // false ✅ (no coercion — only true NaN)
Number.isNaN(NaN)     // true  ✅
Number.isNaN(undefined) // false ✅ (undefined is not NaN)

// isNaN vs Number.isNaN:
// isNaN: coerces value to number first, then checks
// Number.isNaN: no coercion, only returns true for actual NaN value

// NaN in operations always propagates:
NaN + 1     // NaN
NaN * 5     // NaN
NaN === NaN // false — always

// typeof NaN:
typeof NaN  // "number" ⚠️ (NaN is technically a "number" type!)
```

---

### Number Methods

```javascript
const num = 3.14159

// toFixed — rounds to N decimal places (returns string):
num.toFixed(2)        // "3.14"
num.toFixed(0)        // "3"
(1.005).toFixed(2)    // "1.00" ⚠️ (floating point rounding issue!)

// toPrecision — significant digits (returns string):
num.toPrecision(4)    // "3.142"
num.toPrecision(2)    // "3.1"

// parseInt — parse integer from string:
parseInt("42")        // 42
parseInt("42.9")      // 42 (truncates decimal)
parseInt("42px")      // 42 (stops at first non-numeric)
parseInt("abc")       // NaN
parseInt("0xFF", 16)  // 255 (hex base 16)
parseInt("111", 2)    // 7 (binary base 2)
parseInt("077", 8)    // 63 (octal base 8)

// parseFloat — parse decimal from string:
parseFloat("3.14")    // 3.14
parseFloat("3.14abc") // 3.14 (stops at first non-numeric)
parseFloat("abc")     // NaN

// Number.isInteger:
Number.isInteger(42)   // true
Number.isInteger(42.0) // true
Number.isInteger(42.5) // false

// Number.isFinite vs global isFinite:
Number.isFinite(Infinity)  // false ✅ (no coercion)
Number.isFinite("123")     // false ✅ (no coercion — string is not finite)
isFinite("123")            // true ⚠️ (coerces "123" to 123 first)
```

---

### Math Object — Complete Reference

```javascript
// Rounding:
Math.round(3.5)        // 4 (rounds to nearest integer, 0.5 rounds up)
Math.round(3.4)        // 3
Math.floor(3.9)        // 3 (rounds DOWN)
Math.ceil(3.1)         // 4 (rounds UP)
Math.trunc(3.9)        // 3 (removes decimal part)
Math.trunc(-3.9)       // -3 (Math.floor(-3.9) = -4, Math.trunc = -3)

// Min/Max:
Math.max(1, 5, 3, 9, 2)      // 9
Math.min(1, 5, 3, 9, 2)      // 1
Math.max(...[1, 5, 3, 9, 2]) // 9 (with spread for arrays)
Math.max()                    // -Infinity (no args)
Math.min()                    // Infinity (no args)

// Powers & Roots:
Math.pow(2, 10)        // 1024
2 ** 10                // 1024 (ES2016 exponentiation operator)
Math.sqrt(16)          // 4
Math.cbrt(27)          // 3 (cube root)
Math.abs(-42)          // 42

// Random:
Math.random()          // 0 to <1 (never reaches 1)
Math.floor(Math.random() * 10)     // 0-9 random integer
Math.floor(Math.random() * 100)    // 0-99 random integer

// Random integer between min and max (inclusive):
const randomInt = (min, max) =>
  Math.floor(Math.random() * (max - min + 1)) + min

randomInt(1, 6)   // dice roll: 1-6

// Logarithms:
Math.log(Math.E)   // 1 (natural log)
Math.log2(8)       // 3
Math.log10(1000)   // 3

// Trigonometry (in radians):
Math.PI            // 3.141592653589793
Math.sin(Math.PI / 2)  // 1
Math.cos(0)            // 1

// Sign:
Math.sign(42)      // 1 (positive)
Math.sign(-42)     // -1 (negative)
Math.sign(0)       // 0

// Hypotenuse (Pythagorean theorem):
Math.hypot(3, 4)   // 5 (sqrt(3² + 4²))
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 3.5

**Q1: Why does `0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3` in JavaScript?**

> JavaScript uses IEEE 754 double-precision floating-point format for all numbers. In binary representation, `0.1` is a repeating fraction (like `1/3` in decimal), so it cannot be stored exactly. Both `0.1` and `0.2` have small rounding errors in binary, and when added, the result is `0.30000000000000004`. The solution for financial calculations is to use integers (work in cents instead of dollars) or use libraries like `decimal.js`. For comparison, use `Math.abs(result - expected) < Number.EPSILON`.

**Q2: What is the difference between `isNaN()` and `Number.isNaN()`?**

> `isNaN()` (global) first **coerces** the argument to a number, then checks if it's NaN. This means `isNaN("hello")` returns `true` because `"hello"` coerces to `NaN`. `Number.isNaN()` performs **no coercion** — it only returns `true` if the value is literally the `NaN` value. `Number.isNaN("hello")` returns `false` because `"hello"` is a string, not NaN. In modern code, always use `Number.isNaN()` for accurate NaN checking.

**Q3: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3)
console.log(Math.round(1.005 * 100) / 100)
```

> `false` (0.1 + 0.2 = 0.30000000000000004) → `1` (not 1.01!)
> `1.005 * 100 = 100.49999999999999` due to floating-point, `Math.round(100.499...)` = `100`, divided by 100 = `1`. This shows why floating-point is dangerous for financial calculations.

## 3.6 Conditionals

<a id="36-conditionals"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| if/else, switch | Basic conditional flow and fall-through trap |
| Ternary operator | Concise single-expression conditionals |
| Short-circuit: `&&` `\|\|` | Lazy evaluation pattern |
| Optional chaining `?.` | Safe property access |
| Nullish coalescing `??` | Default values for null/undefined only |
| Logical assignment `\|\|=` `&&=` `??=` | Combine assignment with logic |

---

### if / else if / else

```javascript
// Basic:
const score = 75

if (score >= 90) {
  console.log("A grade")
} else if (score >= 80) {
  console.log("B grade")
} else if (score >= 70) {
  console.log("C grade")
} else {
  console.log("Below C")
}

// Truthy/falsy in conditions:
const user = null
if (user) {           // falsy check
  console.log(user.name)
}

// Guard clause pattern (preferred in modern code):
function processUser(user) {
  if (!user) return          // Early return for invalid input
  if (!user.isActive) return // Guard against inactive users
  // Main logic here — no deep nesting
  console.log(`Processing ${user.name}`)
}
```

---

### switch Statement — Fall-Through Trap

```javascript
const day = "Monday"

switch (day) {
  case "Monday":
  case "Tuesday":
  case "Wednesday":
  case "Thursday":
  case "Friday":
    console.log("Weekday")
    break                    // ← Must break! Without it, falls through
  case "Saturday":
  case "Sunday":
    console.log("Weekend")
    break
  default:
    console.log("Invalid day")
}

// ⚠️ Fall-through trap without break:
const val = 1
switch (val) {
  case 1:
    console.log("one")   // prints
    // NO break!
  case 2:
    console.log("two")   // also prints! (fall-through)
  case 3:
    console.log("three") // also prints!
    break
  case 4:
    console.log("four")  // doesn't print
}
// Output: "one", "two", "three" — all cases after case 1 run!

// switch uses strict equality (===):
switch ("1") {
  case 1:
    console.log("number 1")   // Doesn't match ("1" !== 1)
    break
  case "1":
    console.log("string '1'") // ✅ Matches
    break
}
```

---

### Ternary Operator

```javascript
// Syntax: condition ? valueIfTrue : valueIfFalse
const age = 20
const status = age >= 18 ? "Adult" : "Minor"  // "Adult"

// In JSX (React):
return (
  <div>
    {isLoggedIn ? <Dashboard /> : <Login />}
    <span>{count > 0 ? `${count} items` : "Empty cart"}</span>
  </div>
)

// Chained ternary (readable but use sparingly):
const grade = score >= 90 ? "A" :
              score >= 80 ? "B" :
              score >= 70 ? "C" : "F"

// ⚠️ Avoid:
// const result = a ? b ? c : d : e  // Hard to read — use if/else
```

---

### Short-Circuit Evaluation — `&&` and `||`

> **Hinglish:** Short-circuit ka matlab hai JS pehla condition dekh ke decide karta hai ki doosra evaluate karna hai ya nahi — agar pehla hi answer mil gaya, doosra nahi chalata.

```javascript
// && (AND): returns first FALSY value or last value if all truthy
false && console.log("never")   // short-circuits, console.log never runs
true && console.log("runs!")    // "runs!"

null && "hello"       // null    (returns first falsy)
undefined && "hello"  // undefined
0 && "hello"          // 0
"" && "hello"         // ""
1 && "hello"          // "hello" (all truthy, returns last)
1 && 2 && 3           // 3       (all truthy, returns last)

// || (OR): returns first TRUTHY value or last value if all falsy
false || "default"     // "default" (returns first truthy)
null || "fallback"     // "fallback"
0 || 100              // 100
"" || "empty string!" // "empty string!"
"hello" || "world"    // "hello" (first truthy)
false || 0 || null    // null  (all falsy, returns last)

// Practical patterns:
const user = null
const name = user && user.name     // null (safe access)
const port = process.env.PORT || 3000  // default value
const displayName = user.name || "Anonymous"

// ⚠️ || default value trap:
const count = 0
const display = count || "No items"  // "No items" ⚠️ (0 is falsy!)
// Better: use ?? for null/undefined only:
const display2 = count ?? "No items" // 0 ✅ (0 is not null/undefined)
```

---

### Optional Chaining — `?.`

**Optional chaining** (`?.`) safely accesses nested properties/methods — returns `undefined` instead of throwing an error if the chain breaks.

> **Hinglish:** Optional chaining ek safety check hai — agar beech mein koi `null` ya `undefined` mile, toh error nahi throw karta, sirf `undefined` return kar deta hai. Jaise "agar yeh road hai toh aage chalo, warna ruk jao."

```javascript
const user = {
  name: "Rahul",
  address: {
    city: "Mumbai",
    pincode: "400001"
  },
  getAge: () => 25
}

// Without optional chaining:
const city1 = user && user.address && user.address.city  // "Mumbai"

// With optional chaining (cleaner):
const city2 = user?.address?.city   // "Mumbai"
const country = user?.address?.country  // undefined (not error!)

// Method calls:
const age = user?.getAge?.()     // 25
const fn = user?.nonExistentMethod?.()  // undefined (no error)

// Array access:
const users = [{ name: "Rahul" }]
users?.[0]?.name    // "Rahul"
users?.[5]?.name    // undefined (no error)

// Dynamic property access:
const key = "name"
user?.[key]         // "Rahul"

// Real-world React:
function UserCard({ user }) {
  return (
    <div>
      <h1>{user?.profile?.displayName ?? "Anonymous"}</h1>
      <p>{user?.address?.city ?? "Location unknown"}</p>
      <p>{user?.stats?.followers?.toLocaleString() ?? "0"} followers</p>
    </div>
  )
}
```

---

### Nullish Coalescing — `??`

**Nullish coalescing** (`??`) returns the right-hand side only when the left-hand side is `null` or `undefined` (NOT for other falsy values like `0`, `""`, `false`).

> **Hinglish:** `??` sirf `null` ya `undefined` par fallback deta hai — `||` ke jaisa nahi jo `0` aur `""` par bhi fallback karta hai. Yahi fark hai.

```javascript
// ?? vs ||:
null ?? "default"        // "default" (null → use default)
undefined ?? "default"   // "default" (undefined → use default)
0 ?? "default"           // 0 ✅ (0 is NOT null/undefined)
"" ?? "default"          // "" ✅ (empty string is NOT null/undefined)
false ?? "default"       // false ✅ (false is NOT null/undefined)

// || comparison:
0 || "default"           // "default" ⚠️ (0 is falsy)
"" || "default"          // "default" ⚠️
false || "default"       // "default" ⚠️

// Real usage in React:
function VideoPlayer({ volume }) {
  // volume could legitimately be 0 (muted)
  const displayVolume = volume ?? 50  // Use 50 only if volume is null/undefined
  // NOT: volume || 50 — would show 50 when volume is 0!
}

// Combining with optional chaining:
const productName = product?.details?.name ?? "Unknown Product"
const price = product?.price?.amount ?? 0
```

---

### Logical Assignment Operators (ES2021)

```javascript
// ||= (OR assignment): assign only if left side is FALSY
let a = null
a ||= "default"    // a = "default" (null is falsy)
let b = "value"
b ||= "default"    // b = "value" (already truthy, no assignment)

// &&= (AND assignment): assign only if left side is TRUTHY
let user = { name: "Rahul" }
user &&= { ...user, role: "admin" }  // assigns (user is truthy)
let nothing = null
nothing &&= { role: "admin" }        // nothing = null (stays null)

// ??= (Nullish assignment): assign only if left side is NULL/UNDEFINED
let config = null
config ??= { theme: "dark" }  // config = { theme: "dark" }
let count = 0
count ??= 100                 // count = 0 ✅ (not null/undefined)

// Practical: setting defaults
function initUser(user) {
  user.role ??= "guest"          // set role only if not already set
  user.preferences ??= {}        // set preferences only if null/undefined
  user.loginCount ||= 0          // set loginCount if falsy (0, null, undefined)
  return user
}
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 3.6

**Q1: What is the difference between `||` and `??`?**

> `||` (OR) returns the right-hand value when the left is **any falsy value** (`false`, `0`, `""`, `null`, `undefined`, `NaN`). `??` (Nullish Coalescing) returns the right-hand value **only** when the left is `null` or `undefined`. Use `||` for "any falsy → use default." Use `??` for "only null/undefined → use default, preserve `0`/`false`/`""` as valid values." Example: `volume ?? 50` correctly handles `volume = 0` (muted), while `volume || 50` would wrongly default when volume is 0.

**Q2: What does `?.` return when a property doesn't exist?**

> Optional chaining (`?.`) returns `undefined` when any step in the chain evaluates to `null` or `undefined`, instead of throwing a `TypeError`. Example: `user?.address?.city` returns `undefined` if `user` is `null` or if `user.address` is `undefined`. Method calls: `user?.getProfile?.()` returns `undefined` if either `user` is nullish or `getProfile` doesn't exist on `user`. The `?.` operator short-circuits — the rest of the chain is not evaluated.

**Q3: What is the output?**

```javascript
console.log(null ?? undefined ?? "first" ?? "second")
console.log(null || undefined || "first" || "second")
console.log(0 ?? "default")
console.log(0 || "default")
```

> `"first"` → `"first"` → `0` → `"default"`
> First: `??` skips null and undefined, returns first non-nullish: `"first"`.
> Second: `||` skips falsy null and undefined, returns first truthy: `"first"`.
> Third: `??` — `0` is NOT null/undefined, returns `0`.
> Fourth: `||` — `0` is falsy, returns `"default"`.

## 3.7 Loops

<a id="37-loops"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| `for` loop | Classic counter-based iteration |
| `while` loop | Condition-based iteration |
| `do...while` | Runs at least once |
| `for...of` | Iterating iterables (arrays, strings, Map, Set) |
| `for...in` | Iterating object keys (with cautions) |
| forEach vs for...of | Differences and when to use each |
| break, continue, labels | Control flow inside loops |

---

### Loop Comparison Table

| Loop | Use Case | Can Break? | Async Friendly? | Returns Value? |
|------|----------|-----------|-----------------|----------------|
| `for` | Known iteration count | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| `while` | Unknown count, condition-based | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| `do...while` | Run at least once | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| `for...of` | Iterables (array, string, Set, Map) | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| `for...in` | Object enumerable keys | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| `forEach` | Arrays only, no break | ❌ | ❌ (await doesn't work) | `undefined` |
| `map` | Transform array | ❌ | ❌ | New array |
| `filter` | Filter array | ❌ | ❌ | New array |

---

### `for` Loop

```javascript
// Classic for loop:
for (let i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
  console.log(i)  // 0, 1, 2, 3, 4
}

// Reverse iteration:
for (let i = 4; i >= 0; i--) {
  console.log(i)  // 4, 3, 2, 1, 0
}

// Skip by 2:
for (let i = 0; i < 10; i += 2) {
  console.log(i)  // 0, 2, 4, 6, 8
}

// Iterating array with index:
const fruits = ["apple", "banana", "cherry"]
for (let i = 0; i < fruits.length; i++) {
  console.log(`${i}: ${fruits[i]}`)
}

// ⚠️ Performance: cache array length if array is large
for (let i = 0, len = fruits.length; i < len; i++) {
  // fruits.length not re-evaluated each iteration
}
```

---

### `for...of` — The Modern Way to Iterate

`for...of` iterates over any **iterable** (anything with `[Symbol.iterator]`): arrays, strings, Set, Map, NodeList, arguments, generators.

> **Hinglish:** `for...of` modern tarika hai values nikalne ka — array ke elements, string ke characters, Map ke entries sab pe kaam karta hai. Index nahi milta by default, par values milti hain directly.

```javascript
// Arrays:
const nums = [10, 20, 30]
for (const num of nums) {
  console.log(num)  // 10, 20, 30
}

// With index (using entries()):
for (const [index, value] of nums.entries()) {
  console.log(`${index}: ${value}`)  // "0: 10", "1: 20", "2: 30"
}

// Strings (iterates characters):
for (const char of "hello") {
  console.log(char)  // h, e, l, l, o
}

// Map:
const map = new Map([["a", 1], ["b", 2]])
for (const [key, value] of map) {
  console.log(`${key} → ${value}`)  // "a → 1", "b → 2"
}

// Set:
const set = new Set([1, 2, 3, 2, 1])  // {1, 2, 3}
for (const value of set) {
  console.log(value)  // 1, 2, 3 (unique values only)
}

// ✅ for...of supports await (async-friendly):
async function processItems(items) {
  for (const item of items) {
    await processItem(item)  // ✅ Works! Awaits each iteration
  }
}
```

---

### `for...in` — Object Key Iteration (Use with Caution!)

```javascript
const person = { name: "Rahul", age: 25, city: "Mumbai" }

for (const key in person) {
  console.log(`${key}: ${person[key]}`)
  // "name: Rahul", "age: 25", "city: Mumbai"
}

// ⚠️ DANGER: for...in iterates INHERITED properties too!
function Animal(name) { this.name = name }
Animal.prototype.sound = "generic"

const dog = new Animal("Rex")
dog.breed = "Labrador"

for (const key in dog) {
  console.log(key)  // "name", "breed", "sound" ⚠️ (inherited!)
}

// Safe pattern — use hasOwnProperty:
for (const key in dog) {
  if (Object.hasOwn(dog, key)) {  // or dog.hasOwnProperty(key)
    console.log(key)  // "name", "breed" (own properties only)
  }
}

// ⚠️ Don't use for...in on arrays!
const arr = [10, 20, 30]
for (const index in arr) {
  console.log(index)  // "0", "1", "2" (strings! not numbers!)
  // Also includes any enumerable properties added to Array.prototype
}
// Use for...of for arrays instead.

// When to use for...in:
// ✅ Iterating plain object keys (with hasOwnProperty check)
// ✅ Debugging/logging object properties
// ❌ Not for arrays
// ❌ Not when prototype pollution is a concern
```

---

### `forEach` vs `for...of` — Key Differences

```javascript
const arr = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]

// forEach — cannot break, cannot use await properly:
arr.forEach(num => {
  if (num === 3) return  // ⚠️ This 'return' only exits the callback, NOT the loop!
  console.log(num)       // 1, 2, 4, 5 (3 is skipped but loop continues)
})
// There's NO way to break out of forEach early!

// for...of — can break, await works:
for (const num of arr) {
  if (num === 3) break   // ✅ Actually breaks the loop
  console.log(num)       // 1, 2 (stops at 3)
}

// async with forEach (WRONG pattern!):
async function wrongAsync(arr) {
  arr.forEach(async (item) => {
    await fetchData(item)  // ⚠️ forEach doesn't wait! All run concurrently
  })
  console.log("done?")  // Prints BEFORE all fetches complete!
}

// async with for...of (CORRECT):
async function correctAsync(arr) {
  for (const item of arr) {
    await fetchData(item)  // ✅ Waits for each fetch before continuing
  }
  console.log("done!")   // Prints AFTER all fetches complete
}
```

---

### `break`, `continue`, and Labels

```javascript
// break — exit loop immediately:
for (let i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
  if (i === 5) break
  console.log(i)  // 0, 1, 2, 3, 4
}

// continue — skip current iteration:
for (let i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
  if (i % 2 === 0) continue  // skip even numbers
  console.log(i)  // 1, 3, 5, 7, 9
}

// Labels — for breaking/continuing outer loops:
outer: for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  inner: for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
    if (i === 1 && j === 1) break outer   // breaks both loops!
    console.log(i, j)
  }
}
// Output: 0,0  0,1  0,2  1,0  (stops when i=1, j=1)

// Labels with continue:
outer: for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
    if (j === 1) continue outer  // skips to next i iteration
    console.log(i, j)
  }
}
// Output: 0,0  1,0  2,0
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 3.7

**Q1: What is the difference between `for...in` and `for...of`?**

> `for...in` iterates over **enumerable property keys** of an object (including inherited prototype properties). It returns string keys and should primarily be used with plain objects (with `hasOwnProperty` checks). `for...of` iterates over **values** of any **iterable** (arrays, strings, Set, Map, generators). It does not work with plain objects (they're not iterable by default). `for...of` is preferred for arrays and collections; `for...in` for object keys.

**Q2: Why doesn't `break` work inside `forEach`?**

> `forEach` calls a callback function for each element. `break` is a statement that exits the current loop — but inside `forEach`'s callback, there's no loop to break; you're inside a regular function. Using `return` inside the callback only exits that callback invocation, not the `forEach` loop. There's no way to prematurely exit `forEach`. Solutions: use a `for...of` loop (supports `break`), use `Array.prototype.some()` (returns `true` to stop), or use `find()` if you're searching.

**Q3: How would you implement sequential async operations in a loop?**

> Use `for...of` with `await` — it processes each item one at a time, waiting for each async operation to complete before moving to the next. `forEach` does NOT work with `await` properly — all async operations start concurrently without waiting. For parallel async operations (all at once), use `Promise.all(arr.map(async item => await processItem(item)))`.

## 3.8 Functions — Four Types

<a id="38-functions--four-types"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Function Declaration | Syntax, full hoisting, own `this` |
| Function Expression | Not hoisted, var/let/const binding |
| Arrow Function | Lexical `this`, no `arguments`, concise body |
| IIFE | Immediately invoked, private scope pattern |
| When NOT to use Arrow Functions | Methods, constructors, event handlers needing `this` |

---

### The Four Types — Quick Comparison

| Feature | Declaration | Expression | Arrow | IIFE |
|---------|------------|-----------|-------|------|
| **Syntax** | `function f(){}` | `const f = function(){}` | `const f = () => {}` | `(function(){})()` |
| **Hoisted?** | ✅ Fully | ❌ (var: undefined) | ❌ | ❌ |
| **Own `this`?** | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes | ❌ Lexical | ✅ Yes |
| **`arguments` object?** | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes | ❌ No | ✅ Yes |
| **Can be constructor?** | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes | ❌ No | ❌ No |
| **Named in stack trace?** | ✅ Yes | If named | No (unless named) | No |

---

### Function Declaration

```javascript
// Hoisted fully — can call BEFORE declaration:
greet("Rahul")  // ✅ "Hello, Rahul!" (hoisting)

function greet(name) {
  console.log(`Hello, ${name}!`)
  return `Hello, ${name}!`
}

// Has own 'this' — depends on call site:
function showThis() {
  console.log(this)
}
showThis()               // window (browser) / global (Node) / undefined (strict)
const obj = { showThis }
obj.showThis()           // { showThis: [Function] } (obj is the caller)

// Recursive functions — can call themselves by name:
function factorial(n) {
  if (n <= 1) return 1
  return n * factorial(n - 1)  // calls itself
}
```

---

### Function Expression

```javascript
// NOT hoisted (only the variable is hoisted, not the function):
// greetExpr("Rahul")  // ❌ TypeError: greetExpr is not a function (var)
//                        ❌ ReferenceError (let/const)

const greetExpr = function(name) {
  return `Hello, ${name}!`
}

greetExpr("Rahul")  // ✅ Works after declaration

// Named function expression (name only accessible inside):
const factorial = function fact(n) {
  if (n <= 1) return 1
  return n * fact(n - 1)  // 'fact' accessible inside only
}
factorial(5)   // ✅
// fact(5)     // ❌ ReferenceError outside

// As callback:
setTimeout(function() {
  console.log("timeout")
}, 1000)

// Assigned to object property:
const calculator = {
  add: function(a, b) { return a + b },
  subtract: function(a, b) { return a - b }
}
```

---

### Arrow Functions — Lexical `this` Deep Dive

```javascript
// Arrow function syntax:
const add = (a, b) => a + b           // Single expression — implicit return
const square = x => x * x             // Single param — no parens needed
const getObj = () => ({ key: "val" }) // Return object — wrap in parens!
const greet = () => {
  const msg = "Hello!"
  return msg                          // Multiple lines — need explicit return
}

// ⚡ KEY DIFFERENCE: Lexical 'this'
// Arrow functions don't have their own 'this'
// They inherit 'this' from the SURROUNDING lexical context

// ❌ Regular function 'this' (dynamic binding):
const timer1 = {
  seconds: 0,
  start: function() {
    setInterval(function() {
      this.seconds++           // ❌ 'this' is undefined (or window in non-strict)
      // 'this' inside setInterval callback refers to global/undefined
    }, 1000)
  }
}

// ✅ Arrow function 'this' (lexical binding):
const timer2 = {
  seconds: 0,
  start: function() {
    setInterval(() => {
      this.seconds++  // ✅ 'this' refers to timer2 (inherited from start method)
    }, 1000)
  }
}

// Arrow functions have NO 'arguments' object:
function regular() {
  console.log(arguments)   // ✅ Arguments object
}

const arrow = () => {
  console.log(arguments)   // ❌ ReferenceError (or captures outer arguments)
}

// Use rest params instead:
const arrowWithRest = (...args) => {
  console.log(args)        // ✅ Array of arguments
}
```

---

### When NOT to Use Arrow Functions

> **Hinglish:** Arrow functions sabke liye nahi hain — teeno jagah mat use karo: object methods (jahan `this` chahiye), constructor functions, aur event listeners jahan `this` = element chahiye.

```javascript
// ❌ 1. Object methods (where 'this' should refer to the object):
const user = {
  name: "Rahul",
  greet: () => {
    console.log(`Hello, ${this.name}`)  // ❌ 'this' is not user!
    // 'this' is the enclosing scope (window or undefined)
  }
}
user.greet()  // "Hello, undefined"

// ✅ Use regular function for object methods:
const user2 = {
  name: "Rahul",
  greet: function() {
    console.log(`Hello, ${this.name}`)  // ✅ 'this' is user2
  }
}
// Or method shorthand:
const user3 = {
  name: "Rahul",
  greet() {
    console.log(`Hello, ${this.name}`)  // ✅
  }
}

// ❌ 2. Constructor functions:
const Person = (name) => {
  this.name = name  // ❌ Arrow functions can't be constructors
}
new Person("Rahul")  // ❌ TypeError: Person is not a constructor

// ✅ Use regular function or class:
function Person(name) {
  this.name = name
}
const p = new Person("Rahul")  // ✅

// ❌ 3. Event handlers needing 'this' to be the element:
document.querySelector(".btn").addEventListener("click", () => {
  this.classList.toggle("active")  // ❌ 'this' is not the button
})

// ✅ Use regular function:
document.querySelector(".btn").addEventListener("click", function() {
  this.classList.toggle("active")  // ✅ 'this' is the button element
})

// ❌ 4. prototype methods:
function Animal(name) { this.name = name }
Animal.prototype.speak = () => {
  console.log(`${this.name} speaks`)  // ❌ 'this' is not the instance
}

// ✅ Regular function:
Animal.prototype.speak = function() {
  console.log(`${this.name} speaks`)  // ✅
}
```

---

### IIFE — Immediately Invoked Function Expression

```javascript
// IIFE pattern — runs immediately, creates private scope:
(function() {
  var privateVar = "I'm private"
  console.log("IIFE executed!")
  console.log(privateVar)
})()

// Arrow IIFE:
(() => {
  console.log("Arrow IIFE!")
})()

// With parameters:
(function(name) {
  console.log(`Hello, ${name}!`)
})("Rahul")  // "Hello, Rahul!"

// ✅ Use cases:
// 1. Avoid polluting global scope (legacy pattern before modules)
// 2. Create private variables/closures
// 3. Initialize code that runs once

// Module pattern with IIFE:
const Counter = (function() {
  let count = 0           // Private — not accessible outside
  return {
    increment: () => ++count,
    decrement: () => --count,
    getCount: () => count
  }
})()

Counter.increment()  // 1
Counter.increment()  // 2
Counter.getCount()   // 2
// Counter.count     // ❌ undefined — count is private

// Modern alternative: ES Modules (preferred over IIFE today)
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 3.8

**Q1: What is the difference between a function declaration and a function expression?**

> **Function declaration:** `function name() {}` — hoisted completely (both name and body), available before its definition in the code. **Function expression:** `const name = function() {}` — only the variable is hoisted (as `undefined` for var, TDZ for let/const), not the function body. Cannot be called before the line where it's assigned. Both have their own `this` binding (dynamic). Function declarations are better for utility functions you want to use anywhere in a file; expressions are better for callbacks, conditional function creation, and avoiding accidental use before initialization.

**Q2: What is the difference between arrow functions and regular functions?**

> Key differences: (1) **`this` binding:** Arrow functions use **lexical `this`** — they inherit `this` from the surrounding scope where they were defined. Regular functions have their own **dynamic `this`** — determined by how the function is called. (2) **`arguments` object:** Arrow functions don't have one; use rest params instead. (3) **Constructors:** Arrow functions cannot be used with `new`. (4) **Hoisting:** Arrow functions are not hoisted (like function expressions). (5) **`prototype`:** Arrow functions have no `prototype` property.

**Q3: When should you NOT use an arrow function?**

> Avoid arrow functions for: (1) **Object methods** — if the method needs `this` to refer to the object, arrow functions will capture the outer `this` instead. (2) **Constructor functions** — arrow functions cannot be used with `new`. (3) **Event handlers** where `this` should be the DOM element. (4) **Prototype methods** — `this` won't refer to the instance. (5) **`arguments` object** — if you need the arguments object (use rest params as alternative). Use arrow functions for: callbacks, array methods (map, filter, reduce), anything where you want to preserve the outer `this`.

**Q4: What is the output?**

```javascript
const obj = {
  x: 10,
  regular: function() { return this.x },
  arrow: () => this.x
}

console.log(obj.regular())  // ?
console.log(obj.arrow())    // ?
```

> `10` → `undefined` (in strict mode) or `window.x` (browser non-strict)
> `regular()` has dynamic `this` — called on `obj`, so `this = obj`, `this.x = 10`. `arrow()` has lexical `this` — inherited from where `obj` was defined (global scope), where `this.x` is `undefined`.

## 3.9 Parameters & Arguments

<a id="39-parameters--arguments"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Parameters vs Arguments | Definition-time vs Call-time values |
| Default Parameters | ES6 default values, evaluation order |
| Rest Parameters (`...args`) | Collect remaining args into array |
| `arguments` Object | Legacy, only in regular functions |
| Spread in Calls (`...arr`) | Expand array into individual arguments |

---

### Parameters vs Arguments

> **Hinglish:** Parameters woh names hain jo function define karte waqt likhte hain (definition time). Arguments woh actual values hain jo function call karte waqt dete hain (call time).

```javascript
// Parameters: a, b (definition time names):
function add(a, b) {
  return a + b
}

// Arguments: 5, 10 (call time values):
add(5, 10)  // a=5, b=10

// More args than params — extras ignored:
add(1, 2, 3, 4)  // a=1, b=2, 3 and 4 are ignored

// Fewer args than params — missing = undefined:
add(5)       // a=5, b=undefined → 5 + undefined = NaN
add()        // a=undefined, b=undefined → NaN
```

---

### Default Parameters

```javascript
// ES6 default parameters:
function greet(name = "Guest", greeting = "Hello") {
  return `${greeting}, ${name}!`
}
greet()                     // "Hello, Guest!" (both defaults)
greet("Rahul")              // "Hello, Rahul!" (greeting defaults)
greet("Rahul", "Namaste")   // "Namaste, Rahul!" (no defaults)
greet(undefined, "Hi")      // "Hi, Guest!" (undefined triggers default)
greet(null, "Hi")           // "Hi, null!" (null does NOT trigger default!)

// ⚠️ Only undefined triggers defaults, NOT null or false or 0:
function test(x = 10) {
  console.log(x)
}
test(undefined)  // 10 (uses default)
test(null)       // null (null overrides default!)
test(false)      // false
test(0)          // 0

// Defaults can use previous parameters:
function range(start, end = start, step = 1) {
  // end defaults to start if not provided
  // step defaults to 1
}

// Defaults can be function calls (evaluated at call time!):
function getDefault() { return Date.now() }
function stamp(ts = getDefault()) {
  return ts  // Different value each call if ts not provided
}

// Defaults can reference earlier params:
function multiply(a, b = a * 2) {
  return a + b
}
multiply(5)    // 5 + 10 = 15
multiply(5, 3) // 5 + 3 = 8
```

---

### Rest Parameters (`...args`)

```javascript
// Rest collects remaining arguments into an array:
function sum(...numbers) {
  return numbers.reduce((acc, n) => acc + n, 0)
}
sum(1, 2, 3)       // 6
sum(1, 2, 3, 4, 5) // 15

// Mix with regular params — rest MUST be last:
function log(level, timestamp, ...messages) {
  console.log(`[${level}] ${timestamp}:`, ...messages)
}
log("INFO", "10:00", "Server", "started", "on port 3000")
// [INFO] 10:00: Server started on port 3000

// ❌ Rest must be last param:
function wrong(a, ...rest, b) {}  // SyntaxError: Rest element must be last

// Rest vs arguments object:
function withRest(...args) {
  console.log(Array.isArray(args))   // true ✅
  console.log(args)                  // actual Array
}

function withArguments() {
  console.log(Array.isArray(arguments))  // false ⚠️
  console.log(arguments)                 // Arguments object (array-like)
  // Need to convert: Array.from(arguments) or [...arguments]
}
```

---

### `arguments` Object (Legacy)

```javascript
// arguments — only in regular functions (NOT arrows):
function legacy() {
  console.log(arguments)          // Arguments { 0: 1, 1: 2, 2: 3, length: 3 }
  console.log(arguments[0])       // 1
  console.log(arguments.length)   // 3

  // Convert to array:
  const arr = Array.from(arguments)
  const arr2 = [...arguments]

  // ⚠️ Cannot use array methods directly:
  // arguments.map(...)  // ❌ TypeError: not a function
  Array.from(arguments).map(...)  // ✅
}
legacy(1, 2, 3)

// Arrow functions — no arguments:
const arrow = () => {
  console.log(arguments)  // ❌ ReferenceError (or outer scope arguments)
}

// Modern practice: always prefer rest params over arguments
function modern(...args) {
  return args.reduce((sum, n) => sum + n, 0)  // ✅ args is a real array
}
```

---

### Spread in Function Calls

```javascript
// Spread expands array into individual arguments:
const nums = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2, 6]

Math.max(nums)        // NaN ❌ (Math.max doesn't accept an array)
Math.max(...nums)     // 9 ✅ (spread expands: Math.max(3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2, 6))
Math.min(...nums)     // 1 ✅

// Combining arrays:
const arr1 = [1, 2, 3]
const arr2 = [4, 5, 6]
const combined = [...arr1, ...arr2]  // [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]

// Cloning (shallow copy):
const original = [1, [2, 3], 4]
const clone = [...original]   // [1, [2, 3], 4] (shallow!)
clone[0] = 99
clone[1][0] = 99
console.log(original[0])     // 1 (not affected — primitive)
console.log(original[1][0])  // 99 ⚠️ (affected — nested array reference!)

// Object spread:
const obj1 = { a: 1, b: 2 }
const obj2 = { b: 3, c: 4 }
const merged = { ...obj1, ...obj2 }  // { a: 1, b: 3, c: 4 } (b overwritten)

// Real-world React pattern:
function updateState(prevState, updates) {
  return { ...prevState, ...updates }  // Immutable update pattern
}
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 3.9

**Q1: What is the difference between rest parameters and the `arguments` object?**

> **Rest parameters** (`...args`) collect remaining function arguments into a **true Array** — array methods like `map`, `filter`, `reduce` work directly. They can be combined with other named parameters (but must be last). They work in both regular and arrow functions. The **`arguments` object** is an array-like object (not an Array) available only in regular functions (not arrows). It contains ALL arguments (not just remaining ones), requires conversion to use array methods (`Array.from(arguments)`), and has legacy quirks. Modern code should always prefer rest parameters over `arguments`.

**Q2: What is the difference between spread and rest?**

> Both use `...` syntax but in different contexts. **Rest** (`...params`) is used in **function parameter definitions** — it collects multiple arguments into a single array. **Spread** (`...value`) is used in **function calls, array literals, and object literals** — it expands an iterable into individual elements. Same syntax, opposite directions: rest collects (many → one), spread expands (one → many).

**Q3: What is the output?**

```javascript
function test(a, b = a * 2, c = a + b) {
  return [a, b, c]
}
console.log(test(3))
console.log(test(3, 5))
```

> `[3, 6, 9]` → `[3, 5, 8]`
> First call: `a=3`, `b=3*2=6`, `c=3+6=9`. Second call: `a=3`, `b=5` (provided), `c=3+5=8`.

[Back to Top 🔝](#table-of-contents3)

---

## 3.10 Callback Functions

<a id="310-callback-functions"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| What is a Callback? | Definition and purpose |
| Synchronous Callbacks | forEach, map, filter, sort |
| Asynchronous Callbacks | setTimeout, event listeners, fs.readFile |
| Callback Hell | Pyramid of doom problem |
| Inversion of Control | Why callback hell exists deeper than nesting |

---

### What is a Callback Function?

A **callback function** is a function passed as an **argument** to another function, to be **called later** (either synchronously or asynchronously).

> **Hinglish:** Callback ek function hai jo aap kisi aur function ko pass karte ho — woh function decide karta hai kab aapka callback chalana hai. Jaise restaurant mein "tumhara order ready ho jaaye toh mujhe call karna" — yahi callback pattern hai.

```javascript
// Basic callback pattern:
function doSomething(value, callback) {
  const result = value * 2
  callback(result)    // calling the callback with result
}

doSomething(5, function(result) {
  console.log("Result:", result)  // "Result: 10"
})

// Arrow function callback:
doSomething(5, result => console.log("Result:", result))
```

---

### Synchronous Callbacks

```javascript
// Array methods use synchronous callbacks:
const numbers = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]

// forEach — callback called synchronously for each element:
numbers.forEach(n => console.log(n * 2))
console.log("after forEach")  // Prints AFTER forEach is done

// map — transform each element:
const doubled = numbers.map(n => n * 2)  // [2, 4, 6, 8, 10]

// filter — keep elements where callback returns true:
const evens = numbers.filter(n => n % 2 === 0)  // [2, 4]

// sort — sort with comparison callback:
const sorted = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9].sort((a, b) => a - b)  // ascending
// a - b: negative = a first, 0 = equal, positive = b first

// reduce — accumulate:
const sum = numbers.reduce((acc, n) => acc + n, 0)  // 15

// Custom synchronous callback:
function runWithLogging(fn, ...args) {
  console.log("Before:", fn.name)
  const result = fn(...args)
  console.log("After:", fn.name)
  return result
}

runWithLogging(Math.max, 1, 5, 3)
// "Before: max" → "After: max" → 5
```

---

### Asynchronous Callbacks

```javascript
// setTimeout — callback runs after delay:
setTimeout(function() {
  console.log("2 seconds later")
}, 2000)
console.log("synchronous code")  // Runs first!

// Event listeners:
document.querySelector("#btn").addEventListener("click", function(event) {
  console.log("Clicked!", event.target)  // Callback runs when clicked
})

// Node.js fs:
const fs = require("fs")
fs.readFile("./data.txt", "utf8", function(error, data) {
  if (error) {
    console.error("Read failed:", error)
    return
  }
  console.log("File contents:", data)
})
console.log("Reading file...")  // Runs before callback
```

---

### Callback Hell — Pyramid of Doom

```javascript
// ❌ Callback hell — deeply nested, hard to read and maintain:
getUser(userId, function(error, user) {
  if (error) return handleError(error)

  getUserPosts(user.id, function(error, posts) {
    if (error) return handleError(error)

    getPostComments(posts[0].id, function(error, comments) {
      if (error) return handleError(error)

      getCommentLikes(comments[0].id, function(error, likes) {
        if (error) return handleError(error)

        getAuthorProfile(likes[0].userId, function(error, profile) {
          if (error) return handleError(error)
          // 5 levels deep! "Pyramid of Doom"
          console.log(profile)
        })
      })
    })
  })
})
```

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A["getUser(userId, callback)"] --> B["callback(user)"]
    B --> C["getUserPosts(user.id, callback)"]
    C --> D["callback(posts)"]
    D --> E["getPostComments(posts[0].id, callback)"]
    E --> F["callback(comments)"]
    F --> G["getCommentLikes(comments[0].id, callback)"]
    G --> H["callback(likes)"]

    style A fill:#ffcdd2
    style C fill:#ffcdd2
    style E fill:#ffcdd2
    style G fill:#ffcdd2

    NOTE["❌ Callback Hell Problems:\n1. Deeply nested, hard to read\n2. Error handling repeated at each level\n3. Hard to debug\n4. Cannot use return/break\n5. Inversion of Control issue"]
```

---

### Inversion of Control — The Deeper Problem

> **Hinglish:** Inversion of Control = aap control dusre ko de dete ho. Jab aap callback pass karte ho, toh doosra function decide karta hai kab, kitni baar aur kaise aapka callback chalega — yeh problem hai.

```javascript
// ⚠️ Inversion of Control problem:
function processPayment(amount, onSuccess) {
  // We're trusting 'processPayment' to:
  // 1. Call onSuccess exactly ONCE
  // 2. Call it at the right time
  // 3. Not call it with wrong arguments
  // 4. Not fail silently
  // We have NO control over this!
}

// What if processPayment calls onSuccess twice? (double charge!)
// What if it never calls onSuccess? (payment stuck!)
// What if it calls with wrong arguments?

// Solution 1: Promises (you control .then())
processPayment(amount)
  .then(result => handleSuccess(result))  // You decide what to do with result
  .catch(err => handleError(err))

// Solution 2: async/await (most readable):
async function handlePayment(amount) {
  try {
    const result = await processPayment(amount)
    handleSuccess(result)
  } catch (err) {
    handleError(err)
  }
}
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 3.10

**Q1: What is a callback function? What is the difference between synchronous and asynchronous callbacks?**

> A callback is a function passed as an argument to another function, to be invoked later. **Synchronous callbacks** are called immediately within the same event loop tick — array methods like `forEach`, `map`, `filter`, and `sort` use synchronous callbacks; they complete before the next line runs. **Asynchronous callbacks** are called later — after some async operation completes, registered in the callback queue. `setTimeout`, event listeners, and Node.js I/O callbacks are asynchronous.

**Q2: What is callback hell and how do you solve it?**

> Callback hell (pyramid of doom) is deeply nested callback code that results from chaining multiple async operations. Problems: hard to read/maintain, error handling must be repeated at every level, hard to debug, no early return. Solutions: (1) **Named functions** — extract callbacks into named functions, flatten the nesting. (2) **Promises** — chain `.then()` calls instead of nesting. (3) **async/await** — write async code in a linear, synchronous-looking style. (4) **Modularization** — split complex async flows into smaller functions.

**Q3: What is Inversion of Control in the context of callbacks?**

> Inversion of Control (IoC) means giving control of your code's execution to a third-party function. When you pass a callback, you trust that the receiving function will call your callback correctly — the right number of times, at the right time, with correct arguments. If the function is third-party code, you cannot guarantee this. Promises address IoC because your `.then()` handler is controlled by the Promise spec, not arbitrary third-party code — it's guaranteed to be called once, after the promise resolves, with the resolved value.
