## 3.11 Strict Mode

<a id="311-strict-mode"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| What is Strict Mode? | Purpose and how to enable |
| What It Prevents | All restrictions strict mode enforces |
| `this` in Strict Mode | Global `this` is `undefined` |
| ES6 Modules & Classes | Always strict mode |
| React & Strict Mode | React.StrictMode component |

---

### What is Strict Mode?

**Strict mode** is a way to opt-in to a restricted variant of JavaScript that catches common programming mistakes and prevents the use of certain dangerous features. Introduced in ES5.

> **Hinglish:** Strict mode JavaScript ka "strict teacher" mode hai — jo galat cheezein allow karta tha woh ab error throw karta hai. Code better aur safer ban jaata hai.

```javascript
// Enable for entire file (top of file):
"use strict"

// or enable for just one function:
function strictFunction() {
  "use strict"
  // strict mode only inside this function
}

// Note: "use strict" only works at the TOP of a file or function
// It's a pragma (string literal), not a statement
```

---

### What Strict Mode Prevents — Complete List

```javascript
"use strict"

// 1. Undeclared variables — creates global accidentally:
x = 10              // ❌ ReferenceError: x is not defined
// Without strict: creates window.x — pollutes global scope

// 2. Duplicate parameter names:
function test(a, a) {}  // ❌ SyntaxError: Duplicate parameter name not allowed
// Without strict: silently works (second 'a' wins)

// 3. Deleting undeletable properties:
delete Object.prototype  // ❌ TypeError
// Without strict: silently fails

// 4. Writing to read-only properties:
const obj = {}
Object.defineProperty(obj, "fixed", { value: 42, writable: false })
obj.fixed = 100     // ❌ TypeError: Cannot assign to read only property
// Without strict: silently fails

// 5. Using reserved future keywords:
var let = 5         // ❌ SyntaxError (let is reserved)
var static = true   // ❌ SyntaxError

// 6. 'this' in global function calls = undefined (not window):
function test() {
  console.log(this)  // undefined in strict mode, window in non-strict
}
test()

// 7. with statement not allowed:
with (Math) {        // ❌ SyntaxError: Strict mode code may not include a with statement
  console.log(PI)    // 3.14...
}

// 8. arguments object doesn't track parameter changes:
function test(a) {
  "use strict"
  a = 10
  console.log(arguments[0])  // Still original value (not 10)
  // Without strict: arguments[0] changes when 'a' changes
}

// 9. eval has its own scope:
eval("var sneaky = 1")
console.log(sneaky)  // ❌ ReferenceError in strict mode
// Without strict: sneaky leaks to outer scope

// 10. Octal literals not allowed:
var oct = 0777     // ❌ SyntaxError
var oct2 = 0o777   // ✅ ES6 octal syntax (allowed even in strict)
```

---

### Strict Mode and `this`

```javascript
// Non-strict: global function this = window/global
function noStrict() {
  console.log(this === window)  // true (browser)
}

// Strict: global function this = undefined
function withStrict() {
  "use strict"
  console.log(this === undefined)  // true
}

// This matters for class methods called without object context:
class MyClass {
  // Classes are automatically in strict mode
  greet() {
    console.log(this)  // undefined if called without context
  }
}

const g = new MyClass()
g.greet()  // MyClass instance (context: g)

const fn = g.greet
fn()        // undefined ← strict mode! (not the global object)
// Fix: fn.call(g) or fn.bind(g)
```

---

### ES6 Modules, Classes — Always Strict Mode

```javascript
// ES6 Modules are ALWAYS in strict mode — no need to declare:
// file: module.js (any ES6 module)
export const value = 42
x = 10  // ❌ ReferenceError — modules are strict by default

// Classes are ALWAYS strict mode:
class Example {
  method() {
    this.x = 10    // 'this' is undefined if called without context
  }
}

// In Next.js — always strict mode because:
// 1. All files are ES Modules (import/export)
// 2. TypeScript files operate in strict mode
// 3. Babel/SWC compilation applies strict mode
```

---

### React.StrictMode — Different from JavaScript Strict Mode

```jsx
// React.StrictMode is NOT JavaScript strict mode!
// It's a React development tool that:
// 1. Double-invokes render functions, constructors, and state initializers
//    to detect side effects in lifecycle methods
// 2. Warns about deprecated lifecycle methods
// 3. Detects unexpected side effects
// 4. Ensures reusable state works properly (React 18)

// next.js app/layout.tsx:
export default function RootLayout({ children }) {
  return (
    <html>
      <body>
        <React.StrictMode>  {/* Development-only double-rendering */}
          {children}
        </React.StrictMode>
      </body>
    </html>
  )
}
```

> [!TIP]
> **React Strict Mode Double Rendering:** In React 18, components inside `<React.StrictMode>` render TWICE in development (to detect side effects). If your `useEffect` seems to run twice, it's intentional — to verify cleanup functions work correctly. This only happens in development, not production.

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 3.11

**Q1: What does strict mode do in JavaScript?**

> Strict mode (enabled with `"use strict"` directive) makes JavaScript more restrictive to catch common bugs: (1) Throws `ReferenceError` for undeclared variable assignments (prevents accidental globals), (2) Throws `SyntaxError` for duplicate parameter names, (3) Makes `this` inside regular function calls `undefined` instead of the global object, (4) Prevents `delete` on non-deletable properties, (5) Throws on writes to read-only properties, (6) Disallows `with` statement, (7) `eval` has its own scope. ES6 modules and classes are always in strict mode.

**Q2: Does strict mode affect performance?**

> Yes, strict mode can improve performance in some JavaScript engines. When strict mode is enabled, the engine can make certain optimizations it can't in non-strict mode. For example, in strict mode, `this` is `undefined` in non-method functions (not the global object), which allows the engine to avoid looking up the global object. The restriction on `with` statements allows better variable lookup optimization. This is one reason ES6 modules (always strict) can be slightly faster than non-strict scripts.

**Q3: What is the difference between JavaScript strict mode and React's StrictMode?**

> **JavaScript strict mode** (`"use strict"`) is a language feature that changes how JavaScript is parsed and executed — it throws errors for unsafe patterns and prevents certain features. **React's `<StrictMode>`** component is a development tool that has nothing to do with JavaScript strict mode. It causes React to double-invoke render functions and effects to detect side effects, warns about deprecated API usage, and verifies that components work with React's upcoming features. React StrictMode has zero effect in production builds.

```typescript
// app/api/type-check/route.ts
// Safely analyze type of user-submitted values

import { NextRequest, NextResponse } from 'next/server'

function getDetailedType(value: unknown): object {
  return {
    typeof: typeof value,
    isNull: value === null,
    isUndefined: value === undefined,
    isNaN: typeof value === 'number' && isNaN(value as number),
    isArray: Array.isArray(value),
    isInteger: Number.isInteger(value),
    isFinite: Number.isFinite(value as number),
    isTruthy: Boolean(value),
    objectToStringType: Object.prototype.toString.call(value).slice(8, -1),
    constructorName: value !== null && value !== undefined
      ? (value as object).constructor?.name
      : 'N/A'
  }
}

export async function POST(request: NextRequest) {
  const { expression } = await request.json()

  // Safe evaluation using predefined values only
  const safeValues: Record<string, unknown> = {
    'null': null,
    'undefined': undefined,
    'NaN': NaN,
    'Infinity': Infinity,
    '0': 0,
    '-0': -0,
    '""': "",
    '"hello"': "hello",
    '[]': [],
    '{}': {},
    'true': true,
    'false': false,
    '0n': BigInt(0),
    '42n': BigInt(42),
  }

  if (expression in safeValues) {
    const value = safeValues[expression]
    return NextResponse.json({
      input: expression,
      result: getDetailedType(value)
    })
  }

  return NextResponse.json({ error: 'Unsupported expression' }, { status: 400 })
}
```

// lib/coercionExamples.ts — Quiz question bank

export interface CoercionQuestion {

  expression: string
  output: string

  {

    category: 'arithmetic'

  },

    expression: '"" == false',
    output: 'true',

]

```typescript
// components/CoercionQuiz.tsx
'use client'

import { useState } from 'react'
import { CoercionQuestion } from '@/lib/coercionExamples'

interface CoercionQuizProps {
  questions: CoercionQuestion[]
}

export default function CoercionQuiz({ questions }: CoercionQuizProps) {
  const [currentIndex, setCurrentIndex] = useState(0)
  const [userAnswer, setUserAnswer] = useState('')
  const [showAnswer, setShowAnswer] = useState(false)
  const [score, setScore] = useState(0)
  const [answered, setAnswered] = useState(false)

  const currentQuestion = questions[currentIndex]

  const checkAnswer = () => {
    setShowAnswer(true)
    setAnswered(true)
    const correct = userAnswer.trim() === currentQuestion.output
    if (correct) setScore(s => s + 1)
  }

  const nextQuestion = () => {
    setCurrentIndex(i => Math.min(i + 1, questions.length - 1))
    setUserAnswer('')
    setShowAnswer(false)
    setAnswered(false)
  }

  return (
    <div className="max-w-2xl mx-auto p-6">
      <div className="flex justify-between mb-4">
        <span className="text-gray-600">
          Question {currentIndex + 1} of {questions.length}
        </span>
        <span className="font-bold text-green-600">Score: {score}</span>
      </div>

      <div className="bg-gray-900 rounded-lg p-6 mb-6">
        <p className="text-gray-400 text-sm mb-2">What is the output of:</p>
        <code className="text-yellow-300 text-xl font-mono">
          console.log({currentQuestion.expression})
        </code>
        <span className={`ml-2 px-2 py-0.5 rounded text-xs ${
          currentQuestion.difficulty === 'easy' ? 'bg-green-600' :
          currentQuestion.difficulty === 'medium' ? 'bg-yellow-600' :
          'bg-red-600'
        } text-white`}>
          {currentQuestion.difficulty}
        </span>
      </div>

      <div className="mb-4">
        <input
          type="text"
          value={userAnswer}
          onChange={e => setUserAnswer(e.target.value)}
          onKeyDown={e => e.key === 'Enter' && !answered && checkAnswer()}
          placeholder='Type your answer (e.g., "123" or true or NaN)'
          className="w-full border-2 rounded-lg p-3 font-mono"
          disabled={answered}
        />
      </div>

      {!answered ? (
        <button
          onClick={checkAnswer}
          className="w-full bg-blue-600 text-white py-3 rounded-lg hover:bg-blue-700"
        >
          Submit Answer
        </button>
      ) : (
        <div>
          <div className={`p-4 rounded-lg mb-4 ${
            userAnswer.trim() === currentQuestion.output
              ? 'bg-green-100 border-green-400 border'
              : 'bg-red-100 border-red-400 border'
          }`}>
            <p className="font-bold mb-1">
              {userAnswer.trim() === currentQuestion.output
                ? '✅ Correct!'
                : `❌ Incorrect. Answer: ${currentQuestion.output}`
              }
            </p>
            <p className="text-sm text-gray-700">{currentQuestion.explanation}</p>
          </div>

          {currentIndex < questions.length - 1 ? (
            <button
              onClick={nextQuestion}
              className="w-full bg-gray-800 text-white py-3 rounded-lg"
            >
              Next Question →
            </button>
          ) : (
            <div className="text-center p-4 bg-blue-50 rounded-lg">
              <p className="text-2xl font-bold">Quiz Complete!</p>
              <p>Score: {score}/{questions.length}</p>
            </div>
          )}
        </div>
      )}
    </div>
  )
}
```

getType(null)            // "null"
getType(NaN)             // "nan"

  // your implementation
}

const compose = (...fns) => {
  // your implementation
}

const composedFn = compose(square, addOne, double)
composedFn(3)  // square(addOne(double(3))) = square(7) = 49

### 5 Theory Questions

**T1: Explain the complete rules of `==` (Abstract Equality Comparison) in JavaScript. When does type coercion happen and in what order? Why is it generally recommended to use `===` instead?**

**T2: JavaScript functions are "first-class citizens." What does this mean? What capabilities does this enable, and how does this concept underpin callbacks, higher-order functions, closures, and functional programming patterns in JavaScript?**

**T3: Compare the four ways to declare functions in JavaScript (declaration, expression, arrow, IIFE). For each, explain: hoisting behavior, `this` binding rules, when to use, and a real-world scenario where that type is most appropriate.**

**T4: Explain the `const` keyword in depth. What does it actually guarantee? What doesn't it guarantee? How does the concept of "const binding vs const value" apply to objects and arrays? What tools/patterns can you use to achieve true immutability in JavaScript?**

**T5: Explain JavaScript's type system. Why is JavaScript dynamically typed? What are the tradeoffs compared to statically typed languages? How does TypeScript address these tradeoffs and how does it integrate with Next.js?**

**MCP 1: Build a JavaScript Type & Coercion Explorer**

Build a Next.js page that:
- Has an input field where users can type any JavaScript expression
- Shows the result of:
  - `typeof` the value
  - `Object.prototype.toString.call()` result
  - Whether it's truthy or falsy
  - Whether it equals (`==`) and strictly equals (`===`) common values (null, 0, "", false, undefined)
  - The Boolean, Number, and String conversions
- Has a "Coercion Quiz" section with 20 questions from the chapter
- Color-codes results (green = truthy, red = falsy)

Requirements:
- Next.js App Router + TypeScript
- Server Component for static content, Client Component for interactive parts

- Safe evaluation (no `eval()` — use a lookup table for expressions)

**MCP 2: Build a String Methods Interactive Lab**

Build a Next.js page that:
- Shows all 25+ string methods organized in categories (Search, Extract, Transform, Check, Split)
- Has a live input area where the user types a string
- Each method card shows: method name, syntax, live result applied to the user's string, parameters
- Allows users to customize parameters (like `slice(2, 5)` — sliders for start/end)
- Includes a "String Challenge" section:
  - Given: "  Hello World! This is JavaScript  "
  - Tasks: trim it, capitalize each word, convert to URL slug, count vowels, reverse words
  - Users write the code and see live results
- Shows before/after comparison for transformations

Requirements:
- Next.js App Router with Client Components for live interactivity
- TypeScript with proper string method typing
- All string methods must show real-time results as user types
- Responsive grid layout for method cards

---

> [!NOTE]
> **Chapter 3 Summary — Key Takeaways:**
> 1. Prefer `const` → `let` → never `var` in modern JavaScript
> 2. `const` prevents rebinding, NOT mutation — objects/arrays can still be changed
> 3. There are 8 falsy values: `false`, `0`, `-0`, `0n`, `""`, `null`, `undefined`, `NaN` — everything else is truthy
> 4. Always use `===` over `==` — implicit coercion causes too many surprises
> 5. `typeof null === "object"` is a historical bug — check null with `value === null`
> 6. Use `Number.isNaN()` not `isNaN()` — the global version coerces before checking
> 7. `??` is NOT the same as `||` — nullish coalescing only triggers for null/undefined
> 8. Arrow functions have NO own `this` — don't use them as object methods or constructors
> 9. `forEach` cannot be broken and doesn't work with `await` — use `for...of` instead
> 10. ES6 modules and classes are ALWAYS in strict mode — no need to add `"use strict"`

## 4.1 Object Creation Patterns

<a id="41-object-creation-patterns"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Object Literal `{}` | Simplest way — inline key-value pairs |
| Constructor Function | Blueprint pattern using `new` keyword |
| `Object.create(proto)` | Explicit prototype chain control |
| ES6 Class Syntax | Modern OOP — syntactic sugar over prototypes |
| Factory Functions | Functions that return objects — no `new` needed |

---

### What Are Object Creation Patterns?

An **object creation pattern** is a strategy for creating JavaScript objects with specific structure, behavior, and prototype relationships. Choosing the right pattern affects code reusability, memory usage, and the prototype chain.

> **Hinglish:** Object banane ke alag-alag tarike hain — jaise ghar banana: kabhi ready-made (`{}`), kabhi blueprint se (`class`), kabhi custom factory se. Har tarike ka apna fayda hai.

---

### Pattern 1: Object Literal `{}`

The simplest and most common way — directly define an object inline.

```javascript
// Basic object literal:
const user = {
  name: "Rahul",
  age: 25,
  city: "Mumbai",

  // Method shorthand (ES6):
  greet() {
    return `Hello, I'm ${this.name}`
  },

  // Computed property names:
  ['is_' + 'active']: true,

  // Getter:
  get fullInfo() {
    return `${this.name}, ${this.age}`
  }
}

console.log(user.greet())   // "Hello, I'm Rahul"
console.log(user.fullInfo)  // "Rahul, 25"
console.log(user.is_active) // true

// ✅ When to use:
// - Single-use objects (config, options, one-off data)
// - No need for multiple instances
// - Simple data containers
```

---

### Pattern 2: Constructor Function

A regular function used with `new` keyword to create multiple instances — each gets its own properties but shares prototype methods.

> **Hinglish:** Constructor function ek blueprint hai — jaise ek mold se kai saare products banana. Har product (object) ka apna data hoga, lekin methods share honge memory mein.

```javascript
function Person(name, age) {
  // When called with 'new':
  // 1. A new empty object is created: {}
  // 2. 'this' points to that new object
  // 3. Properties are added to 'this'
  // 4. The new object is returned automatically

  this.name = name
  this.age = age

  // ⚠️ Don't add methods here — wastes memory!
  // Each instance would get its own copy of the function
}

// Add shared methods to prototype (memory efficient):
Person.prototype.greet = function() {
  return `Hello, I'm ${this.name}, ${this.age} years old`
}

Person.prototype.toString = function() {
  return `Person(${this.name})`
}

// Creating instances:
const rahul = new Person("Rahul", 25)
const priya = new Person("Priya", 22)

console.log(rahul.greet())   // "Hello, I'm Rahul, 25 years old"
console.log(priya.greet())   // "Hello, I'm Priya, 22 years old"

// Both share the same greet function (memory efficient):
console.log(rahul.greet === priya.greet)  // true ✅ (same prototype method)

// instanceof works:
console.log(rahul instanceof Person)  // true

// ⚠️ What happens without 'new':
const broken = Person("John", 30)  // 'this' = window/undefined in strict
console.log(broken)                 // undefined (no return)
```

---

### Pattern 3: `Object.create(proto)`

Creates an object with a **specific prototype** — gives explicit control over the prototype chain.

```javascript
// Define prototype object:
const animalProto = {
  breathe() {
    return `${this.name} is breathing`
  },
  eat(food) {
    return `${this.name} eats ${food}`
  }
}

// Create object with animalProto as prototype:
const dog = Object.create(animalProto)
dog.name = "Rex"
dog.breed = "Labrador"

console.log(dog.breathe())    // "Rex is breathing" (from prototype)
console.log(dog.eat("bone"))  // "Rex eats bone" (from prototype)

// Prototype chain verification:
console.log(Object.getPrototypeOf(dog) === animalProto)  // true

// Create with null prototype (pure data object, no inherited methods):
const pureData = Object.create(null)
pureData.key = "value"
// pureData.toString()  // ❌ TypeError — no inherited methods!
// Useful for: hash maps without prototype pollution

// Create with initial properties descriptor:
const cat = Object.create(animalProto, {
  name: { value: "Whiskers", writable: true, enumerable: true },
  lives: { value: 9, writable: false, enumerable: true }
})
```

---

### Pattern 4: ES6 Class Syntax

Modern syntax that is **syntactic sugar** over constructor functions and prototypes — more readable and familiar to OOP developers.

> **Hinglish:** Class syntax prototype-based inheritance ko simple dikhata hai — andar se wahi kaam hota hai (prototype chain), par likha zyada saaf lagta hai. Yeh "sugar" hai — meetha dikhta hai, andar wahi purana "flour" hai.

```javascript
class Person {
  // Private fields (ES2022):
  #password = null

  constructor(name, age) {
    this.name = name        // Public property
    this.age = age
  }

  // Instance method (on prototype):
  greet() {
    return `Hello, I'm ${this.name}`
  }

  // Getter:
  get info() {
    return `${this.name} (${this.age})`
  }

  // Setter:
  set info(str) {
    [this.name, this.age] = str.split(",")
  }

  // Static method (called on class, not instance):
  static create(name, age) {
    return new Person(name, age)
  }

  // Private method:
  #validate() {
    return this.age >= 0
  }
}

// Inheritance:
class Employee extends Person {
  constructor(name, age, role) {
    super(name, age)    // Must call super first!
    this.role = role
  }

  introduce() {
    return `${this.greet()}, I work as ${this.role}`
  }
}

const e = new Employee("Rahul", 25, "Developer")
console.log(e.introduce())      // "Hello, I'm Rahul, I work as Developer"
console.log(e instanceof Person)    // true
console.log(e instanceof Employee)  // true

// Under the hood — proof classes are just prototypes:
console.log(typeof Person)                        // "function"
console.log(Employee.prototype instanceof Person) // true

// Static usage:
const p = Person.create("Priya", 22)
```

---

### Pattern 5: Factory Functions

A function that creates and returns an object — no `new` keyword, no `this`, no prototype chain.

```javascript
// Factory function — returns a new object each call:
function createUser(name, age, role = "user") {
  // Private variable via closure:
  let loginCount = 0

  return {
    name,
    age,
    role,

    login() {
      loginCount++
      return `${name} logged in (${loginCount} times)`
    },

    getLoginCount() {
      return loginCount  // Private — not directly accessible
    },

    promote(newRole) {
      return createUser(name, age, newRole)  // Returns new object
    }
  }
}

const rahul = createUser("Rahul", 25)
console.log(rahul.login())          // "Rahul logged in (1 times)"
console.log(rahul.login())          // "Rahul logged in (2 times)"
console.log(rahul.loginCount)       // undefined — private via closure!
console.log(rahul.getLoginCount())  // 2 ✅

// Comparison: Constructor vs Factory
// Constructor: shares prototype methods (memory efficient for many instances)
// Factory: each object gets its own methods (private state via closure, no 'this' confusion)
```

---

### Comparison Table

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A["Create an Object"] --> B{"Multiple instances\nneeded?"}
    B -->|"No — one-off"| C["✅ Object Literal {}"]
    B -->|"Yes"| D{"Need private\nstate via closure?"}
    D -->|"Yes"| E["✅ Factory Function"]
    D -->|"No"| F{"Need OOP inheritance\nwith extends?"}
    F -->|"Yes"| G["✅ ES6 Class"]
    F -->|"No"| H{"Need explicit\nprototype control?"}
    H -->|"Yes"| I["✅ Object.create()"]
    H -->|"No"| J["✅ Constructor Function"]

    style C fill:#c8e6c9
    style E fill:#bbdefb
    style G fill:#f3e5f5
    style I fill:#fff9c4
    style J fill:#ffccbc
```

| Pattern | `new` needed? | Inheritance | Private State | Memory Efficiency | When to Use |
|---------|--------------|-------------|---------------|-------------------|-------------|
| Object Literal | ❌ | ❌ | ❌ | N/A | One-off objects, configs |
| Constructor | ✅ | Via prototype | ❌ | ✅ Shared proto | Many instances, legacy code |
| `Object.create` | ❌ | ✅ Explicit | ❌ | ✅ | Prototype chain control |
| Class | ✅ | ✅ `extends` | ✅ `#field` | ✅ | Modern OOP, React components |
| Factory | ❌ | ❌ (composition) | ✅ Closure | ❌ Each gets copy | Privacy, avoid `this` bugs |

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 4.1

**Q1: What is the difference between a factory function and a constructor function?**

> **Constructor functions** use `new` keyword, `this` to assign properties, prototype for shared methods. Multiple instances share prototype methods (memory efficient). No built-in private state — properties on `this` are public. **Factory functions** are regular functions that return an object — no `new`, no `this`. Each object gets its own copy of methods (less memory-efficient for many instances). They excel at private state through closures. Factories are preferred when you need privacy, want to avoid `this` confusion, or compose behavior instead of inheriting.

**Q2: What does `new` keyword actually do?**

> When `new` is used with a constructor: (1) Creates a new empty object `{}`. (2) Sets the new object's prototype to `Constructor.prototype`. (3) Calls the constructor with `this` bound to the new object. (4) If the constructor returns an object, that object is returned; otherwise the new object is returned automatically. Understanding step 4 is crucial — if you explicitly `return {}` from a constructor, `new` returns that object instead of `this`.

**Q3: Why are ES6 classes called "syntactic sugar"?**

> ES6 classes don't introduce a new object-oriented model — JavaScript remains prototype-based. Classes are a cleaner syntax for constructor functions + prototype assignments. `typeof MyClass === "function"` proves this. Under the hood, `class Animal { speak() {} }` is equivalent to `function Animal() {}` + `Animal.prototype.speak = function() {}`. The key additions classes provide over raw prototypes are: cleaner `extends` syntax, `super` keyword, static methods, and private fields (`#`) — but the prototype mechanism is identical.

**Q4: What is `Object.create(null)` used for?**

> `Object.create(null)` creates an object with NO prototype — it doesn't inherit from `Object.prototype`. This means no inherited methods like `toString`, `hasOwnProperty`, `valueOf`. This is useful for creating pure hash maps/dictionaries where: (1) You want to use any string as a key without risk of prototype property name collision (e.g., a key named `"toString"` won't conflict with `Object.prototype.toString`). (2) You want cleaner property iteration. (3) Slight performance improvement for large lookup tables. Used in: parser symbol tables, memoization caches, routing tables.

**Q5: What is the output?**

```javascript
function Car(brand) {
  this.brand = brand
  return { brand: "Toyota" }  // Returns a different object!
}

const myCar = new Car("Honda")
console.log(myCar.brand)
```

> **Output:** `"Toyota"`
> When a constructor explicitly returns a **non-primitive** (object/array), that returned object is used instead of the automatically created `this` object. So `myCar` is `{ brand: "Toyota" }`, not `{ brand: "Honda" }`. If the constructor returned a primitive (like a string or number), `this` would be returned.

## 4.2 Property Access & Manipulation

<a id="42-property-access--manipulation"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Dot Notation | `obj.key` — static, clean, most common |
| Bracket Notation | `obj['key']` — dynamic keys, special characters |
| Computed Property Names | `{ [key]: value }` — dynamic keys at creation |
| Property Shorthand | `{ name, age }` instead of `{ name: name }` |
| Optional Chaining | `?.` — safe nested access |
| Property Existence Checks | `in` vs `hasOwnProperty` vs `?.` |

---

### Dot vs Bracket Notation

```javascript
const user = {
  name: "Rahul",
  age: 25,
  "first-name": "Rahul",  // Special character in key
  "123abc": "special",     // Starts with number
}

// Dot notation — clean, static key (must be valid identifier):
console.log(user.name)    // "Rahul"
console.log(user.age)     // 25
// user.first-name        // ❌ SyntaxError — '-' is subtraction operator

// Bracket notation — dynamic or special keys:
console.log(user["name"])        // "Rahul"
console.log(user["first-name"])  // "Rahul" ✅ (bracket handles special chars)
console.log(user["123abc"])      // "special" ✅

// Dynamic key access — must use bracket:
const key = "name"
console.log(user[key])    // "Rahul" ✅ (dot notation can't use variables)
// user.key               // undefined ❌ (looks for key named "key")

// Practical: iterating with dynamic keys
const settings = { theme: "dark", lang: "en", fontSize: 16 }
const keyToGet = getUserPreference()  // returns "theme"
console.log(settings[keyToGet])       // "dark"

// Nested access:
const data = { user: { address: { city: "Mumbai" } } }
console.log(data.user.address.city)          // "Mumbai" (dot)
console.log(data["user"]["address"]["city"]) // "Mumbai" (bracket)
console.log(data.user?.address?.city)        // "Mumbai" (optional chaining)
```

---

### Computed Property Names

Create object keys dynamically at definition time using `[]` syntax.

> **Hinglish:** Computed property names matlab object banate waqt hi dynamic key use karo — jaise form se user ka input leke object key banana.

```javascript
// Basic computed property:
const prefix = "user"
const obj = {
  [`${prefix}Name`]: "Rahul",       // "userName": "Rahul"
  [`${prefix}Age`]: 25,              // "userAge": 25
  [`${prefix}City`]: "Mumbai",       // "userCity": "Mumbai"
}

console.log(obj.userName)  // "Rahul"

// Dynamic enum-like keys:
const FIELDS = { NAME: "name", AGE: "age", EMAIL: "email" }

const formData = {
  [FIELDS.NAME]: "Rahul",
  [FIELDS.AGE]: 25,
  [FIELDS.EMAIL]: "rahul@example.com"
}
// Equivalent to: { name: "Rahul", age: 25, email: "rahul@example.com" }

// Real-world React pattern — dynamic state updates:
function updateField(fieldName, value) {
  setState(prev => ({
    ...prev,
    [fieldName]: value  // Computed key from parameter
  }))
}

updateField("username", "rahul123")
updateField("email", "rahul@example.com")
```

---

### Property Shorthand

When variable name equals property name, use shorthand.

```javascript
const name = "Rahul"
const age = 25
const city = "Mumbai"

// Old way:
const user1 = { name: name, age: age, city: city }

// Shorthand (ES6):
const user2 = { name, age, city }  // Same result!

// In functions returning objects:
function createUser(name, age) {
  // Old way:
  return { name: name, age: age }

  // Shorthand:
  return { name, age }
}

// In destructuring + shorthand combo:
function processUser({ name, age }) {
  const processed = true
  const timestamp = Date.now()
  return { name, age, processed, timestamp }  // All shorthand
}
```

---

### Property Existence Checks

Three main ways to check if a property exists on an object:

```javascript
const obj = {
  name: "Rahul",
  age: 0,           // Falsy value
  address: null,    // Null value
}

// ❌ Incorrect — doesn't work for falsy values:
if (obj.age) console.log("has age")      // Skipped! 0 is falsy
if (obj.address) console.log("has addr") // Skipped! null is falsy

// ✅ 1. 'in' operator — checks OWN + inherited properties:
"name" in obj        // true (own property)
"age" in obj         // true (own, even though value is 0)
"toString" in obj    // true ⚠️ (inherited from Object.prototype!)

// ✅ 2. hasOwnProperty — checks ONLY own properties:
obj.hasOwnProperty("name")      // true
obj.hasOwnProperty("age")       // true
obj.hasOwnProperty("toString")  // false ✅ (inherited, not own)

// ✅ 3. Object.hasOwn() — modern, safer version (ES2022):
Object.hasOwn(obj, "name")     // true
Object.hasOwn(obj, "toString") // false

// ✅ 4. Optional chaining — returns undefined instead of throwing:
obj?.name           // "Rahul"
obj?.missing        // undefined (no error)
obj?.missing?.deep  // undefined (no error)

// Comparison:
const proto = { shared: "inherited" }
const child = Object.create(proto)
child.own = "mine"

"shared" in child                   // true (includes inherited)
child.hasOwnProperty("shared")      // false (own only)
child.hasOwnProperty("own")         // true
Object.hasOwn(child, "shared")      // false
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 4.2

**Q1: When must you use bracket notation instead of dot notation?**

> You must use bracket notation when: (1) The property key contains special characters, spaces, or hyphens (`obj["first-name"]`). (2) The key starts with a number (`obj["123abc"]`). (3) The key is stored in a **variable** (`obj[dynamicKey]`). (4) The key is a template literal or expression (`obj[\`${prefix}Name\`]`). Use dot notation for readability when keys are valid identifiers and statically known.

**Q2: What is the difference between `in` operator and `hasOwnProperty()`?**

> The `in` operator checks whether a property exists on the object **OR anywhere in its prototype chain** — so `"toString" in {}` returns `true` because `toString` is on `Object.prototype`. `hasOwnProperty()` checks only **own properties** — properties directly defined on the object, not inherited. In modern code, prefer `Object.hasOwn(obj, key)` over `obj.hasOwnProperty(key)` because: (1) `hasOwnProperty` can be overridden/shadowed by own properties, (2) Objects created with `Object.create(null)` don't have `hasOwnProperty`.

**Q3: What is the output?**

```javascript
const key = "length"
const arr = [1, 2, 3]
console.log(arr.key)
console.log(arr[key])
```

> `undefined` → `3`
> `arr.key` looks for a property literally named `"key"` — doesn't exist. `arr[key]` uses the variable `key` which holds `"length"` — so `arr["length"]` = `3`.

## 4.3 Object Destructuring

<a id="43-object-destructuring"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Basic Destructuring | Extract properties to variables |
| Renaming | Assign to different variable names |
| Default Values | Fallback when property is undefined |
| Nested Destructuring | Extract deeply nested properties |
| Function Parameters | Destructure directly in function signature |

---

### What is Object Destructuring?

**Destructuring** is a concise syntax to extract multiple properties from an object into individual variables in one statement.

> **Hinglish:** Destructuring matlab object se ek ek cheez nikalna — jaise dabba khol ke sab cheezein alag-alag rakhna. Ek line mein kai variables ban jaate hain.

```javascript
const user = {
  name: "Rahul",
  age: 25,
  city: "Mumbai",
  email: "rahul@example.com"
}

// Without destructuring (verbose):
const name1 = user.name
const age1 = user.age
const city1 = user.city

// With destructuring (concise):
const { name, age, city } = user
console.log(name, age, city)  // "Rahul" 25 "Mumbai"

// Extract only what you need:
const { email } = user         // Just email
const { name: n, age: a } = user  // Renamed
```

---

### Renaming, Defaults, and Nesting

```javascript
const product = {
  id: 101,
  title: "Laptop",
  price: 50000,
  specs: {
    ram: "16GB",
    storage: "512GB",
    display: {
      size: "15.6 inch",
      resolution: "1920x1080"
    }
  }
}

// 1. RENAMING — extract with different variable name:
const { title: productTitle, price: productPrice } = product
console.log(productTitle)  // "Laptop"
// 'title' is NOT created as a variable; 'productTitle' is

// 2. DEFAULT VALUES — fallback when property is undefined:
const { color = "Black", warranty = "1 year", price = 0 } = product
console.log(color)     // "Black" (not in object — uses default)
console.log(warranty)  // "1 year" (not in object — uses default)
console.log(price)     // 50000 (IS in object — default ignored)
// ⚠️ Default only triggers for UNDEFINED, NOT null:
const { id = 999 } = { id: null }
console.log(id)        // null (not 999! null doesn't trigger default)

// 3. RENAMING + DEFAULT combined:
const { color: productColor = "Silver" } = product
console.log(productColor)  // "Silver" (not in product, uses default)

// 4. NESTED DESTRUCTURING:
const { specs: { ram, storage } } = product
console.log(ram)     // "16GB"
console.log(storage) // "512GB"
// ⚠️ 'specs' is NOT a variable now — only ram and storage are

// Deeply nested:
const { specs: { display: { size, resolution } } } = product
console.log(size)       // "15.6 inch"
console.log(resolution) // "1920x1080"

// Nested with renaming and defaults:
const { specs: { ram: memory = "8GB", gpu = "Integrated" } } = product
console.log(memory)  // "16GB" (from product)
console.log(gpu)     // "Integrated" (not in product)
```

---

### Destructuring in Function Parameters

```javascript
// Without destructuring (old way):
function displayUser(user) {
  console.log(user.name, user.age, user.city)
}

// With destructuring in parameters (modern):
function displayUser({ name, age, city = "Unknown" }) {
  console.log(name, age, city)
}

displayUser({ name: "Rahul", age: 25, city: "Mumbai" })
// "Rahul" 25 "Mumbai"

displayUser({ name: "Priya", age: 22 })
// "Priya" 22 "Unknown" (city defaults)

// With renaming in parameters:
function processOrder({ id: orderId, total: amount, status = "pending" }) {
  return `Order #${orderId}: ₹${amount} (${status})`
}

processOrder({ id: "ORD-001", total: 1500 })
// "Order #ORD-001: ₹1500 (pending)"

// React component pattern:
function UserCard({ name, age, avatar = "/default.png", isAdmin = false }) {
  return (
    // JSX using destructured props directly
  )
}

// With nested destructuring:
function renderAddress({ address: { city, state, pincode = "000000" } }) {
  return `${city}, ${state} - ${pincode}`
}

renderAddress({ address: { city: "Mumbai", state: "Maharashtra" } })
// "Mumbai, Maharashtra - 000000"
```

---

### Advanced Destructuring Patterns

```javascript
// Combined rest + destructure:
const { name, age, ...otherDetails } = user
console.log(otherDetails)  // { city: "Mumbai", email: "..." }

// Destructuring arrays within objects:
const config = {
  servers: ["server1", "server2", "server3"],
  primary: "server1"
}

const { servers: [firstServer, ...backupServers], primary } = config
console.log(firstServer)    // "server1"
console.log(backupServers)  // ["server2", "server3"]

// Destructuring in loops:
const users = [
  { name: "Rahul", role: "admin" },
  { name: "Priya", role: "user" },
]

for (const { name, role } of users) {
  console.log(`${name}: ${role}`)
}

// Destructuring function return values:
function getMinMax(arr) {
  return { min: Math.min(...arr), max: Math.max(...arr) }
}

const { min, max } = getMinMax([3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9])
console.log(min, max)  // 1 9
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 4.3

**Q1: What is the difference between destructuring renaming and creating a new variable?**

> In destructuring, `const { name: userName } = obj` creates a variable called `userName` with the value of `obj.name`. The property `name` is read but no variable named `name` is created. This is purely a syntax for extraction with an alias — useful to avoid naming conflicts or to use more descriptive names in the current scope.

**Q2: What is the output?**

```javascript
const obj = { a: 1, b: undefined, c: null }
const { a = 10, b = 20, c = 30 } = obj
console.log(a, b, c)
```

> **Output:** `1 20 null`
> `a` = 1 (exists, not undefined). `b` = 20 (is `undefined` — default triggers). `c` = `null` (is `null` — default does NOT trigger; null is not undefined). Only `undefined` triggers defaults in destructuring.

**Q3: How does destructuring help in React development?**

> Destructuring is fundamental in React: (1) **Props:** `function Button({ children, onClick, disabled = false })` avoids `props.children`, `props.onClick` everywhere. (2) **useState:** `const [count, setCount] = useState(0)` destructures the returned array. (3) **useContext:** `const { user, logout } = useContext(AuthContext)` extracts only needed context values. (4) **API responses:** `const { data, error, loading } = useQuery(...)` — cleaner than `result.data`, `result.error`.

## 4.4 Object Methods — Complete Reference

<a id="44-object-methods--complete-reference"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Method | Purpose |
|--------|---------|
| `Object.keys(obj)` | Array of own enumerable keys |
| `Object.values(obj)` | Array of own enumerable values |
| `Object.entries(obj)` | Array of `[key, value]` pairs |
| `Object.assign(target, ...sources)` | Shallow copy/merge |
| `Object.freeze(obj)` | Prevent all modifications |
| `Object.seal(obj)` | Prevent add/delete, allow modify |
| `Object.create(proto)` | Create with specific prototype |
| `Object.fromEntries(entries)` | Convert entries array to object |
| `Object.getPrototypeOf(obj)` | Get prototype of object |
| `Object.defineProperty(obj, key, desc)` | Fine-grained property control |

---

### `Object.keys()`, `Object.values()`, `Object.entries()`

```javascript
const product = {
  id: 1,
  name: "Laptop",
  price: 50000,
  inStock: true
}

// Object.keys — array of keys:
Object.keys(product)   // ["id", "name", "price", "inStock"]

// Object.values — array of values:
Object.values(product) // [1, "Laptop", 50000, true]

// Object.entries — array of [key, value] pairs:
Object.entries(product)
// [["id", 1], ["name", "Laptop"], ["price", 50000], ["inStock", true]]

// Practical patterns:

// 1. Iterate over object:
Object.entries(product).forEach(([key, value]) => {
  console.log(`${key}: ${value}`)
})

// 2. Convert object to filtered object:
const filtered = Object.fromEntries(
  Object.entries(product).filter(([key, value]) => typeof value === "number")
)
// { id: 1, price: 50000 }

// 3. Transform object values:
const discounted = Object.fromEntries(
  Object.entries(product)
    .map(([key, value]) => [key, typeof value === "number" ? value * 0.9 : value])
)
// { id: 0.9, name: "Laptop", price: 45000, inStock: true }

// 4. Check object emptiness:
Object.keys(product).length === 0  // false (not empty)
Object.keys({}).length === 0       // true (empty)

// ⚠️ Only own enumerable properties:
const proto = { inherited: "yes" }
const child = Object.create(proto)
child.own = "mine"

Object.keys(child)    // ["own"] — inherited not included
"inherited" in child  // true — 'in' includes inherited
```

---

### `Object.assign()` — Shallow Merge/Copy

```javascript
// Signature: Object.assign(target, ...sources)
// Copies own enumerable properties from sources to target
// Returns the target object

// 1. Copy object:
const original = { a: 1, b: 2 }
const copy = Object.assign({}, original)  // New empty object as target
copy.a = 99
console.log(original.a)  // 1 — unchanged (original not modified)

// 2. Merge multiple objects:
const defaults = { theme: "light", lang: "en", fontSize: 16 }
const userPrefs = { theme: "dark", fontSize: 18 }
const finalConfig = Object.assign({}, defaults, userPrefs)
// { theme: "dark", lang: "en", fontSize: 18 }
// userPrefs properties override defaults

// 3. Mutates target (be careful!):
const target = { a: 1 }
const result = Object.assign(target, { b: 2 }, { c: 3 })
console.log(target)  // { a: 1, b: 2, c: 3 } ⚠️ target is mutated!
console.log(target === result)  // true (same reference!)

// 4. SHALLOW copy limitation:
const obj = { level1: { level2: { value: 42 } } }
const copied = Object.assign({}, obj)
copied.level1.level2.value = 999
console.log(obj.level1.level2.value)  // 999 ⚠️ (nested reference shared!)

// Modern alternative: spread (same shallow behavior):
const copy2 = { ...original }

// vs Object.assign — differences:
// Spread creates literal, assign mutates target
// Spread in JSX/React is more common
// Object.assign can add to existing object
```

---

### `Object.freeze()` vs `Object.seal()`

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph Normal["Regular Object"]
        N1["✅ Add properties"]
        N2["✅ Delete properties"]
        N3["✅ Modify properties"]
    end

    subgraph Sealed["Object.seal()"]
        S1["❌ Add properties"]
        S2["❌ Delete properties"]
        S3["✅ Modify existing properties"]
    end

    subgraph Frozen["Object.freeze()"]
        F1["❌ Add properties"]
        F2["❌ Delete properties"]
        F3["❌ Modify properties"]
    end

    Normal -->|"Object.seal()"| Sealed
    Normal -->|"Object.freeze()"| Frozen
    Sealed -->|"Object.freeze()"| Frozen

    style Normal fill:#c8e6c9
    style Sealed fill:#fff9c4
    style Frozen fill:#ffcdd2
```

```javascript
// Object.freeze() — immutable object:
const config = Object.freeze({
  API_URL: "https://api.example.com",
  TIMEOUT: 5000,
  nested: { retries: 3 }  // ⚠️ nested is NOT frozen (shallow freeze)
})

config.API_URL = "https://hacker.com"  // Silently fails (strict: TypeError)
config.NEW_PROP = "value"              // Silently fails
delete config.TIMEOUT                  // Silently fails
config.nested.retries = 999           // ✅ WORKS! Nested not frozen

console.log(config.API_URL)           // "https://api.example.com" (unchanged)
console.log(config.nested.retries)    // 999 (nested was mutated!)

Object.isFrozen(config)               // true
Object.isFrozen(config.nested)        // false ⚠️

// Object.seal() — no add/delete, but can modify:
const user = Object.seal({ name: "Rahul", age: 25 })

user.name = "Priya"     // ✅ Modify existing — allowed
user.email = "p@ex.com" // ❌ Add new — fails silently (strict: TypeError)
delete user.age         // ❌ Delete — fails silently

Object.isSealed(user)   // true

// Deep freeze utility:
function deepFreeze(obj) {
  Object.getOwnPropertyNames(obj).forEach(name => {
    const value = obj[name]
    if (value && typeof value === "object") {
      deepFreeze(value)  // Recursively freeze nested objects
    }
  })
  return Object.freeze(obj)
}

const deepFrozen = deepFreeze({ a: 1, b: { c: 2 } })
deepFrozen.b.c = 999    // ❌ Now fails even on nested!
```

---

### `Object.defineProperty()` — Fine-Grained Control

```javascript
const obj = {}

Object.defineProperty(obj, "id", {
  value: 42,
  writable: false,     // Cannot change value
  enumerable: false,   // Won't appear in Object.keys/for...in
  configurable: false  // Cannot delete or redefine this property
})

console.log(obj.id)      // 42
obj.id = 100             // Silently fails (strict: TypeError)
console.log(obj.id)      // 42 — unchanged
Object.keys(obj)         // [] — id is not enumerable!
"id" in obj              // true — 'in' still finds it

// Use cases:
// 1. Read-only constants on objects
// 2. Hidden metadata (non-enumerable)
// 3. Computed properties via getters/setters

// Getter and Setter via defineProperty:
const person = { firstName: "Rahul", lastName: "Sharma" }

Object.defineProperty(person, "fullName", {
  get() {
    return `${this.firstName} ${this.lastName}`
  },
  set(name) {
    [this.firstName, this.lastName] = name.split(" ")
  },
  enumerable: true,
  configurable: true
})

console.log(person.fullName)   // "Rahul Sharma"
person.fullName = "Priya Mehta"
console.log(person.firstName)  // "Priya"
```

---

### `Object.fromEntries()` — The Reverse of `entries()`

```javascript
// Convert array of [key, value] pairs back to object:
const entries = [["name", "Rahul"], ["age", 25], ["city", "Mumbai"]]
const obj = Object.fromEntries(entries)
// { name: "Rahul", age: 25, city: "Mumbai" }

// Convert Map to object:
const map = new Map([["a", 1], ["b", 2], ["c", 3]])
const fromMap = Object.fromEntries(map)
// { a: 1, b: 2, c: 3 }

// ✅ Power pattern — transform object:
const prices = { apple: 100, banana: 50, mango: 150 }

// Apply 10% discount to all:
const discounted = Object.fromEntries(
  Object.entries(prices).map(([fruit, price]) => [fruit, price * 0.9])
)
// { apple: 90, banana: 45, mango: 135 }

// Filter object by value:
const affordable = Object.fromEntries(
  Object.entries(prices).filter(([, price]) => price < 120)
)
// { apple: 100, banana: 50 }

// Rename all keys (lowercase):
const normalized = Object.fromEntries(
  Object.entries(prices).map(([key, value]) => [key.toUpperCase(), value])
)
// { APPLE: 100, BANANA: 50, MANGO: 150 }
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 4.4

**Q1: What is the difference between `Object.freeze()` and `Object.seal()`?**

> `Object.freeze()` makes an object completely immutable — you cannot add, delete, OR modify properties. `Object.seal()` prevents adding and deleting properties but still allows **modifying values of existing properties**. Both are shallow (don't affect nested objects). Use case: `Object.freeze()` for configuration constants. `Object.seal()` when you want a stable structure but mutable values.

**Q2: What is the difference between `Object.assign()` and the spread operator for objects?**

> Both perform a **shallow copy** of own enumerable properties. Differences: (1) `Object.assign()` **mutates** the target object and returns it; spread creates a **new object literal**. (2) Spread is part of object literal syntax and can only be used in that context; `Object.assign()` can merge into any existing object. (3) `Object.assign()` triggers setters on the target object; spread does not. In practice, spread (`{ ...obj }`) is preferred in React for its immutability-friendly style.

**Q3: What is the output?**

```javascript
const a = { x: 1, y: 2 }
const b = Object.assign(a, { y: 10, z: 3 })
console.log(a === b)
console.log(a.y)
```

> `true` → `10`
> `Object.assign()` mutates and returns the **target** (`a`). So `a` and `b` are the same reference. `a.y` is overwritten to `10` by the source object `{ y: 10, z: 3 }`.

**Q4: When would you use `Object.create(null)`?**

> When you need a **pure hash map** without any prototype chain — useful for: (1) High-performance lookup tables where keys might conflict with `Object.prototype` properties (`constructor`, `toString`, `hasOwnProperty`). (2) Safer key-value stores where you iterate with `for...in` without needing `hasOwnProperty` checks. (3) Polyfill implementations and compiler output. Note: these objects lack `toString`, `valueOf`, etc., so avoid when the object might be coerced to a string.

## 4.5 Spread & Rest with Objects

<a id="45-spread--rest-with-objects"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Spread to Copy | `{ ...obj }` — shallow copy |
| Spread to Merge | `{ ...obj1, ...obj2 }` — combine objects |
| Spread Override | Later keys win |
| Rest to Collect | `const { a, ...rest } = obj` |
| Shallow Copy Limitation | Nested objects share references |

---

### Spread Operator with Objects

```javascript
// 1. COPY object (shallow):
const user = { name: "Rahul", age: 25 }
const userCopy = { ...user }
userCopy.name = "Priya"
console.log(user.name)    // "Rahul" — original unchanged ✅

// 2. MERGE objects:
const defaults = { theme: "light", lang: "en", fontSize: 16 }
const overrides = { theme: "dark", fontSize: 20 }
const merged = { ...defaults, ...overrides }
// { theme: "dark", lang: "en", fontSize: 20 }
// ↑ overrides wins for duplicate keys

// 3. Override specific property:
const product = { id: 1, name: "Laptop", price: 50000, inStock: true }
const updated = { ...product, price: 45000, discount: 10 }
// { id: 1, name: "Laptop", price: 45000, inStock: true, discount: 10 }

// ⚠️ ORDER MATTERS — later spread wins:
const a = { x: 1, y: 2 }
const b = { y: 99, z: 3 }
console.log({ ...a, ...b })  // { x: 1, y: 99, z: 3 } (b's y wins)
console.log({ ...b, ...a })  // { y: 2, z: 3, x: 1 }  (a's y wins)

// 4. Add properties while copying:
const enriched = {
  ...user,
  email: "rahul@example.com",
  createdAt: new Date().toISOString()
}

// 5. Shallow copy limitation ⚠️:
const original = {
  name: "Rahul",
  address: { city: "Mumbai", pin: "400001" }  // Nested object
}

const shallow = { ...original }
shallow.name = "Priya"           // ✅ Doesn't affect original
shallow.address.city = "Delhi"   // ⚠️ DOES affect original! (shared reference)

console.log(original.name)          // "Rahul" — unchanged
console.log(original.address.city)  // "Delhi" ⚠️ — changed!
```

---

### Rest with Objects

```javascript
// Extract specific properties, collect the rest:
const { name, age, ...otherInfo } = user
console.log(name)       // "Rahul"
console.log(age)        // 25
console.log(otherInfo)  // { city: "Mumbai", email: "..." }

// ⚠️ Rest must be LAST in destructuring:
// const { ...rest, name } = user  // ❌ SyntaxError

// Practical — omit properties from an object:
function omit(obj, ...keysToOmit) {
  const { ...result } = obj
  keysToOmit.forEach(key => delete result[key])
  return result
}

// Better — without mutation:
function omit(obj, ...keysToOmit) {
  return Object.fromEntries(
    Object.entries(obj).filter(([key]) => !keysToOmit.includes(key))
  )
}

omit(user, "password", "token")  // Returns user without sensitive fields

// Pick specific properties:
function pick(obj, ...keysToPick) {
  return Object.fromEntries(
    Object.entries(obj).filter(([key]) => keysToPick.includes(key))
  )
}

pick(user, "name", "email")  // { name: "Rahul", email: "..." }

// React pattern — pass remaining props:
function Button({ onClick, children, ...htmlProps }) {
  // onClick and children extracted, rest passed to <button>
  return <button onClick={onClick} {...htmlProps}>{children}</button>
}
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 4.5

**Q1: What is the difference between spread in objects and `Object.assign()`?**

> Both perform shallow copy of own enumerable properties. Key differences: (1) Spread creates a new object literal; `Object.assign()` mutates an existing target. (2) Spread does not invoke setters on the target; `Object.assign()` does invoke setters. (3) Spread cannot be used with `null` prototypes without special handling. (4) `Object.assign()` is more explicit about mutation; spread is more declarative and preferred in React for immutable updates.

**Q2: How do you correctly update a nested property using spread?**

```javascript
const state = { user: { name: "Rahul", address: { city: "Mumbai" } } }

// ❌ Wrong — shallow spread doesn't deep-copy:
const wrong = { ...state, user: { ...state.user, name: "Priya" } }
// This correctly updates name but what about address?

// ✅ Correct — spread at each level:
const correct = {
  ...state,
  user: {
    ...state.user,       // Copy all user properties
    name: "Priya",       // Override name
    address: {
      ...state.user.address,  // Copy address
      city: "Delhi"           // Override city
    }
  }
}
```

> For deeply nested objects, each level must be individually spread. For complex state, consider Immer (used in Redux Toolkit) which allows "mutating" syntax that produces immutable copies internally.

## 4.6 Shallow Copy vs Deep Copy

<a id="46-shallow-copy-vs-deep-copy"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| What is Shallow Copy? | Copy reference, nested objects shared |
| What is Deep Copy? | Fully independent copy at all levels |
| Shallow Methods | `Object.assign`, spread, `Array.slice`, `Array.concat` |
| Deep Methods | `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify)`, `structuredClone`, Lodash |
| JSON limitations | What types JSON cannot handle |
| `structuredClone` | Modern browser/Node API |

---

### The Core Problem

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph Original["Original Object"]
        ORoot["{ name: 'Rahul'\naddress: → REF_A }"]
        OAddr["REF_A:\n{ city: 'Mumbai' }"]
        ORoot --> OAddr
    end

    subgraph Shallow["Shallow Copy"]
        SRoot["{ name: 'Rahul'\naddress: → REF_A }"]
        SRoot -->|"SAME reference!"| OAddr
    end

    subgraph Deep["Deep Copy"]
        DRoot["{ name: 'Rahul'\naddress: → REF_B }"]
        DAddr["REF_B:\n{ city: 'Mumbai' }"]
        DRoot --> DAddr
    end

    style Original fill:#e3f2fd
    style Shallow fill:#fff9c4
    style Deep fill:#e8f5e9
```

> **Hinglish:** Shallow copy matlab "upar ki cheezein alag, andar wali cheezein shared." Deep copy matlab "poori duniya alag" — ek badlao doosre ko affect nahi karta.

---

### Shallow Copy — Methods & Limitations

```javascript
const original = {
  name: "Rahul",
  scores: [95, 87, 92],
  address: { city: "Mumbai" }
}

// Method 1: Spread operator
const copy1 = { ...original }

// Method 2: Object.assign
const copy2 = Object.assign({}, original)

// Both behave the SAME — shallow copy:
copy1.name = "Priya"             // ✅ Independent — original.name unchanged
copy1.scores.push(100)           // ⚠️ Shared reference — original.scores also changes!
copy1.address.city = "Delhi"     // ⚠️ Shared reference — original.address.city changes!

console.log(original.name)          // "Rahul" ✅
console.log(original.scores)        // [95, 87, 92, 100] ⚠️
console.log(original.address.city)  // "Delhi" ⚠️

// Array shallow copy:
const arr = [1, [2, 3], { a: 4 }]
const arrCopy = [...arr]        // or arr.slice()
arrCopy[0] = 99                 // ✅ Independent
arrCopy[1].push(99)             // ⚠️ Nested array shared
arrCopy[2].a = 999              // ⚠️ Nested object shared
```

---

### Deep Copy — Methods & Tradeoffs

```javascript
const complex = {
  name: "Rahul",
  birthday: new Date("1999-01-15"),
  greet() { return "Hello!" },
  score: undefined,
  tags: new Set(["react", "next"]),
  meta: new Map([["key", "value"]]),
  nested: { deep: { value: 42 } }
}

// ─── METHOD 1: JSON.parse(JSON.stringify()) ───────────────────
const jsonCopy = JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(complex))

// Limitations:
// ❌ Functions are DROPPED silently
// ❌ Date → becomes string ("1999-01-15T00:00:00.000Z")
// ❌ undefined values → DROPPED from objects
// ❌ Set, Map → converted to empty objects {}
// ❌ Symbols → DROPPED
// ❌ Circular references → throws TypeError

jsonCopy.nested.deep.value = 999
console.log(complex.nested.deep.value)  // 42 ✅ (independent)
console.log(jsonCopy.birthday)          // "1999-01-15T..." ❌ (string, not Date)
console.log(jsonCopy.greet)             // undefined ❌ (function dropped)
console.log(jsonCopy.score)             // undefined but the key is GONE ❌

// ─── METHOD 2: structuredClone() — MODERN STANDARD ────────────
const structCopy = structuredClone(complex)
// ✅ Available: Node.js 17+, Chrome 98+, Firefox 94+

// What structuredClone SUPPORTS:
// ✅ Objects, Arrays
// ✅ Date (preserves as Date)
// ✅ Map, Set (preserves type)
// ✅ RegExp
// ✅ ArrayBuffer, TypedArrays
// ✅ Circular references (handles them!)
// ✅ undefined values

// What structuredClone DOESN'T support:
// ❌ Functions (throws TypeError)
// ❌ Symbols (excluded)
// ❌ DOM nodes (throws DataCloneError)
// ❌ Class instances lose their prototype (become plain objects)

structCopy.nested.deep.value = 999
console.log(complex.nested.deep.value)  // 42 ✅
console.log(structCopy.birthday instanceof Date)  // true ✅

// ─── METHOD 3: Lodash _.cloneDeep() ──────────────────────────
import _ from 'lodash'
const lodashCopy = _.cloneDeep(complex)

// ✅ Handles most types including class instances
// ✅ Best for complex objects in production
// ❌ Adds bundle size (~70KB for lodash)
// For just cloneDeep: import cloneDeep from 'lodash/cloneDeep' (~5KB)
```

---

### Complete Comparison Table

| Feature | Shallow (`...`) | JSON round-trip | `structuredClone` | Lodash `cloneDeep` |
|---------|----------------|-----------------|-------------------|-------------------|
| **Nested objects** | ❌ Shared | ✅ Deep | ✅ Deep | ✅ Deep |
| **Date** | ❌ Shared | ❌ → string | ✅ Preserved | ✅ Preserved |
| **Functions** | ❌ Shared | ❌ Dropped | ❌ Throws | ⚠️ Excluded |
| **undefined** | ❌ Shared | ❌ Dropped | ✅ Preserved | ✅ Preserved |
| **Map/Set** | ❌ Shared | ❌ → `{}` | ✅ Preserved | ✅ Preserved |
| **RegExp** | ❌ Shared | ❌ → `{}` | ✅ Preserved | ✅ Preserved |
| **Circular refs** | ❌ Shared | ❌ Throws | ✅ Handles | ✅ Handles |
| **Class instances** | ❌ Shared | ❌ Loses class | ❌ Loses class | ✅ Best |
| **Performance** | ⚡ Fastest | 🐌 Slow (2 parse) | ✅ Fast | ⚠️ Slowest |
| **Browser support** | ✅ Universal | ✅ Universal | ✅ Modern | ✅ Universal |
| **Bundle cost** | Zero | Zero | Zero | ~5KB |

---

### When to Use What

```javascript
// ✅ Shallow copy — for React state updates (when only top level changes):
setState(prev => ({ ...prev, name: "Priya" }))

// ✅ JSON round-trip — for simple JSON-serializable objects (no Date/Functions):
const saved = JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(config))  // quick deep copy for simple data

// ✅ structuredClone — for general deep copy in modern environments:
const snapshot = structuredClone(complexState)  // best modern option

// ✅ Lodash cloneDeep — for complex objects with class instances:
import cloneDeep from 'lodash/cloneDeep'
const backup = cloneDeep(userWithMethods)

// ✅ Immer — for React state (allows mutation syntax, produces immutable result):
import produce from 'immer'
const newState = produce(state, draft => {
  draft.user.address.city = "Delhi"  // Looks like mutation but isn't!
})
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 4.6

**Q1: What is the difference between shallow copy and deep copy?**

> A **shallow copy** creates a new object where top-level properties are copied by value (primitives) or by reference (objects/arrays). Nested objects and arrays still point to the same memory locations as the original — mutations to nested data affect both copies. A **deep copy** creates a completely independent object at every level — nested objects are recursively copied, so changes to one don't affect the other. Use shallow copy for simple objects with no nesting; use deep copy when nested data independence is required.

**Q2: What are the limitations of `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(obj))`?**

> It drops or corrupts: (1) **Functions** — silently excluded. (2) **`undefined`** — excluded from objects, converted to `null` in arrays. (3) **`Date`** — converted to ISO string (loses `Date` type). (4) **`Set` / `Map`** — converted to empty `{}`. (5) **`RegExp`** — converted to `{}`. (6) **`Symbol`** keys — excluded. (7) **Circular references** — throws `TypeError`. (8) **Class instances** — lose their prototype/class methods. It's only safe for plain objects with JSON-compatible types (string, number, boolean, null, plain arrays, plain objects).

**Q3: When would you prefer `structuredClone` over Lodash's `cloneDeep`?**

> Prefer `structuredClone` when: (1) You're in a modern environment (Node 17+, Chrome 98+) and don't want to add a library dependency. (2) You need to handle circular references natively. (3) You're copying Map, Set, Date, RegExp, TypedArrays. (4) Bundle size matters (structuredClone is built-in — zero cost). Choose Lodash `cloneDeep` when: (1) You need to clone class instances and preserve their prototype chain. (2) Supporting older environments without polyfills. (3) Already using Lodash in the project.

## 4.7 Arrays — Creation & Core Methods

<a id="47-arrays--creation--core-methods"></a>

### Subtopic Breakdown

| Subtopic | What You'll Learn |
|----------|-------------------|
| Array Literal | `[]` — most common way |
| `Array.from()` | Convert iterables to arrays |
| `Array.of()` | Create arrays from arguments |
| `Array.isArray()` | Reliably check if value is array |

---

### Array Creation

```javascript
// 1. Array literal — most common:
const fruits = ["apple", "banana", "cherry"]
const mixed = [1, "hello", true, null, { a: 1 }, [2, 3]]
const empty = []
const withHoles = [1, , 3]  // Sparse array — index 1 is empty

// 2. Array constructor — tricky behavior:
new Array(3)        // [empty × 3] — creates 3 empty slots (NOT [3]!)
new Array(1, 2, 3)  // [1, 2, 3] — only works with multiple args
// ⚠️ new Array(n) is confusing — avoid!

// 3. Array.from() — convert iterables/array-like to arrays:
Array.from("hello")           // ["h", "e", "l", "l", "o"]
Array.from(new Set([1,2,2,3])) // [1, 2, 3] (Set → Array, deduped)
Array.from(new Map([["a",1]])) // [["a", 1]]

// Array.from with NodeList (DOM):
const divs = document.querySelectorAll("div")  // NodeList
Array.from(divs)  // ✅ Real array with all methods
// OR: [...divs]   // Same result

// Array.from with length and mapping:
Array.from({ length: 5 }, (_, i) => i)       // [0, 1, 2, 3, 4]
Array.from({ length: 5 }, (_, i) => i * 2)   // [0, 2, 4, 6, 8]
Array.from({ length: 5 }, () => 0)            // [0, 0, 0, 0, 0]
Array.from({ length: 3 }, (_, i) => ({ id: i, active: false }))
// [{ id: 0, active: false }, { id: 1, active: false }, { id: 2, active: false }]

// 4. Array.of() — creates array from arguments (unlike Array()):
Array.of(3)        // [3]  ✅ (not [empty × 3] like new Array(3))
Array.of(1, 2, 3)  // [1, 2, 3]

// 5. Array.isArray() — most reliable type check:
Array.isArray([])              // true ✅
Array.isArray(new Array(3))    // true ✅
Array.isArray("hello")         // false
Array.isArray({ length: 3 })   // false (array-like but not array)
typeof []                      // "object" ❌ (not useful for checking arrays)
[] instanceof Array            // true (but fails across iframes)
```

---

### 🎯 Interview Questions — Section 4.7

**Q1: What is the difference between `Array.from()` and the spread operator for converting iterables?**

> Both convert iterables to arrays. `Array.from()` has two advantages: (1) It accepts a **mapping function** as the second argument — `Array.from({ length: 5 }, (_, i) => i * 2)` — which spread cannot do without an additional `map()` call. (2) It works with **array-like objects** (objects with `length` property but not iterable), like `arguments` object or `NodeList`. Spread only works with true iterables. In most cases they're equivalent; use `Array.from()` when you need the mapping function or working with array-like non-iterables.

**Q2: Why is `new Array(3)` considered confusing?**

> `new Array(3)` creates a sparse array with 3 empty slots (`[empty × 3]`), NOT an array containing the number 3. This behavior differs from `new Array(1, 2, 3)` which creates `[1, 2, 3]`. The inconsistency is a JavaScript gotcha — `new Array(1)` creates `[empty]` but `new Array(1, 2)` creates `[1, 2]`. `Array.of(3)` was introduced specifically to fix this — it always creates `[3]`. Prefer `Array.from({ length: 3 })` or `[...Array(3)]` for creating arrays of specific length.
